#6938. Céline Dion Trades the Stage for the Stands at Metallica’s Las Vegas Show

Céline Dion might have wrapped the first leg of her Paris residency, but that doesn’t mean she’s not still spending her time in arenas. This weekend, the Harper’s Bazaar cover star headed to Las Vegas to catch Metallica’s Life Burns Faster show at the Sphere, spending an evening soaking up some heavy metal with her sons, Eddy and Nelson Angélil.

Spotted in a box, Dion was captured rocking out to the band’s “Nothing Else Matters,” singing along and playing air guitar (even when she was the only one standing up in her section). Rather than wearing a classic band T-shirt, she dressed up for the occasion in a blue suit, featuring a fitted blazer with a wrap detail at the waist. Her hair was slicked back into a low bun, and she appeared to keep her jewelry minimal. Nothing could get in the way of her dancing, after all.

Back in September, the Grammy-winning artist kicked off her Paris residency at the Plénitude Arena, making an emotional return to the stage after a full six years off. Dion, who hadn’t performed a full-scale concert since her 2022 stiff-person syndrome diagnosis, hosted a total of 16 shows throughout the month, with a starry crowd that rivaled Metallica’s (think Rihanna and Michelle Obama).

She honored her host city with a wardrobe full of pieces from the country’s top couture houses, including a dramatic black Dior gown made custom by Jonathan Anderson and a dusty-pink Givenchy cape. In May, she will head back to Paris for an additional 10 nights.

#6935. House Republicans Couldn’t Have Picked a Worse Minority Speaker

So much for cleaning house. Rep. Jim Jordan of Ohio has been a loyal foot soldier of the MAGA regime for years now.

Politico has a very strange story about the battle for Republican leadership in the House of Representatives. Speaker Mike Johnson already has scratched himself from the race to become the next minority leader. And here is Politico’s take, which seems very, very odd.

So far, this is a run-of-the-mill state of the losers’ postelection story. The losing side, particularly a catastrophically losing side, always spends some time in public introspection. It’s almost a kind of penance. But here’s where the story spins out.

As I’m sure Stutzman knows, on any issue that both involves the administration and drove voters in the midterm elections, there’s not a dime’s worth of difference between Scalise and Jordan (R-Van Heusen). They have both been loyal foot soldiers of the MAGA regime. Both of them supported at least one objection to certify the results in 2020. On Tuesday, Jordan thumped Scalise and became the GOP leader, 106–73, and it will be interpreted as another defeat for Johnson’s old leadership team. But the promotion of Jordan doesn’t answer any of the problems that contributed to the loss of Congress by the Republicans. Jordan’s knees haven’t stopped jerking since 2017. He hunted every snipe at which the administration pointed, even the ones that didn’t exist, which was a substantial number of them. He was rude and approximately as committed to bipartisanship as a manhole cover. If the primary issue in the election was corruption, Jordan has made a career out of supplying alibis.

I hope Speaker-in-Waiting Jeffries realizes all of this. This is the legislative equivalent of Stephen Miller running the White House. I’d say the Republicans could have found someone more dedicated to getting things done, but there isn’t anybody like that left.

#6932. It’s Always Daiquiri Season

The simple, rum-based cocktail has been compromised by identity theft. So what is it, really?

The daiquiri has been compromised by identity theft. Down in Louisiana, the New Orleans Original Daiquiris chain has sullied the classic cocktail’s good name by slinging slushy sugar bombs with outrageous names like the Banana Banshee, Mudslide, and Crawgator.

Savvy Esquire readers know that the original drink involves nothing more than rum, lime juice, and sweetener. Yet modern specialists like Vay Su, cofounder of Vandell in Los Angeles, can take you on a Caribbean odyssey by ruminating on the minutiae: What kind of limes, and how fresh? Cane sugar or simple syrup? Shaved ice or a fat chunk? And how much of the rum character should force its way through?

Restaurants like Kabawa in New York’s East Village have transformed the humble daiquiri into an unforgettable drinking experience. When you step in and spy other people enjoying a concoction poured over 70 heaping grams of shaved ice, you already know what you’re going to order. Kathryn “Pepper” Stashek, Kabawa’s creative bartender—yes, that’s really her title—never gets tired of seeing that look in everyone’s eyes that says, Obviously, I need a daiquiri.

At Kabawa, you get to choose your rum too. Opt for the Jamaica–Barbados blend of Probitas or go agricole with Montebello Rhum Blanc from the islands of Guadeloupe. Maybe veer down a vintage alley with Bacardi Carta Blanca from 1978. You can’t go wrong; the only mistake is assuming you need to wait for warm weather. As Su says, it’s always daiquiri season.

Want to whip up a classic daiquiri at home? Here’s how to do it the Esquire way.

#6928. All the Ways Princess Kate Paid Tribute to Queen Elizabeth II and Princess Diana on Remembrance Day

As the future queen of England, Princess Catherine will always bring a little piece of history with her.

Over the weekend, the British royal family celebrated Remembrance Day—a national day of memorial to honor service members and civilians who died in World War I, World War II, and subsequent military conflicts. For the two-day occasion, Kate dressed in elegant black looks, which she accessorized with layers of meaningful pieces.

On Saturday, to attend the Royal British Legion Festival of Remembrance event at London’s Royal Albert Hall, Kate slipped on a black-and-white polka-dot dress by Alessandra Rich, and added some sparkle with Queen Elizabeth II’s Orbital pendant earrings. The late monarch first publicly wore the two-tier diamond earrings to the state opening of parliament in May 2012, and later loaned them to Kate in 2016. The princess inherited the jewelry after the queen’s passing in 2022.

Top top off her dotted look, Kate pinned on a red poppy brooch from Izzy Ager of Lock & Co. (The poppy is a symbol of Remembrance Day.), and her Irish Guards Regimental Brooch.

On Sunday, the Princess of Wales stepped out in a sophisticated black Catherine Walker coat (one of her favorites), that she adorned with her HMS Glasgow brooch and three red poppies. She also brought a little of the late Princess Diana with her for the special day by accessorizing with Lady Di’s famous Collingwood pearl drop earrings. Each earring features a diamond stud and a diamond bell cap atop a shiny white pearl. The pieces were gifted to Diana in 1981, for her wedding to then-Prince Charles, by London jeweler Collingwood (a favorite of the Spencer family).

Look back at all of Kate’s Remembrance Day looks through the years here.

#6922. The Transformative Experience of Buying Japanese Denim in Japan

I was never a big jeans guy. After a recent trip to Tokyo, I think I’m changed forever.

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

A ponytailed man in head-to-toe denim orbits me a third, then a fourth time in quick succession, like his eyes are twin asteroids and my waist a powerful gravitational force. When he stops, suddenly, he calls out, “Thirty-two!” and asks, “Button or zipper?”

I should’ve expected this sort of wizardry when I entered Momotaro Jeans Aoyama, a Tokyo outlet of the highly esteemed Japanese denim purveyor. Though it’s housed on the second floor of a mall just a three-quarter-mile walk from overstimulating, people-packed Shibuya, this tiny shop feels tranquil, purposeful, reverent. Dark indigo pants are folded and piled neatly in wooden cubbies along the walls; thick jackets hang gently from floating racks. Such a scene differs greatly from my earliest jeans-shopping memory.

In my early teens, on an Easter Sunday trip into New York, my mother shooed me into the Times Square Levi’s to buy my first big-boy pair. I never wore jeans. I attended Catholic school for much of my youth, so most mornings I was stuffed into stiff, ugly khakis. In rebellion against forced formality, I’d sport sweatpants or basketball shorts outside school hours.

Naturally, this appalled my fashionable mother, who couldn’t have lived with herself if she dropped her teenage son off at coed social outings wearing swishy Adidas track pants. But why was her remedy to hit a retail store in the busiest neighborhood in America on a holiday weekend? Trying on fur coats in hell would be comparably pleasant.

We dodged shoppers yanking slim cuts off floor displays and dug through unkempt mounds of unfolded denim until Mom found a few sizes in a few washes and forced me into the back changing room to try ’em all on. Of course, I huffed and puffed. Of course, I whinged and complained. But I left with a pair of medium-wash straight legs that at least wouldn’t look embarrassing on a first date.

In the nearly two decades since, as my interest in menswear has graduated from begrudging to professional, mass-market clothing has plunged in quality but spiked in price. Consumers are faced with a harsh reality when buying new: Either replace your decaying polyester-blend garments every few years or buy pricier, sturdier stuff from reputable manufacturers. I diverted toward the latter path a few years ago when I purchased a $150 heavyweight cotton hoodie from American Giant. (Though that may not seem like much to an Esquire reader, it was quite the jump for a J. Crew Factory sales-rack goblin like me.)

I learned of raw, selvedge denim a year later, when a buddy who’s into menswear sent me a Reddit post depicting a pair of jeans standing up all on their own. This was the beauty of raw Japanese denim, my friend told me, which is manufactured in the spirit of heritage American workwear from a time when durability was paramount. Heavyweight. Premium dye. Selvedge, meaning it’s woven on old-school looms from high-quality cotton. (You can also buy cheaper, significantly less impressive Japanese denim, but we’re talking about the reputable specialists here.) I was intrigued until he mentioned how many hundreds of dollars they cost thanks to import fees and general U.S. financial fuckery. But if I were ever on a trip to Japan at a time when the U.S. dollar was stronger than the yen? Maybe I could swing a pair.

In September, I was invited on a work trip to Tokyo, and finally my chance presented itself. When I landed at Narita International Airport, all I could think about was trying on stiff, uncomfortable jeans—talk about a different mindset from that of teenage me in Times Square.

After a week of tight itineraries, I finally sneaked away on my very last day and made my way past the stanchions lining the perimeter of the Momotaro shop. I read online that some folks wait up to 30 minutes to enter; luckily, there is no queue when I arrive.

A handful of employees stand silently behind a broad counter, one of them the man with the ponytail, who approaches me when he sees me checking out the brand’s #100 Standard Straight jeans in 14.7-ounce denim, which is a measurement of weight per square yard of fabric. Instead of asking for my size, he does the circling bit described earlier. I reply to his follow-up question with “zipper,” and he shows me to a narrow changing room toward the back of the space and asks me to remove my shoes. Japanese etiquette. He arrives with two pairs of the very jeans I was checking out. I try them both on, but they don’t feel quite right.

“Anything thicker?” I ask, trying to overcome the language barrier between us. My Japanese is rudimentary; his English is solid in bursts but not completely fluent.

He returns with a straight-cut pair from Momotaro’s classic line that’s 15.7 ounces, but it has a button fly, which I struggle mightily to close. Alas, there are no zippered pairs of this kind left. Yet, of the three, these fit the best around my waist. I’m puzzled until I look closely at the tags. These classic button flies are a 32, but when I return to the standard zippers, I notice he has accidentally brought me a 31—way, way too tight in the crotch—and a 33, a hair too loose.

His eyes widen apologetically when I point out the error, and he rushes back to the stocks to ensure I get a 32 in my hands. They fit absolutely perfectly. Done and done. He’s relieved.

And just like that, I’m ready to walk out a satisfied customer. This is how I’m used to buying jeans, after all. You try ’em, you like ’em, you buy ’em, and if you get home and they’re not quite what you thought they were, you return ’em. But as I will soon learn, there’s more to it at Momotaro. My guy has me put them back on and step out once more.

“Baggy or not baggy?” he asks of how I’d like my jeans to appear.

In front of a mirror, he squats down and folds the bottoms of the legs up to just about my ankle line and slides a transparent clip over the surface to mark its place. An in-house tailor will hem them for me. I change back into my other clothes, and he explains as best he can how to properly take care of my jeans. I shouldn’t wash them too often, but when I do, I should fasten everything and turn them inside out. Oh, and keep them separate from other clothes, because the indigo will definitely run. That’s part of the fun; the jeans are designed to fade handsomely over time based on how and when I wear them. A personalized pair, if you will. Don’t ever dry them in a machine, though. Take ’em out, smooth the creases, and hang to dry in a cool, dark place.

He hands me a little box with a lifetime-guarantee card and explains that if I’m ever back in Japan and need a repair, they’ve got me covered.

A second employee guides me across the hall to a second storefront, where payment and hemming take place. After I show my passport to receive the 10 percent tax reduction for tourists, I swipe my card. $211. That’s it?! By shopping here, I essentially chop a third off the price listed on the Momotaro U.S. website. I’m told to return in 15 or so minutes once the hemming is done, so I dash to a café downstairs for a matcha over which I reflect on my experience.

Whenever I buy anything over a Benjamin, I immediately get buyer’s remorse. That American Giant hoodie? I spent a good few days lamenting that the sleeves were slightly too long before I talked myself out of a return. And yet, when I pick up my Momotaro jeans once they’re ready—they’re bagged neatly and wrapped in plastic to protect them from the day’s ceaseless rain—I feel beyond cared for. When I finally return home and put them on, I don’t fear that I maybe should have purchased a smaller size in case they stretch—they’re not supposed to—because my jeans fit perfectly. They’re right in proportion with my body, sit higher than I’m used to but quite comfortably, and style well with a graphic tee and light jacket.

Maybe I’m shopping at the wrong stores at home. Maybe I need to raise my price floor to get that kind of service. Or maybe we’ve lost something here in the U.S. that’s now attainable only overseas. Maybe I need to get back on a plane to Tokyo ASAP and hit all the stores I missed—Hinoya, Japan Blue, the Flat Head—then take the shinkansen to Osaka and get myself some really thick boys from Iron Heart, where 25-ounce denim is the gold standard.

Or maybe I should just enjoy the jeans I have now, wear them in nicely over the next decade until they’re washed to sky blue and softer than a lamb’s ear. I can look forward to passing them down to whatever kids I have one day. It’ll save us a trip to the Times Square Levi’s.

#6919. Actually, Robert Pattinson Would Make a Perfect James Bond

My colleagues and editors are sick of hearing me say it, but I believe it with my whole chest: The actor playing James Bond should never be more famous than James Bond. There’s something about Bond—his cultural footprint and the dangerous yet seductive life he leads in his stories—that makes him too big to be a footnote in a great actor’s résumé. James Bond creates leading men; James Bond is not for leading men to create.

So you have to know that it’s not easy for me to say this: Robert Pattinson would be fantastic and exactly the new James Bond we’re looking for.

You’ve probably heard by now that Amazon, which acquired full creative control over all things Bond from longtime owners Eon Productions and the Broccoli family, has been searching for the next 007 in a movie to be directed by Denis Villeneuve.

What was once an arena of speculation has exploded into a circus, with different names being bandied about at once—namely Callum Turner, Josh O’Connor, and Jack Lowden. (There is obviously a specific type of white man they’re going for here.) Just when it seemed as if the new Bond would finally be unveiled, there came a rush of two really big names suddenly in the running. Among them is Pattinson, reported by The Hollywood Reporter on November 6, along with Nicholas Hoult. Pattinson secretly auditioned for the role during a break in filming for The Batman: Part II, which halted filming in October when Matt Reeves attended to a personal matter.

No shade to Hoult or any of the other actors, but Pattinson is now the most interesting candidate despite a whiff of overexposure this year. Between The Drama in the spring, The Odyssey in the summer, Primetime in the fall, and soon Dune: Part Three in the dead of winter, 2026 has been shaping up into a banner year for Pattinson. Not since Twilight has the man been so prolific on our screens.

But even before the year began, Pattinson had long ago outgrown his image of a tween heartthrob. He’s a prodigious talent capable of daring choices and executing on them in a way you can’t ever expect. (Who else would say cock like this?) His popular line from The Odyssey—“Somebody get these beggars out of here!”—didn’t become a meme on its own. Pattinson made beggars sound like a slur, and in the same breath, his nervous blinking and quivering smile cemented Antinous as an unconfident weasel whose puffed-out chest was, in fact, filled with hot air. Unlike previous Batman actors, who often played the Caped Crusader like a knight in matte black armor, Pattinson’s interpretation was/is that of an antisocial weirdo everyone stares at like a freak. Which he is.

Like Batman, Bond has also been a role that people misunderstand, sometimes even by the men who play him. (It is not my biases talking here; I really do think Daniel Craig is the only actor to ever nail the nuances and pathology of the character.) Bond is stylish and can say the right thing at the right time, be it to enemies or lovers. But he’s not a dashing or noble hero. He’s a brute in Brunello Cucinelli. A thug with a $9,000 Omega. He literally kills for a living—and had to kill to get that right—and his attachment to his job is downright destabilizing.

Recall in Skyfall, after Judi Dench’s M orders Moneypenny to shoot, Bond copes with endless drinking, womanizing, and gambling. That might sound like a fun weekend in Vegas, but in the context of Bond, his fallout from discovering his expendability led him to a really, really dark path. It is not normal to drink shots with a scorpion on your hand, nor is it healthy to be up in the morning reaching for booze from behind the bar. Bond might live a life many of us men dream of, but it comes at a severe cost. I don’t know what kind of Bond movie Villeneuve has in mind, but if the creative team is lining up like this, we’re in for something special.

You can’t exactly guesstimate what kind of 007 Pattinson might be based on his previous roles. There is truly little common ground between Batman and Bond. But one thing is for sure: If Pattinson gets the part, we are in for a goddamn show.

#6917. The Kitchen Trends Designers Expect to See Everywhere in 2027

The National Kitchen & Bath Association’s 2027 forecast has arrived.

Above: the kitchen of an Athens home designed by Nashmita Rajiah.

Partial walls, hidden appliances, and pantries with room for prep are among the kitchen features expected to gain ground next year, according to the National Kitchen & Bath Association’s 2027 Kitchen Trends Report. Based on a survey of 830 kitchen-industry professionals—including designers, manufacturers, remodelers, and architects—the report looks at the layouts, materials, lighting, and technology they expect to be popular over the next three years. Here are the main takeaways.

NKBA reports growing interest in partial walls, movable partitions, and separate kitchen zones—a shift from last year’s forecast, when open layouts were still gaining ground. Dedicated prep spaces and back kitchens are also emerging features, cited by nearly half of designers.

Kitchens are still expected to get bigger, with 52 percent of designers anticipating growth. The kitchen’s surrounding rooms are growing, too. NKBA reports that mudrooms and laundry spaces are increasingly being integrated into kitchen plans. The vast majority of designers expect attached mudrooms to be popular over the next three years, with 45 percent anticipating mudrooms getting larger and 43 percent expecting laundry spaces to grow.

Appliances are increasingly being incorporated into cabinetry rather than left on display. Eighty-five percent of designers agree cabinets are being designed to hide appliances and other functional elements, and roughly two-thirds say open shelving is on its way out in favor of enclosed storage.

Pantries are taking on more work, too. Nearly 90 percent agree they are becoming multifunctional extensions of the kitchen, and three-quarters expect dedicated small-appliance stations to be popular. Separate beverage areas continue to gain support, with 90 percent anticipating their popularity, up from 85 percent in the 2026 report.

Neutrals remain the leading color of choice, followed by greens and blues. For cabinetry, light and medium tones draw equal interest, and white oak is the preferred wood for roughly half of designers. Islands don’t necessarily have to match: a little more than half expect them to feature a different color from the surrounding cabinets. Quartzite and quartz lead countertop preferences, with nearly half of designers favoring honed or matte finishes.

The lighting forecast includes under-cabinet lights, illuminated cabinet interiors, nighttime path lighting, sconces, and pendants. Eighty-four percent of designers agree kitchens will incorporate multiple layers of light, and 78 percent say lighting is increasingly being used as a design feature rather than just a functional necessity. Under-cabinet lighting leads the forecast at 87 percent, followed by interior-cabinet lighting at 76 percent.

NKBA identifies ventilation, water filtration, touchless faucets, and sound-absorbing materials among the report’s wellness features. Designers are also discussing ways to make kitchens more resilient: 65 percent cite leak-detection systems and automatic shut-off valves, 62 percent cite backup power, and a little over half point to ventilation and air filtration.

Julia Cancilla is the social media & news editor at ELLE Decor, where she oversees the brand's socials and covers design, pop culture, and emerging trends. She also authors the monthly ELLE Decoroscope column. Her work has appeared in Inked magazine, House Beautiful, Marie Claire, and more.

From Politico:

Tuesday began with another bravura tantrum from Hegseth, who held a press availability in order to stomp and shout. From The New York Times:

At that point, the Republicans were still flexing their independence.

Oh, Ted, you sweet, innocent child. It’s been a decade since the president insulted your wife and you still think he hasn’t stolen the conscience of your party.

#6913. This Colorful California Mountain Home Gives Classic Ski House Style a Fresh New Look

Using the surrounding landscape as palette inspiration, designer Alden Fletcher brought 70s retro flair to this family retreat.

When most people think of a classic ski chalet, they picture log-lined walls, dark corners, a raging hearth, plenty of plaid, and maybe even a taxidermied deer or two. But a certain kind of magic strikes when high-altitude architecture hits the right notes, and this Truckee, California, retreat is a masterclass in making the mountains sing.

Built almost 20 years ago, this family holiday home boasted all the 90s mountain classics: an abundance of knotty pine, traditional slate floors, and a minimalist beige palette. Undoubtedly stylish for the time, the retreat now felt out of step with the Bay Area family looking to make it into a personalized winter escape. The owners knew updates were imminent (necessary, even), but they feared losing the home’s classic California soul. Fighting modernism’s white-box aesthetic, they called in Alden Fletcher of the eponymous San Francisco-based design firm to meld the home’s retro architecture with a fresh, forest-inspired brief.

Noting the owner’s concerns and dedication to sustainability, the project’s goal was not to remodel for grandeur, but to restore the home’s charm. Fletcher’s task was to design around storied memories, the family’s obvious love for winter sports, and their passion for entertaining—all while combining the crisp aesthetic of the alpine wilderness with cozy textiles and natural details to create the ultimate mountain sanctuary. By pulling saturated hues from the surrounding landscape and sourcing perky patterns inspired by the local culture, the redesign breathes new life into the slope-side abode without sacrificing sentiment.

FAST FACTSDesigner: Alden Fletcher Interiors Stylist: Beth Protass Location: Truckee, CAThe Space: A five-bedroom, four-bathroom home across 3,000 square feet

The home's hallways set the tone for the entire design scheme. On the first floor, just beyond the main door, wall-mounted ski runners double as decor. Upstairs, Fletcher redefined the boundaries of the primary suite to carve out a nook for a vignette displaying the home’s most bespoke decor piece: a vintage cherry-red chairlift. “The home’s location is unique,” adds Fletcher. “It sits on a ski slope, and you can view chairlifts out the back windows!”

A true gathering space, the living room was designed around the original stone-faced fireplace, though Fletcher did replace the former pine mantel with white oak, connecting the floor details with the exposed oak beams running along the ceiling.

Swiveling burnt orange armchairs accompany an inky indigo sectional. Both gather around a button-tufted ottoman, which doubles as a coffee perch or game table. Shaded sconces flank the fireplace to give the room a glow, even when the hearth is unlit, while a pair of vintage ski shoes hang on the wall, crowning the design.

In the playroom, the deep green hue of the wallpaper paired with the natural texture of knotty pine borrows inspiration from the forest-like landscape just beyond the window. Vertical lines carried through both the wallpaper and the wainscoting create the illusion of height, while playful ski motifs bring the local sport’s culture into the play space.

“The clients are sustainably minded and didn't want to get rid of what was working,” says Fletcher. With conservation in mind, she opted to keep the original perimeter cabinets, refreshing them with paint, new hardware, and Virginia Black Satin Granite countertops, a sturdier alternative to show-stopping soapstone.

To optimize the space, she nixed the two existing islands, reimagining the room to fit a larger iteration that boasts a sink and easily seats five. The design team then added upper cabinets, transforming the space into a storage haven. Later, she placed a library ladder and golden hardware for easy access and a touch of whimsy. Then, she turned her attention to making sure the lighting was up to par. Fletcher firmly believes a beautiful space should be equally as functional. To ensure no detail fell short, she installed discreet recessed lights alongside under-cabinet lighting, and two large glass pendants to adorn the space. All dimmable for ambiance—of course. “There’s nothing worse than a home kitchen that is just flooded with bright light everywhere. A kitchen should still feel cozy and warm, and good lighting is the key to accomplishing that,” says Fletcher.

Fletcher compensated for this former powder room’s lack of windows with vibrant wallpaper to energize the space and make the most of interior lighting. Borrowing space from an adjacent closet, she converted this former two-piece bath into a full retreat with a shower, vanity, and storage, finishing the space with gold hardware for an additional touch of luxury.

Perhaps the most customized room in the home, the mudroom boasts bold color and smart storage solutions for a space as functional as it is chic. The custom cabinet is an ode to smart design, featuring bench seating, pegs, an overhead shelf, and under-seat shoe storage—but the real showstopper is its bold red hue.

“My favorite element of the built-in is the color. The brick color has just the right amount of earthiness,” says Fletcher.

The family loves to entertain, often hosting large groups of friends and family. To accommodate their social lifestyle, Fletcher designed a hospitality hub. Removing a bathroom door, she altered the room’s layout, allowing space for this guest suite to come to life.

With the new dimensions secured, she covered the walls in printed wallpaper inspired by the great local outdoors. The bold pattern set the tone for the rest of the design as she pulled colors, patterns, and themes to complement it.

For the primary bedroom, the theme was warmth: a warm bed, warm home, warm soul, all channeled through muted terracotta walls. But before it could truly transform, the team had to iron out a few oddball details.

“This room used to have a few odd details, including transom windows to the adjoining bathroom and an exterior door to a deck. We removed those elements, which allowed for a better furniture layout and a cozier feeling,” says Fletcher.

The room features a traditional four-poster bed in a nearly black finish, which carries over to the lighting details and furniture hardware, bringing out the warmth and depth of the surrounding rich red hues.

For the primary bath, the team capitalized on the luxury of the sloped-side views, framing a classic 400-pound slipper tub with a nearly floor-to-ceiling window featuring vintage pine trim. Truly the focal point of the design, Fletcher finished the window treatment with woven shades to tie in the natural wood details of the home and stand up to moisture. Natural grain runs through the room’s doors and vanity, topped with soft white Calacatta marble, channeling the classic ski chalet aesthetic. Gold fixtures extend from the vanity to the lighting, towel rack, and tub, for a touch of glamour.

Boasting the project’s biggest splurge, a custom bunk-trundle bed, the kid’s room combines playful hues with forest-inspired details and maximum sleepover potential. “We wanted to maximize sleeping spots in this home,” Fletcher explains. “The Casa Kids bunk bed in the kid's room was a splurge, but it can sleep 5!”

Alden Fletcher first fell in love with design while working as an art teacher and refurbishing furniture in her downtime. In 2020, she grew that passion into a full-service design firm focused on bringing functionality to storied designs and creating ultra-personalized interiors.

#6906. So Is Lanterns Canceled Or What?

It was one of the best new HBO shows in recent memory, but it seems like that won’t matter at all.

You know what happens when you blow up a universe? You’re left sifting through the rubble. Since the news that James Gunn and his producing partner Peter Safran have been ousted as co-CEOs and cochairmen of DC Studios, there are many questions about the future of their DC Universe. Chief among them is Lanterns. So is Lanterns canceled? That’s what everyone wants to know.

The confusion started when Variety published its report about the exit of Gunn and Safran on November 10. The brunt of the story details how Gunn and Safran’s leadership of the studio was a modest, but not big enough, success for Warner Bros. (and now Skydance) brass. “Efforts to right the ship were mixed at best—with tentpoles and television shows that didn’t always generate enough excitement to justify their price tags,” wrote Variety. “Sources suggest the thinking was they spent big—and lost too much—on projects that just weren’t resonating with audiences.”

In the story, Variety explored the studio’s TV efforts, which we should note have plenty of asterisks. Under Gunn and Safran’s leadership, DC Studios released The Penguin, a spin-off of The Batman that won awards; the caveat is that it was already in development before Gunn and Safran took over. There was also the second season of Peacemaker, a series created by Gunn (as a spin-off of his 2021 movie The Suicide Squad) but whose creation also, weirdly, predates Gunn’s reign over DC. It was also the second season of a television show that premiered three years earlier.

This leads to Lanterns, the first DC show to grow from conception to air under Gunn and Safran. In Variety’s original version of its story, the outlet’s phrasing about the fate of Lanterns made the Internet jump, as it suggested the show, which cultivated a dedicated audience late in the summer, was canceled. “An insider at the company suggests the crime drama isn’t quite capturing the zeitgeist, pointing out the show didn’t get renewed for a second season,” Variety wrote.

Hours later, Variety changed its story to its current phrasing (the new words in bold): “Yet an insider at the company suggests the crime drama isn’t quite capturing the zeitgeist, pointing out the show has yet to be renewed for a second season.”

Variety also added the same source, saying, “People cannot wait to make another season of a hit show.”

You can see why there’s confusion. Gunn and Safran’s dismissal from DC was sudden, blindsiding even the men themselves (according to trades like The Hollywood Reporter) at a time when DC Studios seemed to be in a steady rhythm. Sure, this year’s Supergirl was a fumble, but last year’s Superman had gotten the refreshed DCU off on the right foot, and the recent mid-budget horror thriller Clayface grossed a clean $100 million. Though none of the Gunn/Safran–era movies were eye-watering successes, you also can’t measure long-term impact and sustained popularity of a franchise in box-office milestones alone. That’s not how art works.

It’s especially confusing with Lanterns. Back in October, outlets like Variety reported strong viewership figures for the series on HBO and HBO Max. In Skydance’s own third-quarter earnings call, the studio touted Lanterns as a rousing success, with an average of 22 million global viewers, “among HBO Max’s strongest launches” ever for the platform. To now read from insider sources claiming Lanterns wasn’t “quite capturing the zeitgeist” is downright absurd and frankly an insult to anyone’s intelligence.

Is Lanterns canceled? The good news is it isn’t. The bad news is that it isn’t canceled yet. Lanterns was a key part of Gunn’s grand plans for the DCU. Now that he’s been removed—and his future at the company unclear, despite more projects in development under his guidance—the fate of Lanterns is uncertain, as is the whole DCU.

A likely scenario is that Skydance will burn through all the remaining DC projects and dump them on HBO Max—stuff like Gunn’s Superman sequel Man of Tomorrow, the mockumentary comedy The People vs. Gorilla Grodd, and the animated film Dynamic Duo, which is said to be a highly experimental project using puppetry and animation. Lanterns season 2 could be among them, perhaps fast-tracked to HBO Max, where it would get a swift cancellation regardless of its performance (especially now that audiences will know not to get invested), or it simply doesn’t get made at all. But it’s anyone’s guess what will actually happen.

Whether audiences will get to see John Stewart be the Green Lantern is now in the hands of new studio leaders like Jeremy Latcham, who will replace Gunn and Safran starting December 1. We’ll see if Latcham leads with willpower or through fear.

#6904. The Best Sci-Fi Movies of 2026 (So Far)

Before Avengers: Doomsday and Dune: Part Three compete at the box office, Godzilla returns to remind everyone why he’s called the “King of the Monsters.”

Two of the biggest sci-fi movies of the year are upon us, when Avengers: Doomsday and Dune: Part Three both hit theaters on December 18. We’ll see if either multimillion-dollar blockbuster has the juice to compete with this year’s crop—namely Project Hail Mary, which kicked off the year on a very high note for sci-fi films.

Directed by Phil Lord and Christopher Miller, the Ryan Gosling-starring astronaut film that plays like Interstellar but if Matthew McConaughey had a talking rock for a friend was easily one of the best sci-fi films of the past five years. We also saw The End of Oak Street earlier this summer, and Steven Spielberg returned to the world of aliens with Disclosure Day. Plus, Godzilla Minus Zero just stomped into theaters to remind everyone just why they call him the “King of the Monsters” right before Dunesday.

Until then, these are the best science-fiction movies of 2026.

The atomic monster returns to the screen following the success of Godzilla Minus One, as director Takashi Yamazaki sets up another stellar sequel for Godzilla to pulverize in his fight against King Gedorah. “In this series, Godzilla is a metaphor for war,” the filmmaker told Esquire. “What I wanted to do with this one is see how this family recovers and thrives after having suffered, and then see them survive another challenge.” Plus, leveling some cities with giant monster fights doesn’t hurt either.

The End of Oak Street is the best dinosaur film since Jurassic Park—and yes, I am including all the Jurassic Park sequels in that ranking. David Robert Mitchell’s sci-fi film about a neighborhood that is semi-transported back to prehistoric times stars Ewan McGregor and Anne Hathaway—in yet another great performance this year following The Odyssey—as the parents navigate their new Jumanji-like world.

Esquire’s Eric Francisco said it best when he described the beat-’em-up action flick based on the popular video game series as “incredibly stupid fun.” Noah Centineo and Andrew Koji star as friendly rivals Ryu and Ken alongside an oddball and eccentric cast including David Dastmalchian, Roman Reigns, Cody Rhodes, Andrew Schulz, Eric Andre, Orville Peck, 50 Cent, and Jason Momoa as the game’s various fighters. Still, amid all the chaos, it’s probably Callina Liang’s dedication to Chun-Li that shines through the brightest.

Following Barbarian and Weapons, Zach Cregger’s take on Resident Evil marks the horror director as 3-for-3 in my book. The contained yet still incredibly terrifying story follows a medical courier (Austin Abrams) on the night that a violent virus turns everyone into crazed, mutated killers. While the sci-fi/horror film definitely packs in the scares, I was still surprised how funny and accurate the film was to its video game source material. Among all the video game adaptations over the past few years, Resident Evil felt the most like actually playing the game.

One of the biggest movies of 2026 is, without question, Project Hail Mary. Based on the 2021 novel by Andy Weir, the smash hit follows a middle school science teacher (Ryan Gosling) who awakes with amnesia aboard a spacecraft light-years away from Earth. The newest movie from comedy giants Phil Lord and Christopher Miller was the right movie at the right time, its release accidentally coinciding with the Artemis II mission to the moon. That it was still playing in theaters more than a month after its release—an eternity in today’s theatrical landscape—speaks volumes about its out-of-this-world appeal.

The man who invented the modern summer blockbuster came back to theaters in 2026 with his first sci-fi in years: Disclosure Day. Emily Blunt and Josh O’Connor costar as two people who try to reveal the existence of extraterrestrial life, while the government tries to suppress the truth. It may not have been the glorious throwback to the days of E.T. and Close Encounters, but Disclosure Day gets by on career-best work by Blunt and another immaculate score by John Williams.

Alan Ritchson flexes his leading-man muscles in War Machine, a Netflix original that’s actually kinda worth watching. The Reacher star leads the movie as a staff sergeant for the Army Rangers who encounters a massively overpowered alien force during a training exercise. Sure, you’ve seen movies like this before—Predator, anyone?—but this throwback action flick is the best kind of turn-your-brain-off junk food we secretly crave.

You don’t have to be an overgrown ’80s baby to dig Masters of the Universe. This deeply affectionate reboot of the Reagan-era toy and TV franchise once again tells the story of Prince Adam (Nicholas Galitzine), who must save his fallen home of Eternia from the evil Skeletor (Jared Leto). The franchise started out as a cheap competitor to Star Wars, but the 2026 reboot is anything but a pale copy, with its dazzling visual design, snappy humor, and, most of all, a moving story about reasserting one’s place in the world.

How much you want to consider Marvel superhero movies “sci-fi” depends on you, but we safely consider radioactive spiders, mutant telepaths, and gamma-infused monsters to be firmly in the realm of science fiction. The fourth Tom Holland–led Spider-Man movie sees the web slinger working on his own until an invisible menace with the power to hijack people’s consciousness threatens everyone he still loves. It’s a box-office (Hulk) smash, but Spider-Man: Brand New Day remembers that the true story lies in the hearts of heroes.

If Guy Ritchie made Looper, it might look like Mike & Nick & Nick & Alice. Emerging out of SXSW, the Hulu exclusive follows a gangster (Vince Vaughn) who, after regretting murdering his friend (James Marsden), uses a time machine to stop his past self. There are an awful lot of action comedies nowadays, but Mike & Nick & Nick & Alice stands out from the pack with some novel twists on well-worn time-travel formulas.

Sydney Chandler (Alien: Earth) and Takehiro Hira (Giri/Haji) hit the road in this tender and emotional sci-fi road trip from first-time director Brian Tetsuro Ivie. Set in a speculative near future in which one’s consciousness can be uploaded into the cloud, Anima sees Chandler star as an unemployed engineer who drives a wealthy and terminally ill man (Hira) across Connecticut in the hopes of finding his estranged son before the end of his life. Anima premiered to rave reviews at SXSW earlier this year and may soon find wide-release distribution.

Animated children’s films about woodland creatures are usually a mixed bag. For every Bambi or The Wild Robot, you’ll find a dozen films trying to become the next Over the Hedge. Hoppers dodges the repetitive trend by magically turning a young girl into a woodland creature, and the box-office numbers prove that the gimmick has paid off so far. The film follows Mabel Tanaka, who transports her consciousness into a robot beaver. She wants to rally the animals to protect their wilderness from becoming a highway. It’s a crazy idea. But considering the way we’re headed in 2026, I’m down for anything.

This SXSW darling stars Lewis Pullman and Maya Hawke as a couple with the ability to literally shape their environment. After attending a couples-counseling session run by a set of magic wonder twins, the two on-again, off-again lovers learn that their emotions have dire effects on the world around them. The silly, video-game-like premise isn’t Wishful Thinking’s only draw, however. At its heart, director Graham Parkes’s feature debut really touches on a question we all ask ourselves. How do we change for the person we love without sacrificing what makes us who we are?

Ralph Fiennes’s Dr. Ian Kelson experiments with some real science fiction in 28 Years Later: The Bone Temple, which is one of the first zombie flicks I’ve ever seen to reckon with the idea that a cure is physically attainable. This second entry in a planned sequel trilogy to the original 28 Days Later film also features Jack O’Connell (Sinners) as a maniacal cult leader styled after Jimmy Savile—just tearing up the apocalyptic landscape with Danny Boyle’s zany, violent humor.

#6901. Erdem Is on a Red Carpet Style Streak—Here’s Why

Celebrities from Kim Kardashian to Cynthia Erivo to Troye Sivan have pulled looks

At London Fashion Week this September, Erdem debuted a Spring 2027 collection that was instantly celebrated for its very on-brand tug-of-war between masculinity and femininity. Individual ensembles clashed crystal embellishment, tuxedo details, classic shirting, and unexpected flashes of skin. Critics praised it—and celebrity stylists began calling pieces in almost immediately.

Erdem has long had a slew of celebrity friends, supporters, and customers, but something about the reception this season feels different. In less than two months, runway looks have been worn by Kim Kardashian, Rosalía (twice), Cynthia Erivo, Sienna Miller, Troye Sivan, Honey Dijon, Cara Delevingne, Chappell Roan, Inde Navarette, Rosé, Keira Knightley, and Sarah Paulson. An unlikely collective perhaps, but each individual puts forth their own glorious interpretation of style and gender expression.

Erdem Moralıoğlu began this collection with the story of Barbette, the Texas-born trapeze artist whose performances, done in full drag, rose to prominence in Paris in the 1920s and ’30s. The clothes explore this sort of complex, androgynous dialogue between masculinity and femininity. The idea is perhaps best epitomized in a dress (worn by Knightley) that begins as a menswear-inspired gray knit before fading into a crystal-embellished sheer tulle skirt—and styled in the very Erdem manner with socks and black brogues. Or take Sivan’s ensemble: a black tuxedo jacket with satin and tulle draped over an ostrich feather pannier at the hip. Even the most markedly girlish looks, like Rosalía’s ’20s-inspired dress in blush pink silk, come with a certain restraint.

Moralıoğlu celebrated 20 years of Erdem this year and has built a ready-to-wear brand that is both distinctly recognizable and still able to evolve each season. Spring 2027 was not a radical departure for the designer, merely a looser approach to his typical decadence. (After all, Moralıoğlu has always paired his colorful jacquard dresses with simple black brogues.) It seems like a literal snapshot of consumers and their desires: clothes that combine elements of femininity and masculinity, utility and flourish. And it proves that two decades in, Moralıoğlu can continue to woo new audiences.

Ahead, we spoke with Moralıoğlu to gauge his reaction to all of this attention and reflect on what’s resonating most.

Well, the muse, as you know, was someone named “Barbette,” or Vander Clyde Broadway, who was originally from Austin, Texas, and then came to Paris and became this extraordinary muse and then kind of disappeared into obscurity. I love the idea of this idea of someone becoming something and that idea of self-actualization. But also, more importantly, Cocteau wrote a very interesting essay on Barbette and commissioned Man Ray to do this beautiful photo essay on him, as well. It was really about this idea of how relative masculinity and femininity is and about androgyny. So if I had to summarize it into one thing, there's a feeling of androgyny and flitting between masculine and feminine.

Yes, and I think what's interesting about Barbette as a character was that it was equally as feminine as it was masculine. In a way, they weren't trying to emulate becoming a woman, but they also weren't emulating a man. It's almost like the third sex, which is, I think, very interesting.

I definitely have noticed it, and it's definitely been noticed in the studio. All of us are like, "Wow, another one! It really seems to be happening.” But it's such a privilege to dress so many different people from so many different backgrounds and extraordinary, amazing, brilliant, talented people. I mean, it's wonderful. It's thrilling. I love it.

I mean, I can't pick just one. But I thought it was so interesting to see Troye in that piece, to see how that all worked and how it evolved. Interestingly enough, I had originally created trousers for that look, but didn’t use them for the show. So then the trousers came back out for that carpet. Otherwise, Troye was so loyal to how it was shown on the runway, even with the shirt styled exactly the same.

I still feel the same way. I think it's a privilege to be in a position where you dress extraordinarily talented people. That's a wonderful extension of your world that then becomes something else. It’s really interesting to see the collection take on another life. Otherwise, it just exists in the bubble of a show or an editorial.

The honest answer would be no, I don't think about it at all. I think about the collection and the character—her or them or whoever that person is. But when the show's coming together, you also really think about movement, how it's fitted, how something really moves on the body. And I wonder if that's something that's interesting in the context of it being outside of a runway show. I find Rosalia walking down the street in New York so fascinating. They're clothes that move and I really like that.

Well, I just left the other room where we’re working on the next one. But I like that psychology of turning the page. I find that really interesting. I love the idea of the blank, white page. I find it really exciting. But honest answer, no. [I don’t think about evolution]. Maybe what happens is that in time, you become a little bit looser. With that last show, there was something about that kind of world of Barbette. It was easy for us in the studio to really feel it, feel that masculine and feminine energy and to be very loose with it.

I usually tend to have a character. I always find it's like making a movie every six months. It's almost like a new script. I think it helps to take everyone collectively somewhere else. So you have to adjust together, but you have to have something to take everyone there with you.

Too difficult to say. In the context of these extraordinary women…Cynthia, I just think is amazing. Or Inde. And then Rosalía, Chappell, I'm like…

Let's think. Dietrich. [Marlene] Dietrich would be a good one. I love her.

Well, I think there’s something interesting about this idea of how you have a starting point and then you can end up somewhere else. How do you take the idea of something, and make it modern. How are you looking at something and finding its spirit? When you think about all of these questions of gender and expression, it's something that's always existed culturally. That's also a really beautiful thing.

#6900. Kings of Leon’s Caleb Followill Is Finally Ready to Bare It All

The lead singer tells Esquire in the latest episode of “What I’ve Learned” that the band’s new record features its most honest music to date.

Last year, Kings of Leon was forced to cancel part of its summer tour. The band’s lead singer, Caleb Followill, broke his foot and severely displaced his heel during a freak accident that he jokes was “a rock ’n’ roll injury.” But really, he admits to Esquire, he was just playing tag with his kids—a little roughhousing that turned into eight months of recovery before he could walk back onto the stage.

“I was kind of feeling sorry for myself,” Followill tells Esquire editor in chief Michael Sebastian on the latest episode of “What I’ve Learned.” The singer felt like he had let the band’s fans down. He turned 44, he lost his mother not too long ago, and he channeled all that grief and negative energy into a new, stripped-down Kings of Leon record, O My Beloved, which was released on November 6. “I had a weight on my shoulders,” he says. “And one day I picked up the guitar, and it was almost like I walked through a door and I could see things a little more clearly.”

O My Beloved is the most melancholic and reflective album in Kings of Leon’s ten-album discography. Followill tells Esquire that he wanted to speak about his childhood and the family that shaped him on the new songs because he used to feel embarrassed about his past. His family grew up poor—traveling around the South and living out of their car at times. (Kings of Leon is a family band, with Caleb joined by his brothers Nathan and Jared Followill, as well as their cousin Matthew Followill.) But now Caleb wants to pass all his memories on to his kids, and he doesn’t want them to feel ashamed about any part of the journey that led them here.

“When I was writing these songs, my mind just kept going back to small moments in my life—moments that had no significance,” he says. “I wanted to tell these stories, and I wanted to be honest about parts of my life that I didn’t want to be honest about before."

O My Beloved is the band’s first self-produced record since parting ways with RCA, its longtime record label. It’s also the band’s tenth studio album, celebrating nearly 20 years since the 2008 record Only by the Night rocketed it to stardom thanks to hit songs “Sex on Fire” and “Use Somebody.”

“I was always scared of fame, and I think I still am,” Followill says. “That’s the good thing about having your brothers next to you. They’ll rip you right back down, shine a light in your eyes, and say, ‘Hey man, this can all come crumbling down and we’ll be back in our crumbling house.’ ”

You can listen to the episode on YouTube and Spotify. Elsewhere, Followill shares his secret to 15 years of marriage (to model Lily Aldridge) and what he’s learned from touring with Pearl Jam, U2, and Zach Bryan.

#6898. Is Your Home Too Matchy-Matchy? These 5 Designer Tricks Break Up the Blah and Add Instant Character

If your bedroom design began with a furniture set that matches your nightstands and lamps, it may be time to shake things up. Having a space where everything coordinates perfectly may seem like the easiest route to a stylish home, but many times, it can feel more sterile than a true reflection of who you are and what you love.

“A home often looks sterile when everything comes from the same set,” explains Preeti Shah, founder and principal designer at Spotlight Style.

By stepping out of your comfort zone and switching things up a bit, you can create a cohesive look that still brings character and depth to your home. Here are five designer tips to steer you in the right direction.

Just because one of your surfaces is natural white oak doesn't mean everything in the same room needs to follow suit.

“We love mixing dark wood tones with light tones and everything in between,” says Leah Hook, founder and principal designer at Gray Oak Studio.

Repeating wood tones can make a room feel cohesive, but you don't need to use the same tone everywhere. Think outside the box and look for subtle ways to tie the space together, such as repeating a wood tone in an art frame or decorative tray.

Believe it or not, your island does not have to match your kitchen countertops in every possible way.

“Choose a more exciting stone for your island. You can even miter it two inches thick instead of keeping everything the standard 1.25-inch thickness,” explains Gaelle Dudley, founder and creative director at GLDESIGN.

Dining chairs are almost always purchased as a matching set, but sourcing different styles from flea markets or Facebook Marketplace can give your dining room a fun, unexpected look.

“Select just one element from the table that will be repeated in the chairs, and go with something from a different collection or even an entirely different furniture line,” says Pattie Kelly, certified interior decorator and owner of Inspired Home Interiors.

Consider the height of the chairs (so that they still make sense together at the table) and how much room you have to pull them out in (so you’re not running into the wall behind them).

The most interesting homes display a mix of different art styles. Instead of sticking to one color palette or aesthetic, seek out pieces that contrast. For instance, Shah is a fan of contrasting graphic black-and-white drawings with gold-veined abstract canvases in various rooms of the home.

Speaking of artwork, as the space pictured above by Nina Garbiras illustrates, don’t be afraid to mix and match your frame materials. Just keep one element consistent, like the mat size or the frame width, so that it still has a curated (not chaotic) feel.

Just because you’re choosing one type of fixture finish doesn’t mean you have to repeat it everywhere. In fact, your space will look more “expensive” if you match shiny finishes very minimally.

For example, the above space by Vestige Home showcases how well brass and matte-black hardware can play off each other, adding depth, harmony, and visual interest. Choosing to go with one finish throughout this space would’ve become too matchy-matchy very quickly.

#6885. The Ultimate Guide to the Rolex Daytona, the World’s Most Wanted Watch

From the hand-wound watches dealers struggled to sell to the steel model that commands an impressive premium today.

In September, four Rolex Daytonas sold at a charity auction in London for a combined £37.3 million (about $49.5 million). The most expensive, at £10.3 ($13.7) million, had a turquoise dial and a ring of pink sapphires. That same month, Rolex unveiled a steel-and-platinum Daytona with a white enamel dial and a speed scale that recalled the original from 1963.

The regular steel Daytona with a white dial costs $16,900 from Rolex, if you can get one. In September, WatchCharts put its secondary-market value around $35,000. It wasn’t always like this. In its early years, according to Sotheby’s, retailers discounted Daytonas to clear stock.

Rolex launched the Cosmograph in 1963. An early advert called it the “Le Mans,” after the French endurance race. The name that stuck came from Daytona, Florida, where cars raced on hard-packed sand before the city had a speedway.

The first model, reference 6239, had a movement you wound by hand and two buttons to operate the stopwatch. Rolex put the speed scale on the steel bezel, where a driver could read it at a glance. The three small counters stood out against the dial, black on a light face or light on a black one.

By the 1970s, two models had emerged that collectors still prize today. The 6263 had a black acrylic bezel, and the 6265 had a metal one. Both had screw-down collars around the stopwatch buttons. On some, the word Daytona curves in red above the bottom counter. Collectors call these “Big Reds.”

Joanne Woodward gave Newman a 6239 around the time he made Winning, the 1969 film that sparked his interest in racing. She had “DRIVE CAREFULLY ME” engraved on the back. “There’s something almost magical about it,” James Cox, who later owned the watch, told Phillips. In 2017 it sold for $17.75 million, then an auction record for a wristwatch.

A “Paul Newman” usually refers to a type of dial. Look for the unusual numerals and small square-ended markers in the counters. The dials were unpopular when new. By 2013, Christie’s could sell 50 Daytonas in a single Geneva auction for $13.2 million. The top lot was a steel watch with a Paul Newman dial. It fetched $1.09 million, four years before the actor’s own watch went to auction.

On one particularly rare black-dial 6263, the word Oyster appears below Rolex Cosmograph. Collectors call it the Oyster Sotto, from the Italian for under. Phillips sold one in 2025 for 1.39 million Swiss francs (approximately $1.67 million). “One mistake people make is that they expect everything to fit neatly into a specific set of rules,” Geoff Hess, global head of watches at Sotheby’s, once explained. A dial can be genuine Rolex and still have come from a different watch.

In 1988 the steel Daytona became the 16520. It grew to 40mm, gained raised gold hour markers and a glossier dial, and wound itself as you wore it. Rolex extensively reworked Zenith’s El Primero movement for the watch. It was the first automatic Daytona and the model that established the basic dimensions of the watch Rolex still makes today.

On some early examples, a gap makes the word Cosmograph appear to float beneath the rest of the lettering. On certain black dials, the rings around the counters have turned brown with age. Those are known as Patrizzi dials, after the auctioneer Osvaldo Patrizzi, who noticed the change.

Three steel references are worth knowing. The 116520, introduced in 2000, has a steel bezel and the first chronograph movement Rolex developed and made itself. The 116500LN, from 2016, brought back the look of the black acrylic bezel in ceramic. The current 126500LN, introduced in 2023, has a slim steel rim around that ceramic bezel, a revised dial, and a new movement that runs for about three days when fully wound. The two ceramic models can look remarkably similar in listing photographs.

The unicorn is a hand-wound 6265 that Phillips described in 2018 as the only known vintage Daytona in white gold. It sold for 5.94 million Swiss francs (about $7.1 million). The Rainbow gets its name from the graduated sapphires around its bezel. A white-gold example made around 1994, nearly two decades before the production versions appeared, sold in 2024 for 5.505 million Swiss francs (approximately $6.1 million).

Sixty years after that early advertisement, Rolex returned to Le Mans. Its 2023 watch for the race’s centenary has a stopwatch that counts the full 24 hours. The regular Daytona counts to 12.

An official Rolex retailer is the place to start if you want a new one at list price. Retailers manage their own allocations, so availability varies. Rolex addressed the shortage in 2021. “The scarcity of our products is not a strategy on our part,” it said, explaining that it could not meet demand without compromising quality.

On the secondary market, compare the same reference and dial. The WatchCharts September estimate for the current black-dial steel model was about $29,000, roughly $6,000 less than the white. If you’re buying vintage, ask a specialist dealer or auction house for a condition report. Find out whether the case has been heavily polished, which parts have been replaced, and whether the dial is correct for that particular watch. On a Daytona, those details can explain more about the price than the reference number.

Like her sister, Sasha also lives in L.A. However, the younger Obama daughter has kept her career private since her 2023 graduation from the University of Southern California.

#6879. Why Wallpaper Is the Secret to Making Even the Smallest Bathroom Feel Special

A little pattern can turn into a true design moment.

When you’re working with limited space, it’s almost instinctive to tone down your design details to avoid feeling overwhelmed—but even small spaces need character. Our 2026 Whole Home in Charleston is a master class in excellent design, no matter the room size or purpose. This year’s top recommendation for a chic bath is wallcoverings.

When installed properly, wallcoverings are one of the most reliable ways to add an extra touch of flair to interiors. Here’s how the designers of our 2026 Whole Home used them to bring big personality to small spaces.

For many designers, powder rooms are the perfect opportunity to play. Designer Mark Kennamer, founder and principal designer of his eponymous Birmingham, Alabama–based firm, used wallcoverings to add bespoke character to a nearly monochromatic powder-pink scheme. In the guest bath, the Zoffany mauve moiré wallcovering lends a subtle shimmer and dimension, while its wood-like grain adds an unexpected, welcome layer of texture.

Anissa Zajac of House Seven Design outfitted the primary bathroom with a botanical-printed wallcovering by Sanderson that incorporated nearly every hue in her palette while adding a dose of whimsy. The pattern’s vertical lines play off the texture of the fluted vanity cabinets, making a bold statement without straying too far from the design scheme.

In the gallery bath, the team at R&R Interior Design 365 took inspiration from Charleston’s famed cobblestone roads. They opted for a blue-brick wallpaper by Perennials to channel the perfect level of city flair while keeping the feel warm, intimate, and inviting.

#6876. Tommy Lee Jones Couldn’t Care Less About His Legacy

The screen legend has more interesting things going on—including a stellar turn on The Lowdown season 2, as an estranged, crusty old father.

Tommy Lee Jones, 80, an Oscar winner and a star of the second season of The Lowdown, the noirish Tulsa crime story undercut with terrific dime-store waggery, sits flat-footed in the green room at Good Morning America, in downtown Manhattan. He has just done an on-camera interview with Ethan Hawke, who stars as the show’s protagonist. In the next dressing room are many adorable, pawing puppies being primped for a segment on National Adopt-a-Dog Month. Jones is adorable too, in his avuncular way, his face an old-dog scowl that softens when you ask the right question, as if you’d scratched him in the right spot.

On The Lowdown, he plays Hawke’s estranged father, a crusty old bastard who holds secrets. There’s a bad history between them, and Hawke’s character, an impulsive, truth-seeking investigative journalist, at first wants nothing to do with his dad, but that situation evolves. They’re both named Lee Raybon.

Jones wears a glen-plaid sport coat, a blue dress shirt, and comfortable sneakers. When the interview begins, he looks at you, ready but giving nothing until he’s asked to.

ESQUIRE: I just watched the first six episodes of this season, and man, I wasn’t ready for [REDACTED].

TOMMY LEE JONES: Oh, you watched all those episodes? The [REDACTED] scene was—I hope that was kind of shocking.

We didn’t see it coming, which I guess is a testament to you all.

That’s the whole point. You didn’t see it coming. That’s the reason we did it.

The way you developed your character’s way of speaking, the way he trails off here and there, tells us that this guy knows some stuff he’s not giving away. He’s shifty. It’s just brilliant. I’m just trying to give a compliment, not really asking a question.

You said to Charlie Rose in an interview a long time ago that there’s no through line that runs through the characters you play. At the same time, you don’t take just any role that comes along. Do you ever think about legacy? How are they going to remember Tommy Lee Jones? Or is it really just “This role would be a challenge to play, so I’m going to take it?”

I don’t have long-term thinking, and I don’t even think in a term like legacy. I think more like a dog. I’m absorbed with what’s in front of me. I might run off and chase a squirrel.

I believe you. It’s just that I’ve started thinking recently, What am I writing about? Is there a body of work here? I hadn’t thought that way before. With each story it was just This is a cool story. I’ll do this story.

That’s what I thought. I thought this show was really cool. The people that were working on it were cool, and Tulsa is an extremely cool place. Ethan made a good point on TV just now when he said good noir stories always have a sense of place, so the location was very important. It’s true about film noir, and this show really qualifies as part of that genre. A keen sense of place, leading to inarguable originality.

How far are Tulsa and eastern Oklahoma from where you live in Texas?

Oh, I don’t know. It’s northeast of Dallas. So I think the drive up—

[He turns to his wife of of 25 years, Dawn, who sits in the corner looking at her phone.]

How long would it take to drive to Tulsa from San Antonio?

When I was in prep school, we went up to Oklahoma to play a school in football, and they always cheated. Also when I was in high school, they would sell 3.2 beer to minors, and it was fun, a big adventure every once in a while, to pile into a car and drive to Oklahoma to buy a beer.

Ethan’s hilarious on The Lowdown, but you’re also very funny! Always have been. The warden in Natural Born Killers, Dwight McClusky, is your funniest role, I feel. Samuel Gerard in The Fugitive. And then I’m watching this show and was reminded how funny you can be. How would you describe your sense of humor? What’s funny to you?

People. People are hilarious. The best humor is found in human behavior. Any student of human behavior is going to be highly amused, when they’re not horrified.

Did you watch stuff as a kid that made you laugh?

The Three Stooges. Slapstick. Warner Brothers cartoons. That’s fundamental humor. I mean, if I see a fat lady slip on a banana peel, I swear I’m going to laugh.

You share some beautiful scenes with the young actress who plays your granddaughter, Ryan Kiera Armstrong. I couldn’t think of many films in which we see you spend a lot of time with children.

There was a movie called River Rats, and I was on a boat for a good part of this movie with two children, one of whom was Martha Plimpton, as a little slip of a girl, playing my daughter. We became good friends on that, and I was happy to see her on The Lowdown.

You and Ethan, and Martha now too, are seasoned veterans. Is the approach different when you’re working with young Ryan, who doesn’t have 50 films under her belt? Is there a greater need with a child actor to get to know each other before filming?

Oh, actors are actors, so it’s not difficult for me to relate to an actor regardless of their age. And she’s a pro. Actors, they have a way of getting to know one another very quickly.

Yeah, she is. And she’s a fearless and admirable young lady.

[Shit.] Not from you. I don’t mean in your scenes. I mean her other scenes.

Getting back to the idea of a through line: If I may point out one commonality across your performances, it’s that your characters have wisdom. You yourself are obviously well-read. You went to Harvard, and, for example, you’ve compared the satire of Natural Born Killers to Molière. Is reading still a big part of your life?

I’ve been making a study of Texas history, which begins with—well, there is no beginning. It’s always been there. But its experience as an American territory, for me, begins with James K. Polk. I read a political biography of Polk. That’s a good place to start a study of modern Texas history.

Yeah, well, he was Andrew Jackson’s bitch.

Getting back to Lee Raybon Sr., whose nickname is Frog, and his son. Fathers and sons are always rich territory.

Ethan’s character changes his attitude toward you. It gets better. Did you see that as Frog’s project, what this character is after—his son’s love?

[Mildly annoyed.] Well, I compared myself to a dog earlier because I really deal with what’s in front of me.

And all the qualities that you see in that character are ones that Sterling Harjo, the show’s creator, wrote. We learn that this character is dying. That slowly begins to dawn on us, and we see that he’s trying to somehow reconcile life with his son and get to know his granddaughter. But that quest in this character is not advertised. It’s discovered as we watch things unfold in an often amusing way, and always original. That was appealing. I mean, I think that’s the way it works.

Can I, at the risk of pissing you off: I know you say that you’re like a dog. Are you telling me that—

I don’t want to belabor that too much. I’m a little bit like a dog. [Laughs, kind of.]

But this is what I’m getting at. I’ve seen every film you’ve ever made, and you don’t give a lot of interviews, but in the ones I’ve seen over the years you’ve made it clear that each character is its own separate project. No connections. But at the same time, I know how smart you are, and so part of me thinks, Come on. Of course you must see some continuum along the characters you play. We see similarities among characters in literature, even across different authors in different centuries. The through line of all your parts is that you chose them. So pushing back a tiny bit, I’m suggesting that your performance as Lee Raybon has inherited something from these previous men that you’ve inhabited in the last 50 years. Is that fair at all?

[Breathing a sigh of relief, and perhaps satisfaction.] Okay.

[Pauses, hoping this line of questioning was over.] Oh. Well, I don’t know. I’m not aware of any continuum. [Any sense of Esquire’s relief or satisfaction disappears.] I don’t think it’s in my best interest, and I’m not inclined to think about what all the characters I play have in common. The only thing they have in common is that I’m playing them. And they’re all built out of experience. Let me refer you to an essay by T.S. Eliot called “Tradition and the Individual Talent.” It’s an indication of what happens to actors, really. Their performances are made out of the shards and the pieces and the bits of your experience of life and your thinking about it. One of the reasons you read that essay is so you have a better understanding of The Waste Land or even James Joyce. Modern art is comprised of the shards of experience, reassembled in a way that has a dramatic form, with a beginning, a middle, an end, and a purpose.

Do you see a performance such as this one as a piece of art?

No. Not really. I don’t know what it—you know, this is the thing I never give any thought to: What is art?

Compare your view of God when you were six or seven years old to now.

[Coming to life a bit.] Oh! Well? I never imagined a man in a white robe with a long white beard sitting on a throne mounted on a cloud with some lightning bolts in his hand. That’s one image. It’s probably almost as good as any other, but the question is, you either believe in such a thing as God or you don’t. I do.

Has your view or understanding changed over your life?

Not really. My grandmother and my aunts and my mother always took me to a Southern Baptist church. I was baptized in the Southern Baptist church. And, like all organized religions, there’s a good deal of nonsense in it. But it also leads to an awareness of the deity, which by definition exceeds our capability to define it.

[A long pause as Jones stares at the floor, then looks up, eyebrows raised and smiling.]

That was pretty good. I’m gonna write that down.

If you had to cook a meal for dinner, what’s the best thing you could come up with?

[For the first time during the interview, Jones’s body relaxes—arm over the back of the chair.]

I’d probably buy some pork ribs, an entire side of a little young pig, and slowly barbecue that over a mesquite fire and serve it with a mixture of sautéed yellow squash and onions and probably a sweet potato. You can scratch them with a fork and rub them down with butter, wrap them up in tinfoil and cook them alongside the pig right over the fire. You can feed a lot of people that way, and it tastes good. And it’s fun to eat.

There’s a product you can buy in San Saba, Texas, called Bill’s Season All. Bill’s Season All is something you sprinkle on pork ribs and it makes them taste real good. I don’t know what all’s in it, but it’s delectable.

Texas chili: Do you have strong opinions?

[Jones snaps to even more, speaking a little louder and with some of the gusto and urgency of U.S. Marshal Samuel Gerard saying, “Listen up, ladies and gentlemen. Our fugitive has been on the run for 90 minutes. Average foot speed over uneven ground barring injuries is four miles an hour, and that gives us a radius of six miles. What I want out of each and every one of you is a hard-target search of every gas station, residence, warehouse, farmhouse, henhouse, outhouse, and doghouse in that area. Checkpoints go up at 15 miles. Your fugitive’s name is Dr. Richard Kimble. Go get him.”]

Don’t use ground beef. You need to cube it. Cube that beef into pieces about the size of the dice that you play Monopoly with. And after that, your own creativity comes in with the spices. But it’s important to avoid ground beef. And you don’t put beans in chili. That’s on the side. That’s at the discretion of the diner. Some people just like chili, but they just don’t necessarily want to see a bunch of beans floating in it. You’ll find one recipe in the Hill Country of Texas and another one in the hills above Santa Fe. Quite different.

Last night, I was watching either the fifth or sixth episode of The Lowdown, and Martha Plimpton’s character talks about putting a couple spoonfuls of Tang in chili.

I can’t imagine putting Tang in chili. But chili is like gumbo. Or Kentucky burgoo. Or Irish stew. It’s a food that’s in and of the people, so the way of cooking it and the ingredients vary from place to place and from circumstance to circumstance. Real gumbo has always got—well, it can have anything in it. Duck … goose … whatever crawled across the porch last night. Chili’s the same kind of thing. It’s the food of the people, and as such, the main ingredients are whatever you’ve got.

I don’t think so. I’m closer to profligate than frugal. We spend a lot of money on horses. My wife is one of the better female polo players in the United States. I played polo for 40-some-odd years. I’m a bit too old for it now. “Old and worn-out” is what they would call it.

Where did you live when you lived here in New York?

Yeah, it was. When I first came here in 1969, the streets were covered in dog shit. And it really got to be bad. Land mines. They passed some ordinances in the city that people had to clean up after their dogs. You’d have to be very careful walking down the street. Nothing more embarrassing than going into a meeting with dog shit on your shoes.

So now that you’ve turned in this astonishingly beautiful performance as Lee Raybon—

—and are there hills left to conquer as an actor? Are there types of roles you have yet to play?

I don’t know. There’s a schedule for the day. And I looked at it, but I don’t really—I’d have to ask her. That’s the reason I don’t have to know. I have her.

Okay, well, I appreciate this. I hope it wasn’t too painful and I didn’t—

I know talking about acting is not the most interesting thing.

Well, it’s breakfast time. When it’s first thing in the morning at home, if your cook’s not there and you’re hungry, what could you whip up?

I have a French press for coffee, a toaster for a bagel. And we’ve always got cream cheese and smoked salmon. Bagel, lox, cream cheese, coffee, and a grapefruit. That’s what I ordinarily eat for breakfast.

You’re right, this is more interesting than talking about acting.

Oh, breakfast. Breakfast is more interesting than anything.
