#6818. WNBA Champion Gabby Williams: ‘I’m My Best When I Don’t Have a Choice’

The Golden State Valkyries star and Finals MVP on the moment when everything changed in Game 7.

At the end of the first half of Game 7 of the WNBA Finals, the Golden State Valkyries trailed the Atlanta Dream by six points. The Valkyries’ star, Gabby Williams, one of the best two-way players in the league, had scored a mere two points. But when the teams returned to Ballhalla—as Golden State’s Chase Center arena in San Francisco is lovingly nicknamed—for the second half, it was Williams’s time to shine. She took over, going on a 15-point run, including back-to-back-to-back three-point shots that flipped her team’s deficit into a narrow lead. By the time the buzzer sounded at the end of the game, Williams had 30 points and the Valkyries were WNBA champions.

Before she won the title—and was named Finals MVP—Williams, who was raised in Nevada but spent her summers in the Bay Area, played basketball in college for the UConn Huskies, winning back-to-back national championships. She was drafted by the Chicago Sky in 2018, before joining the Seattle Storm in 2022 and the Valkyries in 2026. As a dual French-American citizen, she also plays for the French national team, including at the 2020 and 2024 Olympics, where she led the team to bronze and silver medals, respectively.

Just over a week after she became a champion, Williams came to New York City to make the media rounds before taking a much-needed and well-deserved spa day. Below, she reflects on coming to the Valkyries, her performance during Game 7, and what little Gabby would think of her now.

It was the longest season I’ve ever played, but it went by so fast. I felt like it flew by; it was just such a really fun group of people—the staff, even the medical staff, the communication staff—everyone was just always great energy. I didn’t want it to end. Usually during playoffs, I am already playing overseas or on vacation, and watching the finals this year, I was like, “Dang, everyone else has gone and moved on and I’m still in season,” which was such a great feeling, obviously. But wow, it was long. And the longer it went on, the harder it was to sleep and eat with all the stress and stuff, but I still loved every moment of it.

I think it’s just, how do you define a star? I just define stars as people who win, as people who aren’t selfish, as good people, as good role models, as good teammates. So, I think we had 12 stars on the team, really. I know that’s what made us different and everyone is going to try to copy us now. It’s just about finding great people who want to win versus a superstar who wants accolades and stats. And then you win a championship.

We knew what we had from day one. I went to the Valkyries because I wanted to contend for a championship. That was my biggest thing in free agency—I didn’t want to go to a team that maybe I felt like wasn’t going to try to win a championship or at least didn’t have ambitions to. At the beginning of the season, obviously we’re figuring things out, but we figured it out very quickly, and we could tell just the culture of the group was a good one, and that we had something special. So, we always knew we had what it took to win and that’s all that we needed.

I wanted to go somewhere where I wouldn’t dread going to the gym every day. I think anyone, whether you play basketball or any sport, or any profession that you have, you just want to want to go to work every day—that’s a huge thing. I think you’re able to perform better when you’re in that mindset, and I knew that I would need that in order to win. So, that was one of the few questions I asked [Valkyries guard] Veronica Burton in my free agency. I was just like, “How did you feel showing up every day?” Because everyone’s going to say, “Oh, the team is great...” But you can’t lie about how the locker room is and the work environment. And she was like, “Every day was a blast, we have fun, and I genuinely enjoy coming in every day,” and that was all I needed to hear.

It started with Ohemaa [Nyanin], our general manager, and the selectiveness she uses in choosing not just players, but people. I remember that being a big thing when I was being recruited by UConn as well. They were like, “Yes, we’re looking for great players, but we’re looking for a certain type of kid too, because we have a culture here.” I saw a similar thing with Ohemaa and what she wanted to build. It was also the first time that I played for a staff that didn’t point fingers at each other. And as players, you follow their lead, so then players don’t point fingers at one another either. Also, when we went on road trips for away games, they would go by so fast because we all would spend time together. Normally, you just want to sit in your hotel room all day alone and then go home, but we wanted to spend time with one another.

There wasn’t even a team in the Bay when I was playing basketball when I was young. So it’s one of those things where people ask you what your biggest dream is and you say it’s to win a championship, but you can’t even imagine that it’s going to be in your hometown, or in the team’s second year, or your first year there. I couldn’t even dream that big. So that was a really cool moment. I’ve been watching the Warriors win since I was a kid, and having the female version of that now, and knowing how big of a party it was in the Bay when the Warriors won, and being like, “Now I get to do that?” That’s so crazy.

Don’t get me wrong, I understand the story that everyone wants to create, so I’m not mad at it that people go that route. But I think that was the American storyline. I noticed the American media and fans were really stuck on [France losing to the U.S.] at the Olympics and FIBA. I’m like, I’ve always been hungry for a W championship; I didn’t have to get two silvers to want that. And then all of the European media was like, “She won Euro league. She won the silver medal. Can she cap it off with the WNBA championship?” So, it was weird seeing these two very different ways of storytelling. Either way, I didn’t need any more motivation to try to win a WNBA championship.

We were all sad at first. I remember the whole group was really sad when we lost just because we loved each other so much and we had a really good group. But then shortly after that night, we were like, “Oh, we just got the first silver medal in France’s history.”

I learned this phrase from Coach [Geno] Auriemma [at UConn]. He’s like, “You see the best athletes when they don’t have a choice—they perform at their best. That’s when stars are created and big moments are created,” and that’s always stuck with me. I always have that playing in my head during those certain moments like, “Okay, I don’t have a choice. I just have to do it.” I even said it to Nat [Nakase, the Valkyries head coach] the day before Game 7, and she didn’t like it. She was like, “Okay, we’re going to switch your matchup. It’s something you haven’t done before; we’re going to ask you to do this in the first half. Can you do it?” And I was like, “Yeah, I’m my best when I don’t have a choice.” And she was like, “I don’t like that.” I was like, “Trust me, trust me. I like these moments.” I think now she gets it.

Yes and no. This game, more than any game I’ve played in my entire career, I didn’t really fully understand in the moment what I was doing until I went back and watched it. And I just watched the game for the first time two days ago. I’d seen highlights before of course, but I hadn’t watched it from start to finish. And watching it, I was like, whoa, I didn’t even realize half of the stuff I did. Because it’s such a high-pressure moment, and we’re very big on every possession matters. I didn’t even realize that my threes were back-to-back-to-back until I got home that night and saw it on Instagram. There was a point where I did feel the basket slowly start to feel bigger, but I wouldn’t say I knew exactly in the moment what I was doing.

I just couldn’t wait to celebrate with my teammates. It’s the moment you see on TV since you were a kid, where the whole team comes together and huddles and jumps and up and down on the court. I’ve had those moments before overseas, but it just was different after a seven-game series. To finally have it, I just couldn’t wait.

I just couldn’t believe it. I mean, I didn’t need MVP to feel as happy as I’m feeling today. Even now I don’t have the words for it. I was like, “I’m the one that gets to hold this trophy?” I didn’t even need it. This is enough. I’m just feeling really lost for words and lucky.

Oh man, she would be so happy. I hope little Gabby would understand the environment that she’s in is so loving. Just my village that I’ve had all year—I think she’d be really happy to know that she has that. And then also just going into an environment every day where people are smiling and dancing and laughing, and then you get to cap it off with a championship in the community. Yeah, she’d be really proud; she’d think I was cool.

It’s bittersweet because you just know you’re living in a moment that you know will never, ever happen again as far as this exact group, this exact timing, etc. So, the only thing that we really wanted to do after we won, was just spend as much time together as we could. Because we were like, “Oh, Ceci [Cecilia “Ceci” Zandalasini] is going overseas tomorrow; Ami [Aminata Gueye] has to go to the national team. So, it was just slowly saying goodbye to people. Everyone was like, “Well, did you party? Did you go out and stuff?” And I was like, “Oh, we just wanted to hang out.”

I wish I would’ve fought for myself a lot earlier than I did. I think I settled a lot. I was talking to Auriemma at UConn yesterday. I woke him up from his nap by accident. But he said something that really resonated with me. He was like, “I see people who have the same rough start to their career, and they kind of just end up blaming it on the coach or someone else and not living up to their full potential.” And he was like, “I’m so happy that you didn’t end up being that story.” He was like, “I’m so proud of you for taking control of your own story and making it what it is.”

Yeah, I think I was content being whatever they wanted me to be. They would call me the Swiss Army Knife, like, “Whatever you need.” I think I was a bit too complacent in that role in the W. I think it’s just my mindset: What does the team need? I always put the team first. But that means I didn’t fight for myself to have more responsibility or speak up more and understand that my voice does have the power it does. And that’s also why I wanted to go to Golden State because that was something like, Ohemaa talked me into free agency. She’s like, “I want to push you in being a leader. I want to push you on and off the court into being more vocal.” So, I’m glad I learned it now.

When he said that, I got really scared at first. But then at the same time, I was like, “Alright, you’re asking me to step up to the plate too.” It gave me motivation because I was like, this man really believes in me, wholeheartedly, for him to go to the media and say that and put his reputation on the line. So, it gave me confidence, it gave me joy of just like, “I can’t believe I’m in an organization that thinks so highly of me.” Because I’ve never asked for him to say things like that. So, the fact that he did means a lot. It felt great to be appreciated.

Just how much fun it was for everybody—that’s what sports are made for. We get lost in the competing for trophies and we forget the moments we create. Ballhalla [where the Valkyries play] was such an experience for everyone who lived it this year. I read there was like 20,000 people outside of the arena during Game 7 in addition to the 18,000 inside screaming their heads off. I hope that’s what people remember. Because that’s also what the Bay Area is about, and that’s what it’s like to win in the Bay. I’m saying that as a fan. So, I hope the party just doesn’t stop.

More of the same. I’m happy to have signed on for three years, knowing the ambition and the motivation from everyone there. And Nat is already talking about what needs to be done for next year.

It’s been cool being a part of a trailblazing franchise—that’s been the best part. We just want to continue to make history. That’s also what’s fun about an expansion team—everything is a first. I think we have a lot more firsts ahead of us.

Competing against each other is easy because it’s just work; it’s a job. And I know her style really well, so that helps. [Laughs.] During the finals, it was so nice because she gets it. She knows, “Okay, today is the day where Gabby’s going to need to think about something else.” And then, “Today’s a day where Gabby may want to hear my opinion on some things, or she might want to talk it out.” Or “Okay, she doesn’t want to talk it out.” And not taking anything personally, like, “Okay, if Gabby just wants to be quiet and watch a movie today, I know why.” So, it’s very helpful having someone who’s in the same environment as you. Also, the night before the biggest game of your life, and you’re playing everything over in your head a million times, having a million thoughts, just being able to go to a dinner and laugh, and think about something else for two hours of your day, it makes a huge difference. Then, you come back refreshed the next morning, your brain is clear, your mind is ready to take on the next challenge because you’ve been able to compartmentalize. Because when you’re by yourself and the thoughts are just going and going and going, and it can be miserable.

Playing each other in the final would have been cool too, though. The matchup would have benefited us too. [Laughs.]

It’s been a tough balance because I understand the importance of having queer representation, and what that means to people. But obviously we’re very, very private, so it’s pretty daunting having, I don’t know, all of the parasocialness and people thinking they know you or your relationship and hearing opinions about it. So, we try to protect our relationship as much as we can, but at the same time, I understand how important it can be for girls and women looking up to us. So there’s give and take. We try to stay as private as we can, but at the same time, we’re not going to hide who we are.

It was funny because there was a time in the season where my teammates all found out that I had a tattoo machine and they were like, “Oh, if we win, let’s do this.” And then we won and everything just happened so fast that we didn’t have time [for me to tattoo everyone]. We talked about doing it one night, and I was like, “Guys, I can’t tonight, I’m exhausted.” But if it happens eventually, I’ll definitely say yes to it.

She actually came over to help me get ready for this interview and we were just talking about our favorite looks. We loved the tie one. My stylist was a seamstress and designer before this; I’m the first person she’s ever styled. But she’s really an amazing seamstress, and she made an entire top out of ties. And we were like, that was the best one. And then she made me another one out of zippers that I’m excited to wear. It’s literally a corset made out of zippers. She’s so creative.

This interview has been edited and condensed for clarity.

These are the deals we’re watching in the lead-up to the gifting season.

Every item on this page was hand-picked by a House Beautiful editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Everyone’s making their Black Friday/Cyber Monday lists (and checking them twice) this month. With the holiday extravaganza almost here, we’re tracking which home brands are having the best deals, and one of the biggest retailers that’s running deals is Amazon. Our team’s approach to shopping the items on the internet megastore is not to just buy whatever is the most discounted; instead, we’re adding items to our cart right now, and then waiting to see if the price actually drops on Black Friday.

Thankfully, many of the items we want are already on sale. Will the prices drop more on Black Friday? Perhaps, but these items may also go back to full price, in which case the best time to buy is right now. Our editors are looking for a wide range of products, from kitchen essentials to decorative accents to actual furniture. If you’re not sure what to buy from Amazon yet, let our wish list inspire your own, ahead. #6814. 34 Unique Christmas Tree Themes That'll Inspire You This Holiday Season

From bejeweled ornaments to popcorn garlands, these ideas bring major holiday cheer.

When it comes to Christmas tree decor, some folks keep it classic, using the same ornaments every year, while others choose new themes. Both approaches capture the charm and spirit of the season. However, if you find yourself in the latter cohort, you know how hard it can be to find yearly inspiration that will wow your guests and blend with your existing decor. So, if you’re in need of a muse, we’ve curated a list of tree themes so stunning, you’ll want to repeat them next year.

While many people stick to the traditional charm of classic hues, this year offers bold alternatives. From glittering jeweled ornaments to vintage-inspired touches like tinsel, popcorn garlands, and flocked branches, social feeds are buzzing with fresh, trendy ideas and new possibilities. There's a lot of Christmas creativity to spread the cheer this year.

To spark your imagination, we've compiled 34 merry and bright Christmas tree themes that are sure to bring jolly spirits. Whether you're drawn to the rustic farmhouse aesthetic or punchy plum color trends, these seasonal styles will infuse your home with magic and joy—and as a bonus, they look fantastic on both real and faux firs.

A Christmas tree can quickly become crowded, so take inspiration from Anthropologie and Terrain's holiday house and swap your standard ornaments for oversized baubles. These larger, multicolored orbs make a true statement, especially when paired with vibrant lights or flocked branches.

Enjoying a warm drink after a long day of skiing was the inspiration for this miniature tree. Featuring gloves, boots, and a Santa with his own pair of skis, take inspiration and decorate a tree dedicated to your favorite seasonal hobby.

For those who like their ornaments to be matchy-matchy, it might be time to start collecting. Anthropologie displayed an adorable miniature tree decked out with festive mice. It's accompanied by a village fit for their Christmas-ready friends.

Ideal for the girly-girl in your life, or maybe even a coastal holiday setting, think outside of the box and abandon the evergreen altogether this year. Instead, take inspiration from design firm BEFoundCollective's white Christmas tree, decorated with cheerful pastel ornaments.

There are few things cozier than a flocked Christmas tree. This one by Pottery Barn is adorned with cozy and metallic ornaments like stars, birds, and even pinecones. This Christmas tree theme is great for a mountain or woodsy cabin, but you can even bring that countryside Christmas energy into your city apartment.

A soothing yet vibrant shade, you can't go wrong with a Christmas tree decked out in peach and orange tones. The silver tinsel contrasts perfectly while adding some glitz and glam. This tree, by the design firm BEFoundCollective in Virginia, showcases vintage ornaments among the lush foliage.

Interior designer Beata Heuman ties dozens of bows on the branches of her showroom's Christmas tree for easy and low-cost DIY decorations. Use ribbons in the traditional Christmas color scheme, match your interior's decor, or, like Heuman, choose hues from the entire rainbow.

Perfect for warmer climates (or those wishing spring would circle back around already), forgo traditional ornaments and adorn your tree with faux florals. Take inspiration from the Winterthur Museum, Garden, and Library in Delaware and use more voluminous blooms like hydrangeas as filler between smaller flowers like mums or daffodils.

A classic color scheme beloved by modern, vintage, and even Art Deco lovers, a black and gold palette is timeless. Make your tree stand tall with a bold trunk cover and top with a snowflake-inspired star to tie your entire decor scheme together.

Topped with a cheerful snowman, this Christmas tree theme is all about the seasonal icons we've come to love. Gnomes and mini Santas stand out against the lush foliage of the tree while striped baubles and twinkling stars glam up the traditional red-and-white color palette.

If your little one is still running around singing "Part of Your World" from The Little Mermaid, pay tribute to their favorite princess with a sophisticated Christmas tree theme. Chock-full of glittering mermaids, chic metal whales, and glossy sea turtles, this tree designed by Pottery Barn is the perfect way to bring the film to life for the holidays.

While winter whites always get the bulk of the attention, blue is the other color of the season we're suggesting you embrace. Opt for chinoiserie-style ornaments and a decadent velvet ribbon garland to make it feel just as cozy as red and green.

Perfect for the diva of the family, this Anthropologie-designed Christmas tree is all about glamorous, textured accents like peacock feathers, Depression-style glass, and velvet accents. Christmas is allowed to be over-the-top!

The summer of Barbie may be over, but that joyous love of all things pink can (and should) continue all year long. If you're feeling as bold as Margot Robbie's iconic character, go all in on a bright pink tree with gold and white accents. Like Barbie, your Christmas tree can be anything you want it to.

If you're always looking at moon phases and tarot cards, bring that love of all things astrological to your Christmas decorations this year. Golden crescent moons, flying doves, and iron-star-studded wreaths are incorporated in this flocked tree. Add warm-hued string lights, and the decorations take on an ethereal glow.

Classics are classics for a reason—and nothing is more instantly festive than a hallmark red-and-white Christmas tree. This bountiful beauty was decked by Inspired by Charm, who included lodge-inspired elements like buffalo check wrapping paper and wooden skis to make it feel fresh and fanciful.

If a streamlined and sleek aesthetic is more your style, use this Christmas tree by For the Home as inspiration. The intentionally sparse look nods to traditional Scandinavian spruces, which feature less dense branches and are left mostly bare save for a few basic ball ornaments and twinkling white lights.

Why choose just two colors when you can have all of them? This Technicolor tree created by Serretta Style leans into a more-is-more aesthetic with tiers of ornaments in every color of the rainbow. While decorations in the same shapes and designs are used here (just in different shades), you could get the same effect by hanging your existing ornaments by color from top to bottom.

The weather outside may be frightful, but it certainly inspires a delightful Christmas tree design! Nod to all things snow and ice with a winter-wonderland-themed evergreen. This scene designed by Lolly Jane combines a flocked tree with an assortment of white and silver ornaments—like plush "snowballs" and frosty pinecones—for a snowy and chic scene. Plenty of warm white lights and a pair of ice skates complete the charming look.

Follow House Beautiful on Instagram and TikTok.

On top of being the magazine's go-to Waco expert, Meghan also touches on quite a few other verticals in the digital space, like hot news content featuring your favorite HGTV stars, home design inspiration, and the latest interior TikTok trends. However, her favorite pieces are the ones that start conversations, such as stories about resale value and whether or not the Solo cup is chic. When she's not interviewing interior designers about home trends, you can find Meghan reading a fictional thriller with her cat in her lap, planning themed parties, or strolling through flea markets searching for chunky candle holders and vintage bookends.

#6810. Stop Hunting for Pricey Antiques—Designers Say These 9 Humble Finds Give a Home So Much Character

They’re affordable, widely available, and stylish.

It’s any avid thrifter’s dream to stumble upon an extremely valuable piece of decor while browsing the shelves of their favorite antique store. But while you’re hunting for rare treasures, don’t discount the less-expensive finds—they can be the secret to a home that looks instantly filled with character.

“To me, a collected home is much more interesting when everything isn’t perfect or chosen for its monetary value,” designer Lauren Sullivan of Well by Design tells House Beautiful. “Often, it’s the less precious pieces that give a room its personality.”

Even as older generations are continuing to downsize and offload unwanted things to local thrift stores, it still might be hard to find the diamonds in the rough. Keep reading to find out what Sullivan, and a handful of other designers, say you should be on the lookout for on your next thrifting visit.

The most valuable antiques are the ones that have been passed down through generations. In Sullivan’s own bathroom, the designer keeps her great-grandmother’s vanity set on the countertop, its aged finish providing beautiful contrast with the more contemporary marble and finishes. In her bedroom, the same great-grandmother’s small bedside clock found a place on Sullivan’s nightstand.

“Neither is particularly valuable monetarily, but I love incorporating pieces that have been passed down through generations into a home filled with both old and new,” Sullivan says.

Designers love incorporating artwork into homes to add personality and dimension, but Sullivan, as well as designer Becca Meyer of BB Meyer Design, believe that antique shops are where you can really find some hidden gems.

“Some of my favorite pieces of art have come from flea markets or antique shops for very little money,” Meyer says. “I much prefer these to mass produced prints.” You’re more likely to find pieces with aged textures at these types of shops, and that patinaed quality creates an entirely unique look.

Sullivan explains that she’s actually particularly drawn to prints, as well as smaller paintings and silhouettes. She even has an inexpensive print of The Skating Minister in a vintage frame in one of her guest rooms, pictured above. “These smaller, or sometimes quirky, pieces can bring so much personality to a space without requiring a significant investment,” she adds. They may not be originals, but they’re definitely unique.

Old pottery and ceramics are widely available, and they are usually affordable as well. Designer Brooke Snyder of Design Hutch loves how they make any place feel more curated.

The imperfections these handmade pieces carry, from uneven shapes, variations in glaze, or evidence of years of use, are what help these items bring a warmth to a room, particularly when placed near more refined materials, according to Sullivan. They feel humble, maybe even utilitarian, especially when displayed in a kitchen. Or, better yet, used in a kitchen (as long as they’re safe).

“They're beautiful on their own but also so functional for holding utensils or branches or flowers,” Meyer adds. “They make any space feel a little more lived-in and loved and help tell a story—and they don't break the bank!”

“I love incorporating vintage plates, old copper pots, and woven baskets into a home,” explains designer Joe Waroquier. Though these are run-of-the-mill, everyday objects, they can take on and provide an entirely different life when displayed in the home as decor. Mismatched plates on a wall, a collection of copper pots hanging on a rod or resting on shelves in the kitchen, an old crock filled with branches—these items invite in warmth, texture, and more personality.

“I’m particularly drawn to pieces that show their age, whether it’s a little patina, a worn finish, or an imperfect glaze,” he adds. “I love mixing these finds with more contemporary furnishings because that contrast makes a room feel collected and personal rather than overly designed.”

Mirrors can be intriguing focal points of a room, or they can add dimension to a gallery wall. However you want to style them, Snyder loves finding these reflective pieces at antique malls and vintage shops.

“Purchasing something that you don’t see every day from a retailer allows this unique piece to stand out on its own, without feeling like something you see everywhere or feels too simple,” she explains.

As Snyder puts it, antique table lamps provide high visual impact with low investment. Adding one to your accent table or on a bookshelf is an easy way to make your space feel more curated while inviting in another level of ambience with the lighting.

These vintage lamps in the above space, for instance, were bought from a collector and make the primary bedroom feel much more charming and curated. And unlike antique sconces, there’s no need to hardwire these into the wall.

Besides pottery and lamps, designer Ryann Swan Hackett of Ryann Swan Design loves to find and collect other small tabletop decor for her projects. Think candleholders, vintage coffee table books, and trays—things that can layer a little history and individuality into a room or simple vignette.

“If you’re lucky,” Swan Hackett says of the vintage coffee table books, “you’ll find an old inscription inside, which gives the book a story of its own.”

In terms of midsize accent pieces, Meyer says that she has a soft spot for finding antique stools “because you can pop them into any space to add depth and dimension.” Tuck them beside an armchair in the living room, add them to an empty kitchen corner and place a plant on top, or stick one in a bathroom by the tub for somewhere to keep a drink while relaxing. And if you find an upholstered one that could use new fabric, switching it out for a pattern you love is probably worth it.

“These bring a natural warmth that can be difficult to replicate with something new, and their small size makes them fairly reasonable in price for a vintage furnishing piece,” Meyer says.

A larger piece of furniture is likely always going to be more expensive than a smaller one, but designer Yena Jung of By Yena Designs thinks vintage hutches are the versatile antiques people should pay more attention to. They come in many different shapes, styles, and sizes, and can easily make a home in any room of the house.

“A beautiful vintage hutch can add warmth, charm, and a sense of history to a space,” Jung says. “I especially love how their imperfections and natural patina make them feel collected over time.”

Follow House Beautiful on Instagram and TikTok.

#6790. GQ’s 13 Breakout Stars of 2026

Sometimes it’s fun to be early to the party. GQ launched the Breakouts portfolio in 2014 to celebrate the Hollywood upstarts who were poised to do big things. And not to brag, but we nailed it; that first group included Miles Teller, Shailene Woodley, Eddie Redmayne, and the late, great Chadwick Boseman. Over the ensuing years, we featured plenty more soon-to-be-superstars: Rami Malek. Brie Larson. Daniel Kaluuya. Riz Ahmed. Barry Keoghan. The kids from Stranger Things, when they were still kids. The cast of Succession before Succession became everyone’s favorite show.

In 2017, we put a baby-faced Timothée Chalamet on the list for his turn in Call Me by Your Name. (Our global entertainment director, Dana Mathews, fondly recalls him turning to her during the Man of the Year Party and saying, “This is the best party I’ve ever been to in my life.”) Since then, Chalamet has appeared on three GQ covers, earned four Oscar nominations, and become a household name (and maybe been to a few more great parties).

We took a few years off (okay, fine, seven), but 2026 feels like exactly the right time to revive GQ’s Breakouts portfolio—now through a wider lens. Yes, though we’ve got eyes on Hollywood’s next big things—like, say, Sly Stallone doppelgänger Anthony Ippolito; Gen Z class clown Owen Thiele, of Adults; Misty Green’s astounding leading woman, Rosalind Eleazar—not everyone is working the camera. We’re excited to introduce you to the West Coast’s hottest up-and-coming rapper in AZ Chike and put you on to Fcukers, whose debut album, packed with rowdy electro-pop bangers, made them first in our hearts and on our pregaming playlists. We’ve also got podcaster and journalist Pablo Torre, whose relentless reporting upended a $7.8 billion NBA franchise, and Jordan Firstman, an internet-born comedian who wrote and directed a film that commanded a lengthy standing ovation from Cannes audiences (right after they finished crying).

Point is, our Breakouts class of 2026 brings together 13 fully minted stars who did something that feels increasingly impossible: They captured our attention in a time when it feels like attention is more fleeting than ever. Welcome to their party.

Dress by Dior. Shoes by Jimmy Choo. Mask from Palace Costume Co.

Age: 25Breakout moment: Playing the queen bee on Ryan Murphy’s ’80s glam fever dream The Shards.—————

It’s a different kind of attention that I actually prefer. Because for so long I was getting attention, but not for a specific thing. And that was confusing and kind of messed with my head a bit. I was really young and people knew who I was, but I couldn’t really point to the work that they knew. And so it’s really fun for me to have people [now say], “I watched The Shards,” or “I watched Bottoms,” and then we have something to talk about.

Infinite Jest—no, I’m kidding, they’ve already read it, all of them. A Man’s Place by Annie Ernaux is very good and easily digestible. I think Just Kids is wonderful. What We Talk About When We Talk About Love. That one’s really great. Oh, and this is so classic, but Nine Stories by J.D. Salinger is really good. I know guys—male actors especially—love The Catcher in the Rye.

I got really worried the other day because I started getting a lot of followers. It was two days of so many followers, and I was like, “Oh my God, I’ve been doxxed.” The last thing I logged was Hitch. My top four are A Woman Under the Influence, The Dark Knight, Phantom Thread, and Eternal Sunshine. My Odyssey review has 112 likes. I don’t care how many followers I have on Instagram, how many likes I get. Letterboxd is truly a hit of adrenaline every time someone likes my review. —Frazier Tharpe

Jacket and jeans by Ralph Lauren Collection. Necklace by Chopard. Ring by Chanel Fine Jewelry.

On Wise: Jacket by Isabel Marant. Top by Gucci. Briefs by Araks. Boots by Herbert Levine. Sunglasses by McQueen. On Walker Lewis: Jacket by Balenciaga. T-shirt by Isabel Marant. Jeans by Gucci. Boots by Timberland. Sunglasses by Tom Ford. Jewelry, his own.

Ages: Wise, 28 | Walker Lewis, 29Breakout Moment: The NYC duo’s 2026 debut album, Ö, featuring indie-sleazy hits like “If You Wanna Party, Come Over to My House” and “Beatback.”—————

Wise: That was probably the most surreal thing I’ve ever experienced. We’re like, “This is what it feels to be like Led Zeppelin.”

Walker Lewis: It’s funny—a reel permeated my Instagram from a Brazilian influencer, and it was like, “Who is this controversial band that everyone’s arguing about opening for Harry Styles?” But I think by the last show, people got it.

Walker Lewis: Easy. The Cure. It’s unbelievable, to see somebody still sound that good and be able to play for two hours, and you know every single song.

Walker Lewis: When I was getting a root canal done and the nurse said, “Can I get a photo with you after?” I was like, “You couldn’t have asked before?” —Alex Pappademas

(from l.) On Firstman: All clothing by Tom Ford. Earrings, his own. On Walker Lewis: Jacket by Louis Vuitton Men’s. Shirt and sunglasses by Tom Ford. Tie by Our Legacy. Necklace, his own. On Wise: Top and pants by McQueen.

Robe by Dolce & Gabbana. Pants by Lu’u Dan. Watch by Cartier. Earring, his own.

Age: 28Breakout Moment: The series Off Campus, as the hottest fictional hockey player from this year’s hottest fictional hockey show.—————

I cannot shut up about this, but it has to be throwing the first pitch out for the White Sox. I will remember that forever.

Yeah, it was. And I hadn’t thrown a baseball in a really long time.

I look forward to the moment where I get to really reflect and digest. Next month I have some time off, and I’m going to go home and eat sandwiches at my mom’s house and take my grandma for a walk. “Go on a walk with my grandma”.… That’s a weird way to put it.

I’m, like, an idiot. I’m a full goofball. Garrett Graham is the heartthrob. To me, I’m just like, “I’m Bel.” And in my head, those are very different things. I’m flattered that people can associate me with heartthrob status, but no, I don’t wake up in the morning feeling like a heartthrob.

I mean, I always wanted to play Holden Caulfield, but who knows? —Trishna Rikhy

Jacket and pants by Prada. Shirt by Brioni. Pocket square by Charvet. Belt by Artemas Quibble. Sunglasses by Jacques Marie Mage. Necklace and ring by David Yurman.

Jacket by McQueen. Shirt by Factor’s. Pants by Versace. Boots by Tom Ford. Sunglasses (throughout) by Louis Vuitton Men’s. Earrings (throughout), his own.

Age: 30Breakout Moment: Being the infectiously charismatic friend-to-all on FX’s zoomer sitcom Adults.—————

Sadie Sink knew who I was, and that was kind of cool. I just love her. She’s so talented and so adorable. It’s like meeting the queen before she’s the queen.

I do watch the fan edits. They’re fun. Some of them pick the worst photos of me, and I’m like, “This is a hate crime, but thank you for putting it on TikTok.”

I got two very smart pieces of advice. One was, “Run. Don’t do this. Save yourself.” And then the other person said, “Stick with it.” I’m kind of doing both. I am simultaneously sticking with it and trying my hardest, but also I’m like, Once you have a win, you do have to celebrate it and run as far away as you can and hide.

I bought a bag from The Row…which looks like—and I say this with love—it could be $20, but it’s the most expensive thing I own. And I did this because I went shopping with Hailey Bieber, and when you’re shopping with Hailey Bieber you buy things you think you can afford but you probably can’t…and now it’s too late to return. It’s a beautiful bag—it’s quiet luxury, babe. It’s so quiet you don’t know it’s The Row. —F.T

Jacket and pants by Saint Laurent by Anthony Vaccarello. Boots by Giuseppe Zanotti. Sunglasses (throughout) by Louis Vuitton Men’s. Earrings (throughout), his own. Ring by Bvlgari.

Dress by Bottega Veneta. Shoes by Herbert Levine. Sunglasses by Celine.

Age: 38Breakout moment: Her riveting performance as the title character in Chris Rock’s Misty Green (and her role in Slow Horses).—————

I mean, the first time I watched it, I just thought, Oh—you’re going to be in every frame. [Laughs]

[In an American accent] “Murderer.” Mur-der-rer. I think every Brit finds that hard. I was like, “Can I say ’killer’ instead?” And “mirror.” It’s when you’ve got a double r. But I had a great dialect coach.

I would’ve loved to have been in a scene with Philip Seymour Hoffman. That would’ve been my dream. And I would love to work with Sandra Hüller.

I was onstage with Ralph Fiennes. We finished Misty Green—I did an overnight shoot on Friday into Saturday morning, got on a plane, arrived in London on Sunday, and went straight into rehearsals with Ralph. It was a pretty mad summer.

I was getting ready before the Misty Green premiere [at the Toronto International Film Festival]. And my mom came down. She sat there and she was just sending pictures and she just had tears in her eyes. Just seeing this whole period at TIFF—for her, it’s her little girl. And every now and again, I would just look at her and she was just happy and in awe in a way. It warmed my heart. —A.P.

Dress by Gucci. Hosiery by Wolford. Earrings by Cartier.

Shirt and pants by Brioni. Bow tie and cummerbund, stylist’s own. Vintage watch by Rolex from Foundwell. Vintage cufflinks by Cartier from Foundwell. Earring, his own.

Age: 35Breakout moment: The Cannes hit Club Kid, which he wrote, directed, and starred in as a party promoter navigating unexpected parenthood.—————

I still do, yeah, at the phone call scene at the end. But it feels very distant from me. I feel like I’m watching it more as an observer now, which is nice, and I feel different than when I made it. I was such an open wound.

Cannes was such an emotional experience. I’m 35, and I’ve always known that I had something to say and I knew how to say it, but I’ve never been given the opportunity. So, honestly, to feel like I was being seen and understood was like 20 years of validation that I didn’t get. And so something shifted, that week, in me. I’ve noticed myself not having to use humor as a shield anymore, as much. I’m less afraid to be vulnerable—and not just in my public life but in my personal life too.

Of course, you shouldn’t need the validation of anyone to feel like you’re worthy, but let’s be real.…

What gagged me this year? I would say, honestly, as a statement of ambition, the Javis’ movie [La Bola Negra]. You marvel at it because you’re like, “How the fuck did they pull this off?” To even want to tell that complex of a story, and to just do it and go balls to the wall, is very inspiring.

I was like, “This is too long. It’s embarrassing now.” —A.P.

Jacket and pants by Canali. Shirt by Amiri. Boots by Alessandro Vasini. Tie by Husbands.

Age: 41Breakout moment: Reporting on how the Steve Ballmer–owned Clippers were allegedly funny with money, on his podcast Pablo Torre Finds Out, leading to one of the most severe team penalties in sports history.—————

Two days ago, I walked into this event that The New York Times was holding…it was like the 175th birthday party [for the paper]. I walk out the elevator and literally the first person I see is [NBA commissioner] Adam Silver. I won’t say exactly what he told me, but the tenor of our conversation was like, This is the HBO series adaptation of what this moment would be.

As a first-generation American—my parents are from the Philippines—there’s always been a dream of mine to be cast as a white person. So…Andrew Garfield, come on. Let’s do this.

The funny part is that I’m not even done reporting this story yet. The federal government is now investigating the Clippers, and so I’m now investigating the government’s investigation in the era of Trump too, which is like its own rabbit hole inside of a rabbit hole, which is fucking wild. But at the same time, it feels like now our number is listed in the phone book…actual other NBA owners are calling me up saying, “Can you look into this?”

It’s hard not to be sappy about this, but the lesson I learned is that if you’re telling the truth, you can walk into any room and feel like that as a suit of armor. Even when the person you’re investigating is one of the 10 richest people in the world. —F.T.

Coat by Michael Kors. Vintage pajamas from Palace Costume Co. Socks by London Sock Company. Glasses by Jacques Marie Mage. Necklaces (throughout), his own.

Age: 37Breakout moments: Stealing scenes as a ’70s Provincetown line cook in the Anthony Bourdain biopic Tony, and his stand-up special Uncle Stav.—————

I just did The Drew Barrymore Show. That experience was so magical. I was so nervous to be around Drew Barrymore. I’ve done a lot of cool shit, and that’s the one where I was like, “I am actually nervous to the point where I’m not a professional. I’m just a stammering little boy who’s with the girl he had a crush on from The Wedding Singer when he was a fat 10-year-old, and I’m trying to keep it together.”

Last week I allowed myself an aimlessly wander around New York City day. Got a little stoned, walked around, stumbled into a movie. Walked along the water on the West Side. Sat down to a little random omakase. Rode a Citi Bike around. I call it the hero’s journey, where you leave your home at, whatever, 11 a.m. ,with just the bare provisions, and you’re not allowed to go home. You just have to keep moving and keep doing stuff.

I filmed this Judd Apatow movie The Comeback King, with Glen Powell, Cristin Milioti, Madelyn Cline. That comes out in February. I’m excited to do stand-up—for fun, not to explicitly work on another hour. I’m excited to go back to clubs and just like, “Can I be the funniest person in this club tonight?” And that’s my only goal.

To be honest with you, the last three years, it’s [been] all go. But you got to have these big years, or else GQ doesn’t talk to you at the end of the year. —A.P.

On Halkias: Jacket and pants by Brooks Brothers. Shirt by Many. Shoes by Louis Vuitton Men’s. Sunglasses by Valention. On Torre: Jacket by Tom Ford. Shirt by Factor’s. Pants by Louis Vuitton Men’s. Shoes by G.H. Bass. Sunglasses by Jacques Marie Mage. Ring, his own.

Shirts and pants by Dunhill. Underwear by Calvin Klein. Earrings (throughout), his own.

Age: 31Breakout moment: Storming the West Coast rap scene with collabs from Pharrell Williams and Tyler, the Creator on his major-label debut album, No Rest for the Wicked.—————

Not like I made it, but I have arrived. We went to Magic City on a Monday for the first time. They were playing the fuck out my records in that motherfucker. The DJ played “Look Like My Mama” in the strip club. You ain’t never heard Tyler, the Creator get played in a strip club. We crossing worlds that’s never been crossed before.

The day before the album came out, we were in Area 29 in Houston, doing our thing. Regular night, it ain’t too packed, throwing money. They tap me on my shoulder like, “My boy want to holler at you.” They take me upstairs to this private section, pull the curtain back…it’s Travis Scott.

It was epic, bro. N-gga came out from behind the curtain like Batman. The light hid half his face. He said, “I fuck with everything you got going on.” We go back down to that section, 15 minutes pass, then one of the owners came and grabbed me: “Look, look, look.” Travis was up there, playing [Chike’s song] “Packed Up.” —F.T.

Shirt by Lu’u Dan. Pants by Valentino. Bracelet by Cartier.

Age: 26Breakout moment: Being a dead ringer for Sly Stallone in I Play Rocky, the film about…Sly Stallone playing Rocky.—————

In the thick of prep. I was training at Gleason’s Gym with Stephan James, who plays Carl Weathers. We were beating the crap out of each other and figuring out how to box. I had gained a bunch of weight, and I was trying to actually cut a few pounds, so I was in those sweatsuits, like a garbage-bag type thing. On the way to the gym, I would listen to Stallone and on the way home.… Everything I could find up to 1980, 1985, every piece of Stallone footage that existed.

I [asked] Seth Rogen, “Dude, how do you handle this stuff?” And he said, “Just go with the flow.” It was such a simple answer, but from somebody like him, I was like, He’s got a good grip on this. The simplicity of it landed.

Philip Seymour Hoffman is one. Chadwick Boseman. Christian Bale, Heath Ledger.

[The first Rocky] is the best movie—but when I was younger, the one that caught my eye was four. Rocky IV is nuts. —F.T.

Jacket by Versace. Shirt by 73London. Pants by Umit Benan. Earring (throughout), his own.

(from l.) On Ambrossi: All clothing by Gucci. Shoes by Christian Louboutin. Pocket square by Brioni. Sunglasses by Ahlem. On Calvo: Jacket, pants, and boots by Tom Ford. Scarf, stylist’s own. Sunglasses by Jacques Marie Mage.

(from l.) On Ambrossi: Jacket and pants by Chanel. Shirt by Factor’s. Shoes by Marsèll. Pin by Cartier. On Calvo: All clothing, tie, and shoes by Ralph Lauren Purple Label. Watch by Cartier.

Ages: Ambrossi, 42 | Calvo, 35Breakout moment: Cowriting and directing La Bola Negra, the gay Spanish historical epic loosely based on an unfinished Federico García Lorca play.——————————————

Javier Ambrossi: Very important. We understood that sometimes people expect LGBTQ+ stories to be niche or for a specific audience. We wanted to do a queer story about universal love.

Ambrossi: It’s a way to put in context how different we are. We change a lot, and the story of our grandparents and our story is very different.

Javier Calvo: What do we do with the freedom our ancestors fought for, and died for?

Calvo: Isabelle Huppert. For me, she’s everything.

Ambrossi: I will go with Julia Roberts. My Best Friend’s Wedding is a very important queer story. The relationship between a woman and a gay man—it was the first time I saw that onscreen. And Rupert Everett was so bold, and I could tell in his eyes that he was gay. I was a kid and I was like, “He’s not faking it.”

How did you celebrate when you won best director at Cannes?

Ambrossi: I put the award on the table and went to sleep. And then Javi came over at 8 a.m.…

Ambrossi: “Give me the award!” And he took it! —A.P.

(from l.) On Ippolito: Sunglasses by Jacques Marie Mage. Necklace by Cartier. On Chike: Shirt and pants by Brioni. Sunglasses by Tom Ford. On Thiele: Jacket by McQueen. Shirt by Factor’s. Pants by Versace.

#6787. Do You Recognize These Past GQ Breakouts?

After a seven-year hiatus, we’re thrilled to announce the return of GQ’s Breakouts portfolio. First launched in 2014, the series has highlighted (often young) stars just as they hit. Our group in 2026 includes Belmont Cameli of Off Campus, Owen Thiele of Adults, and Rosalind Eleazar of Misty Green. We also expanded our scope beyond actors: There’s the electronic musician duo Fcukers, the Spanish director duo Los Javis, Pablo Torre—a longtime podcaster and journalist who won the Pulitzer Prize—and many more. We suspect you’ll be seeing them around for years.

The people we’ve featured in past breakouts have often gone on to become beloved household names. For instance, when this portfolio first began, we included Shailene Woodley, Jack O’Connell, and the late Chadwick Boseman. In 2017, we featured Timothée Chalamet, now a bona fide movie star. Jack Lowden, also included that year, is a frontrunner to play James Bond. And so on.

As we bring back this portfolio, it felt only fitting to take a trip down memory lane. Everyone, after all, has to start somewhere. Here are some stars who were once GQ Breakouts:

A mansion in LA. The best party clothes of the season. An electric musical duo. A model turned actor. A comedian turned director. At least two podcasters. Come on in and meet the 13 people who just had the biggest, most-spotlight-stealing year of their lives.

#6786. Honestly, I Wouldn’t Risk Waiting for Black Friday—Grab the Oura Ring 4 for $150 Off While Your Size Is Still Here

We may earn commission from links on this page, but we only recommend products we love. Promise.

It’s never a bad time to invest in your health, but right now is a particularly good one. While Black Friday is still a couple of weeks away, the Oura Ring 4 (the predecessor to the Oura Ring 5, which came out in June) is getting the early sale treatment. And trust me when I say this isn’t one of those wellness gadgets that’s all hype and no substance. Just ask, ahem, the entire Cosmo shopping team. This little wearable tech tracker—which is over $100 off on Amazon—does it all.

Now, I know what you’re thinking: Why not wait until November 27 and snag one with the rest of your Black Friday haul? Well, while we don’t know exactly what Oura has up its sleeve for the Super Bowl of shopping, there’s no guarantee today’s deal will stick around, that it’ll get any better, or that your size will even be in stock by the main event. Waiting is just a gamble, and hey, a good deal is a good deal! If you’ve been waiting to buy one and don’t need the newest model, I’d take the $249 deal and run! Brand-new models don’t usually see big discounts their first holiday season, so I wouldn’t count on the Oura Ring 5 getting much cheaper this year.

For what it’s worth, the Oura Ring 5 is about 40% smaller and gets up to nine days of battery life, compared to the Ring 4’s eight. But many of its new app features are rolling out to older models too, so unless a slimmer ring is a priority, the Ring 4 still does the job. The Oura Ring 4 is sleek, lightweight, and pairs with the rest of your jewelry so effortlessly—whether you’re a gold or silver girly, it’ll stack gorgeously. There are six different colorways, which vary slightly in price: Silver, Black, and Stealth are $249 (previously $349), and Gold and Rose Gold are $279 (originally $399). The ceramic design in midnight is even marked down to $279 from $399 today.

But it’s so much more than just a style choice. The device keeps tabs on your steps, activity, sleep, stress levels, heart rate, temperature, and cycle, and it keeps all that trackable data in a convenient mobile app.

One shopper called it a “game-changer” as someone living with chronic illnesses and said they’re able to “better understand [their] body, track [their] patterns, and recognize changes.” Another added: “As a woman, the cycle tracking alone has been incredibly valuable. Being able to see how different phases of my cycle affect my sleep, recovery, stress levels, energy, and overall readiness has helped me make much better decisions about work, exercise, and self-care.” That’s a pretty compelling argument alone!

Megan Schaltegger is an NYC-based writer. She loves strong coffee, eating her way through the Manhattan food scene, and her dog, Murray. She promises not to talk about herself in third person IRL.

#6785. All About Este Haim’s Husband, Jonathan Levin

Este Haim is one of three sisters forming the superstar pop group Haim. The trio has historically been very discreet about their personal lives. Most of their stories are shared through their songs, which are often about both love and heartbreak. In 2023, Este did share some more intimate thoughts with The Cut about self-love.

“I don’t think I truly understood self-love until the last three or four years,” she said. “There’s an air of confidence that comes from just loving yourself, the good and the bad.”

But in mid-February 2025, the oldest of the musical sisters revealed on Instagram that she was engaged to tech CEO Jonathan Levin. The post showed Este smiling in celebration, flashing a square-cut diamond engagement ring and wearing a T-shirt that read, “I’M TAKEN.”

On Jan. 1, 2026, a source told People that the couple had gotten married on New Year’s Eve.

Here’s everything to know about Levin and his relationship with Este so far.

Levin is originally from the United Kingdom, where he studied at the University of Bristol for economics before attending the University of Oxford, where he received a master’s in philosophy and economics, per his LinkedIn profile. Levin then moved to New York City, where he cofounded Chainalysis, a cryptocurrency company.

Talking to The Big Whale in February 2025, Levin said he “discovered crypto in 2012 at a time when only computer scientists were researching Bitcoin, which was then the leading cryptocurrency.”

Levin said he wrote a number of academic studies on the topic and “why people use cryptocurrencies,” believing this understanding would support industry growth. Levin has stated he wants to “bring greater trust and transparency to cryptocurrency markets.” He was made CEO of the company in December 2024.

Chainalysis is known for focusing on tracing data and attempting to institute safety measures around blockchains and cryptocurrency. Levin has even testified before the U.S. Congress around national security and blockchain technology.

The couple has not shared anything about how they got together or how long they’ve been dating. However, fans have noticed Levin on Este’s arm at various events dating back to 2023, when he was her date at Taylor Swift’s birthday that December.

In September 2024, he joined Swift and Este in a suite for a Kansas City Chiefs game and was seen with the friend group again that month at the US Open.

Since announcing her engagement, Este has been posting on Instagram with allusions to Levin. Her first came on Valentine’s Day, where she shared a screenshot of herself FaceTiming him in her Instagram Stories. She called him her “love,” writing, “happy valentine’s day, i love you j.”

She then posted about Haim’s upcoming album on March 12, writing in the caption, “i don’t think my fiancé is mentally prepared for relationships to be out.”

Este told Vogue on Jan. 1, 2026, that Levin popped the question on Dec. 22, 2024, during a hike. “I had Mario Badescu acne medication on, my hair was in a top knot, I had no makeup on, I was in sweats and sneakers,” she said.

He had a placeholder ring, she recalled: “A beautiful ring still, but the second he was like, ‘Will you marry me?’ and I said yes, he also was like, ‘We need to design this ring, because this is not the final thing.’ I thought [that] was really, really sweet.”

A source told People that they tied the knot on Dec. 31, 2025, with Vogue revealing that they had their “intimate ceremony in California.”

Este spoke to Vogue about her Louis Vuitton wedding dress, saying, “I’ve been looking forward to this day since I was five. When I was five, I had this vision of me, coming down a spiral staircase, there’s a scene in Cinderella that probably is what I was mimicking, but I would descend in this giant ballroom gown, and then my fiancé, [my] husband, would be at the bottom and grab my hand.”

On July 13, Kloss shared an Instagram Story from Jay-Z’s concert at Yankee Stadium featuring herself and husband Joshua Kushner seated with Este and Levin.

Kloss and Este had been guests at Taylor Swift’s wedding 10 days before.

At the New York City premiere of One Night Only, Haim shared some of the relationship advice she applies to her own marriage to Levin.

“Keep pursuing, even if you’re in a relationship,” she told People. “You should always be dating. You should always be working toward having a better relationship. Relationships are work, and I think people forget about that.”

In a November 10 interview with People, Haim elaborated on her previous advice about continuing to date your spouse, sharing some of the ways she and Levin keep their relationship exciting.

“I think people misunderstood that,” she said. “What I meant was, you need to continue leaving little notes for each other. Flowers are nice. Don’t get complacent—and separate bathrooms, I think, are also pretty essential.”

Haim also revealed that she and Levin recently returned to Pioneer Works in New York City, where they had their first date.

“We went back to where we went on our first date. We went to [NYC nonprofit cultural arts center] Pioneer Works...It was a day of music and art, and there was good beer and wine, and it was just really nice to go back to our roots,” she said.

#6784. All the Gifts GQ Editors Want This Holiday Season 2026

As the desk at GQ that handles all things shopping, the GQ Recommends team has developed a rather motley assortment of wants, needs, and highly specific desires. We have good taste, if I may say as much, and no shortage of opinions about what makes a thing worth owning. So this holiday season, I asked everyone to set aside the usual assignments and show me what's on their own wishlists. What follows is an eclectic grab bag of A1 gifts, straight from the folks who spend all year finding the good stuff.

Still got names to cross off? Peruse our gift guides and sign up for the GQ Recommends newsletter for more good stuff worth giving (or keeping).

#6781. Hanifa Is Relaunching After an 8-Month Hiatus

Hanifa is back. A little over eight months after founder Anifa Mvuemba paused operations for her label, which had been in business for 15 years, it’s relaunching with a new collection and updated business model.

The collection features the colorful party clothes the brand is known for, like a red evening gown with a bright blue feathered brooch and a blue liquid organza dress (a 2022 revival bound to become the season’s made-to-order showstopper). There are also more practical pieces, like Hanifa’s take on the track pant (barrel-legged with a silk stripe) and bomber jacket. The Harper dress, a green and black-dotted hit from last year, is now available in a deep blue.

Mvuemba hopes the new Hanifa feels true to the brand’s original ethos, just a little more mature: the designer was 21 when she started the brand in 2011, and now that she’s about to turn 36, her style and taste has evolved, she says, alongside her customers’. Mvuemba spent months reworking the brand’s fabrications, cuts and patterns, especially for knitwear, its biggest category. She went through dozens of iterations to find the right cashmere blend, for example, that will hold its shape and have cashmere’s soft handfeel, while keeping the brand in the price range its customers have come to expect.

The brand worked to improve its fabric quality without compromising on price.

The relaunch will be a test in regaining consumer trust. Things began to unwind last December, when purchases made during her brand’s annual Hanifa Friday sale in November failed to ship, thanks to production delays and supply chain issues. For some customers, they said months went by without any communication — or delivery — from the brand. Disgruntled customers took to social media to complain, not just about delayed orders (all of which Hanifa said it was eventually able to fulfill, or issued refunds for), but about the brand’s general direction, which they believed went downhill. In a statement posted online in March, Mvuemba apologized, and announced the brand would go on hiatus.

“Mentally, I wasn’t prepared to continue,” Mvuemba says. “I wasn’t in the right headspace. And in order for me to be the best version of myself, the best leader, mother, wife, I could be to my family, my team, my business — I needed that mental clarity to just take a step back, rest, recalibrate, to get a clear view on what we needed to do for the business as a whole. I needed a break,” Mvuemba says. “I’m just not the type of founder or person that performs for the optics. It was extremely hard to do, but it was necessary, because in this space, it’s not something that we see often. But it’s something that we as founders need to be able to do.”

Hanifa’s new collection comes after the brand paused orders in March.

In the end, Mvuemba only took about two weeks off from work, she says. She found a therapist and spent time with her baby, who was born in December and whose maternity care was interrupted by crisis management in the fallout. Then, she turned her attention to Hanifa 2.0.

“I love this business so much. I love this brand so much. I had to get back,” she says. “We uprooted everything. We looked at our communications, our branding, our sizing, our website. Now, everything — our processes, our manufacturers — has shifted, and it’s nice to have had this time to sit through everything, go back, revise, go back, revise, and then say, ‘OK, we’re ready.’”

Now the brand is ready to relaunch, Hanifa is returning to market on different terms.

Mvuemba is eyeing outerwear as an expansion opportunity for her collection.

The biggest change to Hanifa’s operations is switching to a fully ready-to-ship model, versus a past pre-order model. With no pre-order period, where the brand could produce only a committed number of SKUs, the company has fronted the entire new collection (which Mvuemba says is “expensive but necessary”) and plans to use real-time performance data to determine production cadence and inventory investments. Having inventory overhead also shortens lead times for purchases to be fulfilled, and gives the brand space to ship items by sea rather than air, which cuts costs.

“We thought it was really important to come out the door swinging,” Mvuemba says. “People want their things when they want their things.” It’s also invested in new customer service and logistics partners, with brand plans to build data analysis into its production cycle to ensure that it’s responding quicker to what’s selling. Mvuemba hired a brand strategist, an art director and a PR firm to help steer the comeback story. The brand has always been bootstrapped, but she says she’s open to outside funding if the right partner comes along. She’s also open to the right retail partners, but that right now, the direct-to-consumer model is the best fit thanks to production and shipping timing.

Her focus above all, she says, is her customer. Mvuemba comes back time and again to the importance of data, and how much she wants to spend the rollout of this collection listening to feedback. She hopes that the brand’s core customer — the company has a legion of longtime loyalists who were sad to see it go — returns, and that the brand can grow its reach. By category, she’s excited about expanding the brand’s shoes and outerwear, both of which are steps in building a more complete ready-to-wear label, which is her main goal for next year. Scaling, Mvuemba says, will come at the right pace.

“I used to say I want it to be as big as LVMH. And with this year, I just had so much time to reflect and I’m OK with the pace that we’re going,” she says. “I just want to have a globally recognized brand that a lot of women can identify with and that we can continue to create these beautiful, amazing pieces. I’m so excited to show them this new era of the brand. We’re sharper, we’re clearer, we understand who our girl is, who our customer is, and I’m excited to give them more.”

“My Community Made It Happen”: Inside Mexican Brand Neithan Herbert’s Milan Debut

‘Rather Marvelous and Refreshing’: First Reactions to Christopher Kane’s Mulberry

Behind the Scenes: Henry Zankov Means Business

#6780. RIP to LIV, the Golf League No One Ever Really Cared About

The most remarkable thing about the era of LIV Golf, a five-year run characterized by near-parodic blunders and missteps, is that leadership and its public-facing talent don’t appear to have learned any lessons from their many failures.

From the beginning, the upstart league, established in 2021 as a rival to the dominant PGA Tour, was plagued by its own ineptitude. It’s only fitting—after LIV’s newfound lifeline, private investment firm BC Partners, reneged on a $300 million investment—that the brand’s figureheads are now celebrating the end by taking a victory lap. Tone-deaf doesn’t begin to describe it.

LIV CEO Scott O’Neil—who took over for original boss and Major winner Greg Norman in January of 2025 and was faced with finding new cash flow after Saudi Arabia's Public Investment Fund announced in April that it would no longer finance the operation—clearly exists in a different reality than our own. “Together, our team delivered world-class individual and team competitions across five continents, built a global business, secured pathways to The Open and U.S. Open… and built a league capable of attracting both elite and rising talent from around the world,” he said in a statement late last week. It’s unclear if the farewell tour will continue, though, an even newer report suggests that BC is looking to purchase a bankruptcy loan from LIV’s current owners. If this Hail Mary works, LIV’s future would look much different than the one O’Neil has promised.

Another way to look at it? One of LIV’s biggest accomplishments was that the league, despite its intentions to diversify the game and bring fun back into a sport that had come to personify privilege and exclusivity, inadvertently consolidated the PGA Tour’s power as a monopolistic behemoth now primed to solidify its status as the only league in town. They helped usher in a new era of leadership and investment for the Tour, including the introduction of CEO Brian Rolapp, who is by all accounts the most well-liked and accomplished boss it’s had in quite some time.

LIV also managed to tarnish the reputations of its biggest stars. Jon Rahm, who was vehemently opposed to the dog and pony show when it was announced, only to sheepishly take a bag of blood money so big his great grandchildren will never have to work a day in their lives, is now one of the game’s great “What If?” stories. Prior to the US Open at Brookline Country Club in June 2022, the defending champion was asked about his thoughts on the upstart league. “Money is great,” he said. But Rahm and his wife thought about it further. “Will our lifestyle change if I got $400 million? No, it will not change one bit.” Just over a year later, he defected to LIV.

Then there’s Phil Mickelson, who was poised to become a fixture of the American Ryder Cup team and widely considered a shoo-in to hold a cushy job as an analyst for golf’s biggest broadcasting events. Upon joining the league, he admitted in a conversation with Alan Shipnuck that Saudi Arabia was a terrible bedfellow. (Shipnuck and Mickelson have disagreed over whether the comments were on or off the record.) “They killed Khashoggi and have a horrible record on human rights. They execute people over there for being gay,” he explained. But there was a caveat: LIV was simply a “once-in-a-lifetime opportunity to reshape how the PGA Tour operates.” Miraculously, he got that part right.

Prize money on the PGA Tour is greater than ever, the Tour’s main events have recalibrated, and its season-ending championship series has been completely reimagined. Mickelson won’t be allowed within 100 miles of it.

The league’s biggest attraction, Bryson DeChambeau, is theoretically the worst-situated of the game’s many turncoats, though he’s probably more excited about growing his YouTube channel than he is about playing golf anyway. No one’s put their reputation on the line for this gaggle of buffoons quite like DeChambeau, who spoke proudly about how LIV was “growing the game” and “bringing team golf to the world.” This past weekend, he shared a statement on the dissolution of LIV. “I still believe in team golf,” he wrote. “It may look different in the future, but I still believe it’s essential to growing the game.” A loyal foot soldier, even from the grave.

It was always about the money. Nothing more, nothing less. This was a cash grab of epic proportions, and golfers not good enough to earn their keep on the PGA Tour took a life raft and briefly found themselves in possession of luxury superyachts. Then, low tide left them holding bags of cash and not much else. Was it worth it? For most of these golfers, probably. They were past their primes when LIV began; they’re still past the primes now, and just a helluva lot richer.

The league positioned itself as a disruptor, and disrupted so poorly that they’ve turned the PGA Tour into the strongest version of itself we’ve seen since Tiger Woods was at his peak. All that bloviating about the good of LIV Golf from its loudest talking heads is a bunch of nonsense.

It’s hard to tell if these statements are one last line of defense or more genuine delusion from LIV’s last advocates. They came in with the idea of sticking it to the PGA Tour, which at the time was facing bleak circumstances, bogged down in nonsense before redirecting its energy towards batting this gnat into extinction. Ultimately, LIV helped the PGA Tour save itself.

Now it’s gone for good, and five billion dollars later, we’re not gonna remember a damn thing about it. Not the players, not the leadership, and surely not all the ways they supposedly grew the game while merely growing their own bank accounts.

#6778. Amanda Seyfried Lights Up Tiffany & Co.’s Iconic Bird on a Rock to Usher in the Holiday Season

On Tuesday evening, all that glitters was indeed gold—and adorned in diamonds. For the third year in a row, Tiffany & Co.’s festive fête at its Fifth Avenue flagship served as the unofficial ribbon-cutting moment to kick off the holiday season, hosted this time around by Amanda Seyfried.

The actress, who is the House’s newest ambassador, was also tapped to illuminate the Bird on a Rock installation. A glitzed-up and glammed-out guest list made its way to Midtown for the occasion, passing the Fifth Avenue snowflake—now lit in Tiffany Blue for the holiday season—and stopping to marvel at the Landmark’s twinkling facade.

A brief elevator ride upstairs opened up to reveal a winter wonderland. Sparkling displays showcased the House’s pieces in front of the floor-to-ceiling windows, trays were laden with Tiffany Blue martinis, and the belle of the ball—the Bird on a Rock—gleamed outside. As guests counted down the moments until its lighting, they nibbled on petite T&C stamped-paninis and micro tuna crispy rice bites.

Seyfried arrived, dazzling in a Tiffany HardWear white gold and pavé diamond link necklace and the hallmark Bird on a Rock Wing brooch—with over 13 carats of diamonds. The award-winning star was just as excited for the party as everyone else. “As a young American girl, you have these fantasies that you might get to be a part of something really special. It’s pretty iconic that I’m here and that I get to represent Tiffany for the holiday season. There’s something very classic, traditional, and symbolic about Tiffany’s—it’s the best legacy jewelry brand there is,” Seyfried told Vogue. “My sister-in-law is here tonight with her baby, my godson. I wish my kids were here, but I didn’t even think to invite them, and they’re actually in the city. I don’t know what I was thinking!”

The holidays evoke all types of fuzzy feelings, and Mikey Madison reminisced about one of her favorite family traditions. “My dad and I always cook, so we’ll cook a big Hanukkah dinner and we’ll do all the classics that my grandma used to make. He does the brisket, I’ll do the latkes, the matzah ball soup, the kugel,” Madison shared. “It’s always meaningful to go to an event with Tiffany. It feels like a big family for me, and there’s so much intention behind their artistry, what they do, and how they bring people together,” the Oscar-winning ambassador added. “It’s the loveliest job that I have to get to work with them and get to wear all this incredibly beautiful jewelry as well.”

When it was finally time to light the Bird on a Rock, everyone gathered near the tree—one constructed from countless coveted Blue Boxes. “Love is a gift,” Matthieu Garnier, president and CEO of Tiffany & Co. North America, told the crowd during his toast. “The most meaningful gifts carry stories, create memories, and connect generations.”

Garnier wasn’t just referring to the tree: The entire 2026 holiday campaign—starring Seyfried—centers on gifts becoming future heirlooms, which in turn bridge a connection between generations. Just like in 1965, when Jean Schlumberger’s Bird on a Rock design became one of those treasured keepsakes intended to be passed down.

Right on cue, Seyfried stepped up, placing a Tiffany ornament on the tree, and immediately, the Bird on a Rock shimmered to life, illuminating the bustling streets below. Let the holidays begin!
