#6777. I Tried Taylor Swift’s Posture-Correcting Sports Bra—and I Have Some Thoughts

Is the $218 price tag worth it? Here’s the verdict.

We may earn commission from links on this page, but we only recommend products we love. Promise.

I have clocked a handful of years as a shopping editor, which means that my eye is trained to see things other people might miss. For example, when we were all oohing and ahhing over Taylor Swift’s TIME Person of the Year profile, I noticed that TayTay was wearing an interesting sports bra in one of her Eras Tour dance rehearsal photos. I did some digging, and it turns out that she was rocking the Forme Power Bra, a posture-correcting bra designed to create proper alignment in your shoulders and upper back. Fancy, fancy!

Considering how grueling Taylor’s Eras Tour rehearsals were (“Every day I would run on the treadmill, singing the entire set list out loud,” she told TIME), having supportive gear designed by an orthopedic surgeon makes all the sense in the world.

And while my days consist more of imagining I’m performing “Cruel Summer” while Hot Girl Walking on the treadmill than actually doing stadium tours, I immediately thought to myself that the Forme Power Bra might just be the posture solution I needed. Like, why yes, I’d love to sit less like a shrimp and more like a 5’11” pop star engaged to an NFL player. So I, along with a couple of fellow Cosmo editors, tested it out—because if I can't have an eight-carat diamond engagement ring (or a wedding at Madison Square Garden), I'll take this instead.

Keep reading to get the real scoop on whether Taylor’s posture-correcting sports bra is worth the $$$—and which version to snag—below.

If the phrase “posture-correcting device” conjures up images of a scary, hardware-filled contraption, think again. The sports bra is less brace-like and more supportive, using fabrics of various tensions throughout the design to keep your shoulders back and gently guide your body into correct alignment whether you’re standing up, sitting down, or exercising.

The Power Bra comes in a range of colors in sizes XS to 3XL. I’ll admit it’s not the most affordable thing out there at $188. But it’s definitely fancier than your average sports bra thanks to all the FDA-registered technology—you can even spend your pre-tax HSA money on it if you want!

When I first reviewed this bra a couple of years ago, my biggest wish was that it had some sort of inner shelf bra lining or padding. And in 2025, the brand fixed that!

Forme debuted an upgraded Power+ Bra last year, a premium style that features discreet, removable padding and a wider underarm opening to allow for more mobility. It now comes in seven colors and has a slightly higher price tag at $218.

For someone like me who prefers a little nip coverage, it's totally worth opting for this slightly-upgraded option. I know myself, and I'm just way more likely to wear this one to the gym or under clothes because it functions more like a sports bra or regular bra thanks to the padding.

To illustrate my commitment to testing all the things that Blondie owns, I will tell you all that I did, in fact, try on the sports bra in a very tiny office bathroom stall (pls see photo evidence below). And not going to lie, it was a little tricky to put on at first. But don't let that scare you! It was easier to put on the second time, and even easier the third time, and the fourth time, and so on. The brand recommends stretching the armhole a few times before wearing it, which I found to be helpful.

My initial reaction was that the Forme bra provided instant relief from my slouch-induced shoulder and neck pain. It really does help you be more mindful of your posture. I even tried to curve my shoulders forward at one point, and wildly enough, realized it was way harder than normal to slouch my shoulders into Gremlin Mode. It acted as a gentle reminder that the most comfortable way to sit is with my shoulders down and back.

Also, it’s worth noting that I have a mild case of scoliosis, and I was worried that the bra would feel like a brace because the natural resting state of my spine is a liiiiittle bit off center. But I didn’t run into any issues—it even supported my one shoulder that has a tendency to pop out!

As far as sizing, Cosmo’s Deputy Shopping Director Kim Duong and I tried on our regular sizes, and we both found the sports bra to be pretty dang tight. It’s very compressive and lacks cups—this led to flat-from-the-side, noticeable-from-the-front Regency-style cleave à la Bridgerton, so I’d suggest sizing up if you have a large chest. Kim and I agreed at the time that the Power Bra, which is made of a pretty thin, nippy athletic material, would be vastly improved with the addition of removable cups or inserts—and in August 2025, the brand heard our prayers with the Power+ Bra that features removable padding!

Beauty Editor Beth Gillette also tested it out: "I was hesitant—I'd read reviews from our team but hadn't seen anyone in my size (14, DD chest) rave about it. But I might actually love it more than Kim and Hannah! It's very comfortable, shockingly, considering I have horrendous posture, according to my chiropractor. It pulls back my shoulders a bit without pinching anything and doesn't feel unnatural, either," Gillette raved.

"And I love how good it looks," she continued. "I am not particularly ~gifted~ in the ~perky~ boob department. I often describe my boobs as living permanently in my armpits. But! This lifts and shapes them rather nicely. I felt v confident walking around in it and even felt compelled to take some selfies, which is how you know."

I enjoyed the Power Bra the most while wearing it on my busiest, most active days. I’m talkin’ workout classes, running to catch the bus, and hauling boxes out of your friend’s apartment—on those days, the garment felt less tight and more supportive in the same way that a good pair of compression leggings or a high-impact sports bra helps with your performance.

However, I struggled to wear it for long periods of time on work days spent typing away at my desk, especially when I first started wearing the Power Bra. I was always dyingggg to take the sports bra off by the end of the day. My body would be tired of being reminded to sit perfectly straight for hours, and it felt great to rip off the posture-correcting garment.

But after getting used to the sports bra over the course of more than a year, I actually feel relief putting the Power Bra on after a day of slouching. It's a way of reminding my body that it ultimately feels the best when it's aligned correctly rather than automatically slumping into a weird, compressed curve. But it's definitely something that takes practiced stamina to wear for long periods of time, so don't give up!

Kim had a similar experience: “It’s not the miracle bra one may hope it is. It definitely requires some active willpower to transform your slouching habits,” she explained. “Overall, I think it would be a good bra to wear while you’re running errands or rehearsing for a worldwide tour, but I found it to unfortunately be too uncomfy for me to wear at my desk.”

Beth, on the other hand, totally fell in love with the bra—especially for workouts. "My Pilates instructor actually kept complimenting my form(e, hehe), and even kept nudging me to add heavier resistance and to do more intense poses," she noted. "In her words, I was 'a pro." And I'll be totally honest, no instructor has ever, ever, ever said that to me, so I have to believe that some of it is because of this bra."

And as far as results? While wearing the Power Bra would give me an instant posture boost, I honestly haven't seen a noticeable, lasting difference in my posture despite “training” it by wearing the Power Bra under my clothes. When I took it off, I felt like I sagged right back into my slight but regular slouch. All of the posture magic seems to happen while you’re wearing the Power Bra, which is why I’d recommend it as extra support during a yoga class, dance rehearsals, or a super active day… not as something to wear Every! Single! Day! in hopes of magically curing your posture.

Taylor's sports bra comes in a bunch of colors...

While the Power Bra didn't transform me into a billionaire with an NFL husband (🥲), I totally understand why Taylor invested in this product for her dance rehearsals. The Forme bra (with stretchier fabric in the front and tighter fabric in the back to align your shoulders properly!) is a great way to remember to mind your posture when you’re being active, especially for shorter periods of time when you can tolerate the tight compression of the fabric.

And although I haven't seen any mind-blowing, permanent results over more than a year of testing, anything that reminds me what correct alignment should feel like (for both aesthetic and general health purposes) feels like a win in the long run. It was super easy to wear under clothes, so while it’s probably not going to be an everyday bra for me, I’ll definitely be taking advantage of it for Pilates classes, hiking, or more physically taxing days.

Next step? Learning Taylor’s Vigilante Sh*t choreo...

#6775. How Martine Rose Became Menswear’s Secret Weapon

After much speculation, the fashion rumors were true: Martine Rose is officially Balenciaga’s next creative director. It’s undoubtedly her biggest job yet—and a historic appointment: she is the first woman creative director to lead the French house. Rose succeeds Valentino vet Pierpaolo Piccioli, whose departure after just three seasons was announced yesterday. This is a major moment for the British-Jamaican designer, who has spent nearly two decades building a namesake label that wields outsize influence.

Rose has long occupied a curious position in menswear: a designer’s designer with a devoted following of both high-fashion heads and the streetwear obsessed. Since launching her eponymous brand in 2007, she’s developed an unmistakable visual language of playful styling, graphic-driven sportswear, offbeat tailoring, and covetable sneaker collabs. Her clothes take the familiar and make it strange, infusing everyday garments with humor, a touch of romance, and occasionally an ugly-chic sensibility: think square-toed loafers, chap-covered suit trousers, and soccer jerseys with lace-up fastenings. The results can be loud, wildly stylish, and routinely fun.

“I’ve always been drawn to the way men are told to dress,” the designer told GQ in 2023. She’s been on a mission to subvert those conventions ever since.

On the Martine Rose runway, sportswear and tailoring regularly share the spotlight. Much of that sensibility comes from her ability to borrow from and fuse London’s historic subcultures: ravers and club kids, football fanatics and nattily dressed mods. She tends to embrace the unusual and eccentric rather than focusing on making everything read as conventionally stylish. One look from a recent collection featured a swishy track jacket tucked into straight-leg blue jeans, and another paired a perfectly tailored coat with athletic shorts and knee-high socks. In Rose’s universe, there is no wrong way to mix and match. That’s part of her charm.

That sensibility eventually caught the eye of Demna during his decade-long tenure as Balenciaga’s creative director. The firebrand Georgian designer reached out to Rose in 2015, and she spent the next three years as a menswear consultant for the house during a high-profile era defined by supersized proportions and outlandish accessories. The blazers were big and the boots were bigger, but the star power of the celebrities who wore them was the biggest of all.

Rose is no stranger to dressing famous people. Her clothes have landed on some of the most photographed men in the world: Drake, Timothée Chalamet, Bad Bunny, A$AP Rocky. Perhaps her most visible endorsement came at the 2025 Super Bowl, when Kendrick Lamar strutted around the halftime show in a custom red, white, and blue leather varsity jacket designed by Rose. (In 2023, the rapper had boasted about how he wears “Martine when I board jets” on the Baby Keem track “The Hillbillies”; a year later, he sported several of her designs in his “Not Like Us” video.) Over the years, Rose has become one of the rare designers whose collections are covered by both Vogue and Hypebeast, a testament to her appeal across fashion’s various tribes. She’s been a fixture at London Fashion Week, and made her Milan debut in 2024. She has two hit Supreme collaborations under her belt. When Nike came calling, Rose produced remixed versions of the Shox (which Lamar wore while he accepted three Grammys in 2023) and Monarch sneakers—turning two of the Swoosh’s most divisive silhouettes into lustworthy objects. Tommy Hilfiger enlisted her for a collection centered on the streetwise style of Tommy Jeans in the ’90s. Brands know she can deliver the goods.

Which brings us back to the Balenciaga appointment. Rose obviously knows the house, or at least Demna’s vision for it—a vision that brought meme-worthy fashion but also massive creative and commercial success. A statement from Luca de Meo, CEO of Kering (the French luxury group that owns Balenciaga), included all the boilerplate press language about innovation, creativity, and culture. More crucially, Rose has proven she can make familiar clothes feel fresh, taking what guys are already wearing and turning them into something surprising and covetable. The question is now what Rose will be able to do with the resources of a global luxury powerhouse at her disposal. She’s spent much of her career building her own idiosyncratic world at Martine Rose. At Balenciaga, she’ll find herself on a much bigger stage with a vast audience and a completely new rulebook.

In 2020, when discussing her reputation as something of an underground darling, Rose told GQ: “I’ve never really wanted to be part of a big thing. Anything that is mainstream—since I was small—hasn’t really appealed to me.” Six years later, she’s earned one of the biggest jobs in fashion. There’s great fun in evolution, too.

#6774. Rosie Huntington-Whiteley and Wardrobe.NYC Feted Their Next Collaboration at Chateau Marmont

Los Angeles may slow down on Sunday evenings, but this past weekend, Chateau Marmont was anything but quiet. On the patio of the hotel’s penthouse, Wardrobe.NYC hosted an al fresco dinner celebrating its latest collaboration with supermodel Rosie Huntington-Whiteley, who also serves as fashion director at FWRD, where the 15-piece collection is exclusively available to shop.

Despite the gusty weather—which left half the table in near darkness as candles repeatedly blew out—longtime fans of the brand turned out for the occasion, including Laura Harrier, Lauren Perez, Vittoria Ceretti, Kirsty Godso, and Stephanie Suganami.

Not only was the first installment of the ongoing collaboration—a lineup of sculptural black-and-white separates that launched in 2024—a smashing success, but it also sparked a close bond between the brand and the style maven. “After the first one, we didn’t want it to be over,” Christine Centenera, a stylist and Vogue Australia’s editor-in-chief who co-founded the line with designer Josh Goot, told Vogue. Centenera was similarly effusive about Huntington-Whiteley’s magnetic energy and work ethic. “She’s really funny and can take criticism well, and she always has a creative solution,” Centenera noted.

The team took cues from the first drop when designing the second installment. “One of the top-selling pieces from the original line was a sculpted blazer, and so that kind of architectural feel was brought through into this collection,” Huntington-Whiteley, in a shearling stole from the capsule, explained. She then turned her attention to Ceretti, who was hobnobbing near the bar. “Vittoria is wearing the really cool shrunken leather bomber and great leather trousers [from the collection],” she added.

Huntington-Whiteley was equally grateful for the trio’s growing friendship. “I get to spend time with two incredibly talented people in the industry, two of my close friends, and create my dream collection that I want to fill my own wardrobe with,” she said. Ultimately, Huntington-Whiteley wanted to develop a versatile lineup that she could mix and match during her hectic international schedule. “It was all about creating a collection that I can keep in my suitcase and travel around with,” she shared. “It’s about the idea of a little black dress and those staple pieces that can take you from trips to events.”

For the first hour of the evening, guests mingled over cocktails and wine, taking in the twinkling city skyline. “The view here is magic,” said entrepreneur Perez, who arrived in a cropped blazer and slouchy low-rise trousers from the label.

Meanwhile, Raissa Gerona, Revolve’s president of brand and partnerships, looked around the lively crowd, appreciative of the community that had come together for the occasion. “Everybody’s so busy, people have kids, and it’s a Sunday, so people have work tomorrow,” she said. “So for everyone to show up to support Rosie and the brand, that’s the best thing I can ask for.”

Attendees settled into their seats at a candlelit table on the terrace, where servers promptly arrived with baguettes and French butter, roasted beet salad with goat cheese, burrata and tomatoes, and yellowtail crudo. Centenera and Huntington-Whiteley then rose to address the intimate crowd, thanking everyone—as much as possible over the whistling wind—for coming out to celebrate their latest creative pursuit.

Once guests finished their first round of dishes, the main course arrived: roasted chicken with mixed vegetables, and prime petit filet with asparagus spears, grilled broccolini, and truffle fries. The night ended on a sweet note, with bowls of berries and cream served for dessert, banishing any sign of the Sunday scaries.

#6773. The Shattered Bob Is Fall’s Most Wearable Short Haircut

Polished yet effortless, this is a look that can be easily tailored to every hair type.

Every item on this page was chosen by an ELLE editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

The bob is undoubtedly the haircut of the year, and it shows no sign of losing momentum as summer gives way to cooler weather. While warmer days favored sharp edges and sculpted lines, this fall, the bob is taking on a softer, more undone shape, with a relaxed, less polished finish.

Enter the shattered bob: a tousled, textured take on the classic cut that captures the undone mood seen at London Fashion Week and on celebrities including Margot Robbie, Halle Berry, and Indira Varma. Natural texture is taking center stage, bringing movement and ease to a style once defined by sculptural precision.

The shattered bob is a softer, more relaxed take on the classic cut. It looks polished without feeling overly styled. “We’re looking at jaw-to-shoulder lengths, slightly broken edges, subtle graduation, and a heavier front. It should feel lived-in rather than perfectly placed, almost as though the haircut has naturally fallen into that shape,” say hairstylists Stephen Buller and Anita Rice.

Cut to fall softly and grow out seamlessly, it needs far less day-to-day styling while still looking considered. The style reflects a wider shift in beauty toward embracing hair’s natural texture and a style that feels authentic, luxurious, and easy to manage.

Movement is key to the shattered bob. Ask your stylist for soft, varied layers and lightly broken-up ends to add texture and soften the cut’s outline. It can work especially well with wavy or curly hair, as layers enhance its natural shape and movement.

Bring reference photos of people with a similar hair type to help guide the consultation. Your stylist can use them to tailor the cut to your natural texture and face shape.

Keeping products light is the best way to maintain the cut’s airy feel. Styling should be minimal, as the appeal of the look is in its effortlessness. “There’s still that polished fashion element, but it’s being roughed up. It feels more individual and ultimately more wearable,” add Buller and Rice.

For texture and separation without weighing the hair down, work a bit of a texturizing mousse or cream like Davines Dry Texturizer through the mid-lengths and ends.

Ultimately, the shattered bob should be about ease and hair that works alongside your lifestyle, showing that the most sophisticated hair often looks effortless.

#6772. Can Men Have Multiple Orgasms?

One of the many reasons I love having a vagina is because the vagina famously has no refractory period—meaning that once I orgasm, I don’t technically need a break after one orgasm to experience another (unless I’m feeling super sensitive). In fact, I can have multiple Os at once. Iconic.

But I’m just finding out that people with penises might be able to do the same thing.

Yes, the classic male orgasm comes with an intermission, but some men can have two, three, or even more in a single sexual encounter. And no, we’re not talking about round two after a 20-minute break. Some men report orgasming again within seconds or minutes, sometimes without ejaculating at all.

So what’s going on physiologically? And can men actually learn to do this?

To understand how multiples are possible, it helps to know what happens during a typical male orgasm. “At climax, the brain processes intense pleasure while the nervous system coordinates involuntary, rhythmic contractions of the pelvic floor muscles,” explains board-certified urologist Dr. David Shusterman. Heart rate and breathing climb, muscle tension peaks, and then the body relaxes. If ejaculation accompanies the orgasm, semen moves into the urethra and is propelled out by muscular contractions.

Then comes the refractory period I mentioned earlier, a recovery phase in which another erection or orgasm is difficult or temporarily impossible. It can last anywhere from minutes to hours, or even longer. That’s the main reason multiples are harder for men. Male and female orgasms both involve the brain, sensory nerves, and rhythmic pelvic contractions, Dr. Shusterman says. The difference is what happens afterward.

That’s also where orgasm and ejaculation come in. They usually happen together, but they’re separate processes: orgasm is the pleasurable sensation of climax, and ejaculation is the physical release of semen. One can happen without the other, which may be why some men who report multiples skip the recovery phase altogether. Without ejaculation, they may stay sexually responsive.

Considering how much attention other aspects of men’s sexual health get, there’s surprisingly little research on this phenomenon. A 2016 review in Sexual Medicine Reviews, “Multiple Orgasms in Men—What We Know So Far,” estimated that fewer than 10 percent of men in their 20s and fewer than 7 percent of men over 30 are capable of them. The researchers stressed that the evidence was limited. They also identified two patterns. Sporadic multiple orgasms involve one orgasm, a wait of several minutes, and then another. Condensed multiple orgasms are a burst of two to four within seconds or a couple of minutes. Either way, they don’t always involve multiple ejaculations.

In a 1989 study in Archives of Sexual Behavior, researchers interviewed 21 men who reported multiple orgasms. Some had orgasms without ejaculation, both before and after an ejaculatory one, and some had a series of orgasms without losing their erections. More recently, a 2021 survey in The Journal of Sexual Medicine of 122 men found that people reported anywhere from two to 30 orgasms in a single encounter, though most said two to four. Most also kept their erections throughout and ejaculated with every orgasm.

A 2025 qualitative study in Sexual and Relationship Therapy interviewed 12 men who said they were capable of multiples. Ten said they could have them without ejaculating. Their techniques included mental and breathing control, pelvic floor muscle control, sex toys, and abstaining from sex beforehand. Some said they’d always been able to, while others learned over time.

Still, this was a very small study based on participants’ own accounts, not a controlled experiment. These techniques may be worth exploring, but there’s no guarantee they’ll turn anyone into a multiorgasmic man.

And if you’re not one, don’t panic. “Sexual satisfaction is not an orgasm count,” Dr. Shusterman says. “Having one orgasm, needing a longer recovery period, or not experiencing multiple orgasms does not by itself indicate a problem.”

Multiple orgasms might sound like the ultimate sexual superpower, but they’re hardly a requirement for a great sex life. Whether you’re having one, several, or none at all, the goal is to enjoy yourself.

#6771. 50 Marathons in 24 Days. Men Are Inventing Extreme Physical Challenges for the Hell of It

For more than 18 days straight this past May, Mason Wright woke up every morning at 5 a.m. and ran laps around a track at a high school in Layton, Utah. The early-spring air was typically crisp, and the smell of dew-covered grass reminded him of childhood. By the time students would arrive for class, he’d have run 10 miles. At lunch, he kept running. And when student athletes came out for after-school track practice he would still be there, running alongside them. When the sun finally set over the mountains, he’d crawl into a van parked beside the field, sleep as much as he could, and do it all over again. He ran through freezing mornings that left frost on the ground and rainstorms that flooded the track, leaving his feet so waterlogged that his skin split open.

When Wright finished his extended sprint, he became the third person in the world to run 1,000 fairly consecutive miles around a track, a project he embarked upon because, as he puts it, “I wanted something that genuinely sounded impossible.”

Mason Wright camped nearby a high school track to complete a self-assigned challenge of running 1,000 consecutive miles in a row.

When Wright finished his extended sprint, he became the third person in the world to run 1,000 fairly consecutive miles around a track.

“I wanted something that genuinely sounded impossible,” Wright says of the project.

Wright isn’t the only one with an appetite for the absurd. There’s now a never-ending slew of guys inventing and completing preposterous physical challenges, often with elaborate, self-imposed rules meant to make them as miserable as humanly possible. Many have teams chronicling every grueling mile and setback online. If they manage to pull it off the challenge, they emerge as semi-viral niche sports heroes in their corner of the fitness internet. In 2020, ultramarathoner and Navy Seal David Goggins launched his annual 4x4x48 challenge, inviting his followers to run four miles every four hours for 48 hours. In December 2025, UK-based athlete Travis Kerry set the world record for longest nonstop bear crawl after bear crawling 50 kilometers (31 miles) across the streets of London, and nine months later Kevin Ocipeka from Germany burpee broad-jumped his way 26.2 miles along a track in 155 hours, 44 minutes, and 51 seconds—longer and faster than anyone had burpeed before.

“There’s this flywheel effect of extreme one-upmanship going on on social media,” says Michael Ceely, a psychotherapist and high-performance coach who works with athletes.

But beyond bragging rights, why are men motivated to push themselves to such dire limits? And what, exactly, are they getting out of it?

Many of the men I spoke to for this piece are drawn to the unknown. Once they have an idea, they hyperfixate on it until they accomplish it. Professional mountain biker Matt Jones says the concepts come from just about anywhere. “That’s how my brain works,” he says. He can’t drive past a rooftop or construction crane without wondering whether he could ride it or jump off it. “It doesn’t take long before you start going down a rabbit hole of crazy ideas, and then actually becoming obsessed with them enough to wanna do them,” he says. For the stunt he pulled off in November 2025, he spent months calculating the precise timing needed to jump through the open trailers of two trucks traveling in opposite directions, with only a split-second margin for error.

Matt Jones in Sweden strategizing for his 2025 ‘Through’ project, in which he jumped through the open trailers of two trucks traveling in opposite directions.

Matt Jones performed a world first jumping between two moving trucks.

William Goodge, an ultrarunner who embarked on the arduous task of running 50 marathons in 50 states in 24 days this October, is similarly attracted to the uncertainty that comes with each challenge. “If I wasn’t scared by it, I wouldn’t be enthusiastic,” he says. “That’s the whole point in this journey for me.” His quest, which culminated at this year’s NYC marathon, was nearly as logistically challenging as it was physically demanding. A flight delay or bad weather could have set him back a few hours, potentially derailing his entire schedule. On four of the days, he needed to run three marathons with only short stretches of rest or travel between them.

The thrill of chasing a near-impossible goal can seem grim once you’re in the thick of it, though, and that misery is equally part of the appeal. Kerry had dreamed of earning a Guinness World Record since childhood, and when he saw someone bear-crawl a marathon, he decided to take the record even further: 31 miles, compared to 26. He tested the movement on a treadmill, taking 20 minutes to cover a single kilometer. It was, he says, “the hardest fucking thing I’ve ever done.” But he figured if he could do one kilometer, he could do 50. “It’s just one 50 times, and I just need to get better at it,” he says.

Yet halfway through his bear crawl across the cobbled streets of London, rain was coming down, his hands were cold, and he was wearing a pair of garden gloves he had cut the fingertips off to prevent his palms from getting torn on the pavement. “One of my best mate’s jobs the whole time was squeezing water like a literal udder out the end of my gloves,” he says. Kerry thought about calling it quits. “Even though I physically wasn’t at the end of my limit, mentally I was like, I just can’t do this anymore,” he says.

UK-based athlete Travis Kerry completed a 50-kilometer bear crawl across the cobbled streets of London in 2025.

Kerry still hasn't regained feeling in his fingertips after completing the challenge. “I don’t know if that’s ever gonna come back,” he says. “A small price to pay.”

His coach could tell he was losing steam, so she told him to stand up and scream. “I went to the edge of Tower Bridge and screamed the word ‘fuck’ as loud as I possibly could,” he says. The release felt incredible. He got back down on his hands and feet and kept crawling.

Despite the sense of accomplishment they represent, these feats can come at a serious physical cost. Both Wright and Kerry say they may have suffered permanent nerve damage after their massive efforts. Wright, who ran 400 miles on stress fractures, says the pain that would take over his body was “unbearable.” Kerry hasn’t regained feeling in his fingertips. “I don’t know if that’s ever gonna come back,” he says. “A small price to pay.” Jones acknowledges that he’s spent most of his most epic moments in the last 10 years in physical pain. “I think there’s a limit to the force you can take and the number of years you can take it for,” he says.

The men who do these challenges put their bodies through hell in the search for personal growth and self-discovery. Take Goodge. He began running after losing his mom to cancer. “I wanted to feel physical pain to deal with the mental and spiritual pain of what I was going through,” he says. Compared with what she endured, he says, his own discomfort seems trivial. “I’m doing it in her honor. I’m doing it for Stand Up to Cancer. I can carry on. I’ve got no excuse not to take another step.”

Wright also found himself confronting the grief of losing his father when he was younger during his 1,000-mile run. “When you lose someone close to you, your world stops, but the world around you continues,” he says. “You almost feel like you’re stuck in time.” Running endless laps around the same track while the students’ lives continued brought that feeling back. “It forces you to let go of these things or else you’ll go more crazy,” he says.

Matt Valaoras, who holds hours-long planks at music festivals and sporting events, started planking as a way to manage his anxiety. As a college student, he could only plank for 45 seconds, but he added on time every day until he could hold it for several hours. As he’s held his core steady through rough conditions (the worst of which was a swarm of bugs biting him while he planked the duration of a Savannah Bananas baseball game), he’s “stacked the evidence” that he can handle anything that’s thrown at him—physical or otherwise. “I can confidently say, ‘Okay, I can do this because I, I did that, and it was worse than I expected,’” he says.

Ceely says it’s common that physical suffering can sometimes be a socially acceptable way for men to process emotional suffering. “Men have a hard time expressing weakness or vulnerability or saying ‘I just feel bad, period,’” he says. “They want to say, ‘Here’s what I did to fix it.’ Which, in a way, is a healthy coping mechanism. It’s better than being depressed.”

Valaoras acknowledges that this is part of the equation for him. “For a lot of guys, myself included, it’s easier to exhaust yourself physically than explain what’s exhausting you mentally. I’ve definitely used exercise as a way to avoid confronting things emotionally.” Nevertheless, he says that following some kind of physical exertion, he feels more comfortable opening up.

Meanwhile, Kerry says that he uses his emotional suffering as a way of fueling his physical feats. “One who chooses suffering is choosing to use their brain’s deflection mechanism to channel that emotional suffering into physical capabilities,” he says. He references the so-called pain cave—a term that many distance runners have used to describe the most miserable moments of an event, when the brain and body start to wear thin. “By focusing on that emotional trigger,” he says, that “becomes the person’s ‘why’.”

This form of emotional release can create a powerful cycle of anticipation and reward—encouraging more and more challenges. “You get adrenaline, endorphins, dopamine,” Ceely says. “There’s a physiological kind of addiction that can start just because you’re human and you’re wired that way.”

And it becomes a lot harder to stop if you have an audience expecting an encore. While most of the athletes I spoke to insisted they would still be doing these challenges if no one was watching, social media introduces pressure and expectations; it “moves the goalposts because the wilder the idea is, the more it sets the standards and moves the bar,” says Jones. Wright acknowledges that you can’t “run these miles and post about it on social media without some ego involved.” As he planned his track run, he was set on doubling the stakes of his last challenge, which was a 421-mile run across Utah. “800 miles isn’t cool, so it had to be 1,000,” he says.

Jones says he wouldn’t attempt his most outlandish stunts without the pressure to hold onto his brand sponsorships or please the fans in his comment sections. He, unlike most men who we spoke to for this piece, is a professional Red Bull athlete, whose livelihood in part depends on pulling off these spectacles. “Would I jump through two moving trucks just for a laugh? Probably not,” he says. “No, I wouldn’t at all.” He nevertheless enjoys his audience’s admiration. “I guess that’s vanity in a way, isn’t it?” he says. “But it’s also the currency of what we do.”

None of these men seem particularly interested in finding a comfortable stopping point. Wright says he has yet to find his limit. Valaoras has world records attempts in mind. Goodge doesn’t plan on slowing down.

“I want to be on the edge. How close can we get without falling off?” Goodge says. “How close to the sun can we fly, like Icarus, and not get burnt?”

#6769. How to Plan a Trip to Easter Island, the Filming Location for ‘Wild Horse Nine’

After making a splash during the film festival circuit, director Martin McDonagh’s Wild Horse Nine has finally hit theaters. The dark comedy-thriller starring John Malkovich and Sam Rockwell follows two CIA operatives on their 1973 mission to the remote Pacific outpost of Easter Island. Followers of McDonagh’s work are no stranger to his interest in mind-bending landscapes (who can forget the melancholic ocean cliffs in The Banshees of Inisherin?). But the decision to shoot on Easter Island—or Rapa Nui, its indigenous name—was especially history-making.

To begin, Wild Horse Nine is not only the largest Hollywood production ever shot on Rapa Nui, but it’s also only the second. Then, of course, there are the herculean logistics of shipping period sets by boat to an island that sits more than 2,200 miles west of South America. And most critically, the crew worked hand-in-hand with local officials to coordinate filming locations that would not disrupt the archaeological sites or the sacred Rapa Nui culture. Notably, almost half of the island is part of the protected Rapa Nui National Park.

As British Vogue’s film and culture editor Radhika Seth notes, it’s a story that wins you over emotionally, and that ultimately stays with you. And if you come away from the theater with a burning desire to journey to Easter Island, let the expert-led guide below inform your 2027 travel plans.

Easter Island, or Rapa Nui, was the filming location for Martin McDonagh’s Wild Horse Nine. The island sits more than 2,200 miles west of South America and is among the most remote inhabited places in the world. It is famous for its nearly 1,000 moai statues scattered across the landscape.

Rapa Nui is an island in the eastern Pacific Ocean long regarded as one of the most remote inhabited places on Earth. It is located 2,200 miles west of mainland Chile and is the easternmost outpost within the chain of Polynesian islands.

“I think there’s something incredibly special about traveling somewhere that still requires a little more effort to reach,” Carola Fresno, Timbuktu Travel’s Latin America specialist, says. Arriving by air requires a five-hour flight from Santiago, Chile, and flight connections are often limited. Because of the long-haul journey, Fresno recommends a minimum of three nights on Rapa Nui, but suggests four or five if you can swing it. “There’s so much more to the island than the famous moai statues, and it’s worth leaving time to explore properly and get under the skin of the island and the culture,” she adds.

For an oceanside stay that’s within walking distance of the island’s main town, check into Nayara Hangaroa. Each of its 75 guest rooms comes with a private terrace, and the design is driven by natural materials like cypress logs, clay, and volcanic stone. Sustainability programs go beyond the expected solar panels, extending to a local initiative that collects, recycles, and compresses and molds microplastics to transform waste material into moai figurines—a clever keepsake that reminds visitors of the importance of protecting the surrounding ocean. Guest experiences are shaped by the Hitorangi family, part-owners and cultural stewards of Nayara Hangaroa, and range from traditional dance classes and cooking demonstrations, to visits to a local beekeeper to learn why the honeybees of Rapa Nui are thought to be the world’s healthiest.

If your nationality allows you to enter Chile without a visa, one is not required for Rapa Nui. However, visitors are mandated to complete an online entry form.

It’s possible for visitors to rent a car, scooter, or bicycle to explore Rapa Nui, but many of the archaeological sites are regulated and require an accredited local guide. “Personally, I think the real value of visiting Rapa Nui lies in understanding its culture, and that’s where a knowledgeable local guide becomes invaluable,” Fresno says, who always recommends that her travelers explore with a driver and local guide.

Luxury tour operator Abercrombie & Kent partnered with Searchlight Pictures to create a Wild Horse Nine itinerary inspired by the film. “We’re seeing growing interest in film-inspired travel, particularly when a destination plays such an important role in the story,” explains A&K’s Adam Fogg, who created the itinerary. The journey begins in Santiago, discovering the events surrounding the 1973 coup highlighted in the film, eventually transporting travelers to the remote shores of Rapa Nui to explore its ancient culture and archaeology. On the island, guests enjoy dinner in a local family’s home before embarking on a nocturnal exploration. “Inspired by the legends of Haumaka and Hotu Matu'a, they discover how early voyagers crossed vast stretches of the Pacific using the stars, ocean currents, and natural elements to guide them,” Fogg says.

Beyond the film, excursions on the island range from trekking volcanic craters to horse riding up to the island’s highest point on Mount Terevaka. “One of my absolute favorite experiences is hiking along the northern coast of the island,” Fresno says. This hike showcases a quieter side of Rapa Nui and finishes at the white sands of Anakena Beach. For travelers eager to visit one of the island’s dormant volcanoes, Fresno points to a longer hike in the Poike area, which explores the volcanic landscapes and caves before continuing towards Ahu Tongariki, where 15 moai stand on a single ceremonial platform. “It’s one of the most mesmerizing sites on the island.”

If you’re a diver, the island’s volcanic origins can be explored via underwater cliffs, caves, and rock formations. “The water is this extraordinary, almost electric shade of blue,” Fresno says. “And while you won’t necessarily find the abundance of coral and marine life associated with some tropical diving destinations, the combination of exceptional visibility, volcanic scenery and the vastness of the surrounding Pacific makes diving here really special.”

Fogg notes that one of the most impactful ways to support the local community is being thoughtful about where your tourism dollars go, which includes dining at family-run restaurants. And while Rapa Nui isn’t a destination brimming with elaborate fine dining, that’s not really the point of coming here. Travelers will encounter plenty of Polynesian influences in the local cooking, as well as Chilean flavors. “I always encourage my travelers to try the seafood, particularly the tuna,” Fresno says. “The island’s isolation…is generally much more relaxed and informal, and I think that’s part of its charm.” While here, seek out an umu, which is a traditional Polynesian method of cooking food in an earth oven using heated stones.

In addition to dining at family-run restaurants and booking family-owned hotels, respecting archaeological sites and local traditions is equally important. “The island's moai and ceremonial sites hold profound spiritual and cultural significance, and visiting responsibly helps protect this extraordinary heritage,” he says.

Rapa Nui has a subtropical climate with rain throughout the year. The warmest month is February, with highs around 80 to 82 degrees Fahrenheit, while the coldest tends to be August, with lows around 59 to 63. The best time of year to visit Easter Island is between April and May and September through October when the temperatures are mild, and there are fewer tourists. If your priority is to have ideal beach conditions, consider January, February, or March (but expect bigger crowds).

#6767. 13 Best Bathrobes for Men 2026: Reviewed by GQ

The best bathrobes for men will turn your modest apartment into a luxury spa in two seconds flat. Sadly, you'll still have to acquire all the relevant permits before you can charge randos for the home massage experience, but that's okay—a swaddly robe is more of a solo thing anyway.

The point: If you're gonna treat yourself to a robe, the robe has to treat you right, too. That means avoiding the cheap-o glorified towels with scratchy materials that just end up pilling after a couple washes. What you actually want is the creme de la creme of men’s bathrobes, the ones kitted out with the fluffiest, softest materials that take it from a mere post-shower dry-off to soul-filling self-care.

To help you in your quest for relaxation supremacy, we tested a slew of bathrobes to find the best of the best, which you'll find the below. While you're here, be sure to check out our guide to the best pajamas.

Imagine stepping out of the shower, wrapping yourself in a plush, thick towel... and then never having to take it off. Brooklinen’s best-selling towels are a mainstay of our home coverage, and the DTC brand's robes employ the same tricks of of the trade: absorbent, piled Turkish cotton repackaged into a calf-length, unisex bathrobe that feels as good as it looks. If ensconcing yourself in cloud-like, towel-caliber cotton isn't doing it for you, extra deep pockets to hide your snacks coupled with those eye-grabbing botanical stripes alone are worth the price of admission.

Quince is in the business of providing luxurious staples at non-luxury price tags. Its robe, one that feels like it could've been hung inside a five-star hotel room's closet, fits the bill. It's constructed from an ultra-soft long-staple cotton, complemented with two patch pockets for throwing in your hands (or the television remote) with a simple-to-use sash for making sure no one's getting a peek at your unsightly goods.

You can cancel that ritzy spa appointment: you don’t actually need to leave the house to score the type of restorative treatment that leaves you feeling refreshed and ready to dive back into your inbox—until, like, Wednesday. Turns out, all you need to cultivate peak spa vibes is a peak spa robe, like Onsen's deceptively absorbent, quick-drying silky Supima cotton number. Wearable in any climate, just looking at this cushy long robe from your steamy shower will set your mind at ease, and the extra-chunky waffle weave—cozy, but not clingy—more than justifies the price, especially when you consider the money you'll save not booking that retreat in the Alps.

If the de facto brand for cool-guy jammies doesn't make you want to cancel your plans and post up at home, its royally fluffy bathrobes will. The Scandi loungewear whisperers at Tekla cut their classic bathrobe from a soft-yet-sturdy midweight pile that’s intentionally slinky, so you can sashay from the bedroom to the kitchen fridge like you’re waltzing down a runway. There are, of course, generously-sized pockets for stashing your phone, remote, sundry snacks, etc. But the real selling point is the range of 19 (!) hyper-tasteful colors it's available in. The only con: limiting yourself to just one.

Dusen Dusen's playful takes on homewares and home decor make it the brand you turn to for something that'll have your houseguests going, “where'd you get that from?” Maybe you won't be wearing a robe in front of said guests, but they can admire its beauty when it's hanging in your bathroom. These printed robes are super plush for post-bath drying sessions, and cozy enough to accidentally fall asleep in while you're sitting on the couch waiting for your hair to dry.

There are robes you dry in, robes you lounge in, and then robes like this: an option meant to live in like you own a McMansion, smoke cigars every night, and enjoy a dram of scotch to “take the edge off.” This Derek Rose cotton robe has a satin-like finish, with a subtle stripe detail that gives it more of a pop than just a regular old blue colorway. The white piping also adds a bit more flair to what could otherwise be a boring robe, and three patch pockets help you tote your things around if you don't have anything going on under there.

Reigning Champ, the Vancouver-based brand behind some of our all-time favorite sweats, has been giving us the warm and fuzzies since we first pulled on its stellar heavyweight hoodie. So imagine our delight when Canada's foremost fleece whizzes brought their particular set of skills to the humble bathroom robe, juicing up the silhouette in the same loopback cotton-jersey that makes their loungewear so great. Wear this terry cloth robe while catching up on the Great British Bake Off from the comfort of your couch—and to make the inevitable trip to the grocery store for miscellaneous pie ingredients immediately afterwards.

This is type of robe you throw on when you want to toast things up but have zero plans to leave the house and therefore refuse to put on "actual" clothes. It has a sweet shawl collar and a full length that gives you all the cozy coverage you want.

What's better than sleeping on a cloud? Being swaddled in one while you slowly meander aorund your apartment. The Parachute robe offers a barely there feel, made possible thanks to its four-ply gauze construction. This one has side-seam pockets that make it easy to slip into

Hommey's catalog of cheerful colors and patterns help the brand stand out, but they're more than just nice to look at. They're made of a plush 100% cotton terry fabric that's as cozy as it gets and cut with a comfortable but un-sloppy silhouette. And while so many other robes use a shawl collar, we love that this one features an indulgently huge notch lapel.

Missoni's unmistakable and kaleidoscopic chevron patterns make for a suite of riche robes that telegraphs your bathroom is full of fancy soaps and lotions. Cut from an all-cotton French terry with a velour-like brushed hand, we'd understand if you'd never want to leave your home.

You may not live in a palazzo in Florence, but Brunello's rakish robe will make you feel like you do. The only thing missing? A cheeky cigar.

A Purple Label look, for Polo prices. Thank you, Ralph.

Material: A lot of fabric is about to touch your naked body, so you better make sure your robe feels good. Higher quality fabrics lead to higher price tags, but we found that it's worth it considering your whole body is about to be swaddled in it. For robes, cotton is the preferred fabric, and there are three common types: Egyptian cotton is the softest and plushes, Turkish cotton is known to be fast-drying, and supima cotton is just as soft as Egyptian cotton with the same quick-dry properties as Turkish. Lastly, you'll also see a lot of waffle weaves, which is mostly a design choice. Not only does it lend a certain look, we also like it because it's extremely absorbent and dries quickly.

Features: Robes don't offer too much in the features department, but at a bare minimum, find a robe with pockets. Two is probably the norm, with some offering a treasured third in the form of a chest pocket. Waist ties keep everything hidden and secure underneath, while some robes offer the option to remove them. And while maybe not a “feature” we chose to highlight robes that come in a variety of prints, patterns, and colors, because bone white robes for everyone isn't exactly fun.

Sizing: Shopping for robes is a lot like shopping for clothes: You need to find something that fits. We preferred to highlight robes that were size inclusive, in that a wide selection of sizes were available. Do note that some of the robes here feature combo sizes, which could make it difficult to figure out which option correlates to the typical shirts you'd wear. Take advantage of any size guides to really help you lock in your correct sizing.

Testing robes is no easy feat. We had to strip down to our skivvies, throw on a bunch of cozy options, and, well, chill. We made note of each robe's comfort factor, which usually had to do with the material of the robe. All of the robes we featured here are sure to suit the preference of basically anyone who would ever shop for a robe, whether they want a hood, prefer something to keep them dry and warm after a shower, or just lounge in like a baller.

#6766. Gwyneth Paltrow Wants You to ‘Experience the Magic’ of Learning With Her New Podcast, The Goop Guide

Gwyneth Paltrow is no stranger to the world of podcasts. In 2018, the actress-turned-wellness brand founder launched The Goop Podcast to further tap into the intersection of health and lifestyle, bringing A-list celebrities and experts on the show to create an open dialogue on the best approach to living—but in Paltrow’s own words, that version of the podcast arrived just ahead of the long-form content boom and felt more “homespun” as a result.

Her new podcast, The Goop Guide With Gwyneth Paltrow—premiering today, November 11, through Spotify and Netflix—will see her joined by “a friend, fellow founder, doctor, family member, colleague, ex-boyfriend, guru, or expert” to “guide her audience on how to live Goop in all the ways that can be defined.” She plans to dive into topics like “the art of reinvention, raising a family, breaking up with friends, Thanksgiving dinner,” and more, depending on the guest of the week.

The goal of the podcast, Paltrow tells ELLE, is to offer a learning opportunity to herself and her listeners. “I think learning defines me more than anything else, like I have so much curiosity about us as human beings, how we move through the world, how we can heal, how we can be better citizens of the world,” she says. She wants her fans to “experience the magic that comes with understanding someone else’s life, their point of view, and what’s making them feel inspired.”

Below, Paltrow speaks exclusively with ELLE about The Goop Guide, detailing her future hopes for the podcast, her dream guests, and more.

We had done The Goop Podcast for many years and it launched before the podcast space became what it is now, so there was no visual component and it was very homespun. As we went on, people wanted it to have video, and so we took a pause so I could think about launching a new podcast in a new format and figure out what I wanted to say or what was missing from the original. The Goop Guide goes back to the very, very early days of Goop and what the point of what Goop originally was, which was to provide recommendations and get to the heart of the best thing. I’m enjoying looking at it through the lens of like, “This person is the Goop Guide to X.”

I would say it’s a completely new show, a completely revamped format. Media is in an interesting place where there’s so many different platforms and our algorithms are surfacing what we see, but at Goop, we like to think about it being this source of trust and bringing that kind of humanity back into curation, which excites me about the show.

It’s also in a longer format, which juxtaposes YouTube Shorts, TikTok, and other bite-sized ways we digest content. It’s interesting that podcasts are growing so much in popularity, because it’s like a healing balm. You put on a podcast and cozy up with someone in a longer form and they’re very different from the AI- and algorithm-driven content we’re being inundated with all the time.

A lot of the website is what we make, love, and sell, and it’s transactional, but the podcast is about curiosity. We’re doing that through having meaningful conversations with these guests and trying to approach it from, “What is this person’s unique point of view?” and “What are we here to learn?”

It’s important to me that we continue to have a varied show. I love celebrities, but I need variety to deepen my own life, so we have people that aren’t just artists.

The launch episode is with Zoe Saldaña, who is an amazing woman and someone I have real admiration for with how she conducts herself in her career and as a mother and a friend. We also have Ke Huy Quan, the actor who won Best Supporting Actor at the Oscars for Everything Everywhere All at Once, and he was in The Goonies when I was a kid, so I’ve loved him my whole life. Also, people like Rick Rubin, who, to me, is like a modern-day sage and spiritual figure. I’m also working on bringing in people who are MDs, scientists, sociologists, or modern-day philosophers. I think it’s really important that we have episodes that feel like being in a classroom.

The first person who popped into my mind so strongly—who unfortunately is no longer with us—was Dolly Parton. To me, she represents amazing, strong women who are legendary and who have redefined ideas about what women are capable of. I would love to have Hillary Clinton as a guest, and I would want to have a legend on the podcast, too. Since it can’t be Dolly, I’d love to have Cher.

I hope that fans get out of it what I get out of it, which is getting really interesting stories that make us feel uplifted. I want them to experience the magic that comes with understanding someone else’s life, their point of view, and what’s making them feel inspired.

I would like to take it on the road! I love the idea of bringing the podcast to the U.K. to host an interview. There’s an audience there and it would be slightly less intimate, but live podcast episodes have this other very alive quality. The performance aspect of it would be so interesting to explore.

This interview has been edited for clarity.

#6765. Kate Middleton Opens Up About Her Health Journey in a Rare New Interview

Kate Middleton is sharing a rare insight into her healing journey and cancer recovery. In a new interview published on November 11 in Country Life magazine, whose latest issue was guest-edited by her husband, Prince William, the Princess of Wales spoke to writer Robert Macfarlane about nature, mental health, healing, and more.

“I was told in hospital that my recovery would not be linear,” Catherine said in the interview, which focused on the role of the natural world in the future queen’s life. “There will be good days and bad days, that it will take time. This is the same in nature. It shows us that periods of rest, reflection and renewal are equally as important as moments of growth.”

Catherine has been in remission since January 2025, and has used her royal platform to speak about the ways in which time in the great outdoors has helped her recover. For her 44th birthday in January 2026, she shared a poignant final installment of “Mother Nature,” her video series that explored the relationship between wild spaces, mental resilience, and physical healing. Recently, in October, she visited Dose of Nature, a charity that focuses on nature-based mental health care.

Catherine, Princess of Wales, arrives for a visit to The Chase Community Hospital on November 11, 2026.

On Wednesday, November 11, Catherine made an appearance at The Chase Community Hospital in Bordon, Hampshire, as part of her work with the Royal Foundation Centre for Early Childhood. She wore what looks to be a new suit by London it-girl label Bella Freud, pairing it with pops of red in various shades, including a cranberry turtleneck, a burgundy Smythson bag, burgundy Gianvito Rossi heels, and a remembrance poppy in observance of Armistice Day.

At the hospital, the princess met with doctors and health workers who use the Alarm Distress Baby Scale (ADBB), a training that helps identify early signs of social withdrawal in infants in order to support them. This is the latest step that Catherine has taken with her early childhood healthcare initiative, which she launched in 2021.

In her Country Life interview, Catherine also spoke about her interest in childhood development, including how it impacts her own parenting style as a mother to Prince George, Princess Charlotte, and Prince Louis. “We are often so busy we simply forget to pause, slow down and notice the detail in life. It is something I remind myself to do with my own children; to give them freedom to explore, rather than constantly telling them what to see or what to think,” she said.

Catherine, Princess of Wales, meets nine-month-old Ruby during a visit to The Chase Community Hospital on November 11, 2026 in Bordon, England.

#6764. ‘DWTS’ Fans Are Side-Eyeing Tyler Cameron After He Reposted Jokes About Harry Shum Jr.’s Emotional Salsa Routine

Harry Shum Jr. and Jenna Johnson’s salsa routine unexpectedly brought tears to the Dancing With the Stars ballroom (and it had nothing to do with Jenna’s broken nose).

Before he and Jenna hit their steps to Lady Gaga and Bruno Mars’s Grammy-winning ballad, “Die With a Smile,” the Glee alum opened up about his personal connection to the dance genre—and why he suddenly felt a wave of emotion during rehearsals.

“I was born in Costa Rica, so like people were dancing salsa, merengue, bachata, but I was always an observer. So I’m really excited to tap into my roots,” Harry said of his heritage during the intro package that played before his performance.

After he and Jenna went through their routine in the rehearsal studio, he started tearing up.

“I don’t think I’ve ever felt such a strong emotion damn after dancing. I didn’t realize it, but Salsa is very meaningful to me,” Harry admitted. “I’ve kind of avoided doing salsa dancing for so long. I associated it with the struggles that my family went through, and I went through, moving from Costa Rica to America. But I just got a wave of emotion of just being really sad that I’ve missed the joy of salsa dancing for so long.”

While DWTS fans generally empathized with Harry’s feelings surrounding the routine, others joked about the lack of context in how his package was edited and claimed his reaction was “performative.”

“There’s something very performative about him,” one person wrote, to which another responded, “No he’s very open and honest and kind. I love him.”

A viewer on Reddit separately came to Harry’s defense, writing, “Its always so sad to see someone feeling disconnected from their culture AND it should be cool for people to possibly connect with that and anyone hating has never experienced that emotion most likely.”

Another person who quietly weighed in on the rehearsal package? That’d be Bachelor Nation’s Tyler Cameron, who was eliminated from the competition on October 27.

Fans raised eyebrows when Tyler reposted a TikTok poking fun at Harry’s tearful experience. “Harry Shum jr when the chipotle worker asks if he wants salsa on his burrito bowl,” the post reads, over a video of American Horror Story star Jessica Lange crying.

Once DWTS devotees caught wind of Tyler’s repost, they couldn’t tell if the joke went too far.

“So strange considering Tyler’s still in the comments on literally everyone’s posts every week cheering them on :(,” one person wrote, while another called out the eliminated dancer, writing, “So distasteful for Tyler to repost.”

Another person shared, “i was liking tyler but he still is talking about getting voted off weeks later😭 he’s being a weirdo right now.”

As of right now, the video is still filed under Tyler’s TikTok reposts. He has yet to speak on it, if at all, but he did share his thoughts on Jenna Dewan and Tatyana Ali’s performances on his Instagram Stories posted on November 11.

Harry also hasn’t publicly responded to the online chatter surrounding his performance, but I’ll be keeping an eye out to see if anything else unfolds!

#6763. The 15 Best Chelsea Boots for Men in 2026: Never Go Out of Style

The best Chelsea boots for men are trend-proof in a way that'll make you breathe a sigh of relief. Especially right now, when menswear feels like a WWE Royal Rumble, where anything goes and trends pile up like bodies in and out of the ring. But there, floating confidently above everything, is the trustworthy Chelsea boot.

If we had to make an educated guess as to why the Chelsea boot has stuck around for so long? Good design is good design. The slip-on simplicity, the sharp silhouette, the unadorned, uncomplicated details—the pared-back design makes it highly adaptable to any wardrobe and occasion, trends be damned. So don't be ashamed to cash in on a fleeting fad, but when it comes time for you to do some spring cleaning, we'd advise against letting go of a pair of Chelseas.

We've rounded up 15 of the best Chelsea boots out there, from hardwearing stompers to exquisite stunners, $200 steals to $1,500 beauties. Finding the perfect pair can be hard—but pulling them on? Easy.

Ask us what Chelsea boot you should get, regardless of price, and we'll point to the R.M. Williams Turnout boot, because it does so many things right that the competition can't. Off the bat, it’s a real stunner. The silhouette is sleek and chiseled, timeless and fashion forward. The leather is perfectly suited for a shine and polish to attend any black tie event, but takes on a handsome patina when worn hard. Plus, the quality is fantastic. The wholecut leather uppers are shaped as a single piece of cowhide rather than several panels stitched together, resulting in a shoe that’s as pretty as it is durable. With Goodyear-welted construction for easy peasy resoling, you’ll have these Aussie-born boots for well over a decade.

How does a boot originally built for Down Under cowboys manage to be the best-looking dress boot and hardest-wearing boot at the same time? Hard to say. The only drawback of the R.M. Williams Heritage Craftsman Chelsea is the price. But considering everything it can do—and how long it'll last—the math makes sense.

For the budget conscious/the dude looking for his first pair of “nice” Chelsea boots, my answer is easy: Beckett Simonon. Menswear bros might scoff at a web-only DTC clothing brand, but unlike the other brands with infinite marketing budgets looking to “disrupt the entire industry”, Beckett Simonon seems content with making great shoes at a great price, without all the soulless millennial pandering. The Bolton, a slick sleek, true-to-form  Chelsea boot, is just the poster child for the brand’s modus operandi. It uses full-grain Italian calfskin, which is lined with Vachetta leather, stitched by master shoemakers in Columbia using a Blake stitch construction.

In other words, it’s a Chelsea boot with A-list looks, Northamptonshire-style pedigree, and a price that’s at least half of the competition. But if there’s one thing that will stop most people from buying a pair immediately is, well, you can’t get them immediately. Each pair is made to order, which means you’ll have to wait a month or two to get yours. But you know what they say: patience is a virtue.

Some Chelsea boots lean heavily into their rockstar appeal, while others skew utilitarian, expressly designed to protect you from the elements. Then there, however, are the rare pairs that gracefully straddle the line between. Enter the other Australian boot: Blundstone. Those tough leather uppers might not be monsoon-ready, but on days when you just need to jump a few puddles en route to work, the whole package is the slickest way to ensure your socks stay dry without compromising your fit.

Tired Bushwick memes aside, Blunnies are truly good looking boots built like a tank. They can take a real whooping and still show up for their 5 a.m. shift behind the espresso machine, ready for more. They’re a boot you don’t have to feel precious about, and the de facto kicks to wear when the weather forecast just says “shitty.” They look great with vintage jeans, a pair of Dickies, cargo pants, and chinos—though, in our humble opinion, they don't belong anywhere with a dress code (explicit or implied).

Hedi Slimane’s legacy at Saint Laurent was all skinny jeans, razor-sharp suits, and rockstar Chelsea boots. The look he cooked up over a decade ago has come back again, with guys dubbing themselves Hedi Boys looking to recreate the magic. One glance at boots like these, and you understand why. A leading man gleam and a stacked Cuban heel mean these boots by themselves could headline Coachella. The only update: a very right-now edginess (literally) at the toe. Whether or not the rumored skinny jeans comeback arrives, these are the boots to wear when you want all eyes on you.

Astorflex has long been one of the best value propositions in the shoe space. The Tuscan vegetable-tanned leather and multigenerational craftsmanship alone should be worth more than what you'll actually pay. But value doesn't equate to style. Thankfully, the Bitflex has that in spades. Not too pointy and not totally bulbous, the shape splits the difference masterfully and looks great paired with any kind of pants. Plus, the cushioned crepe sole plays down the dressy factor while adding a ton of comfort straight out of the box.

Unlike other crepe-soled boots more famous than these—ahem—these don’t feel suspiciously soft, despite their extremely comfortable feel. In my experience, Astorflex boots maintain great arch support and mold to your feet, but also are built up enough to provide some noticeable ankle support. This is certainly the most casual Chelsea boot here, so don’t even think of trying to wear these to your friend’s wedding. You’re not “doing something”, you’re being disrespectful.

If you think of Red Wing, it's the brand’s iconic 6-inch moc toe boots you’re probably imagining. But the Minnesotan boot makers can do more than turn out jobsite-endorsed footwear. Introduced in the 1990s, Red Wing’s Chelseas were made for ranchers who wanted something durable enough for the farm, but easy enough to nap in. They’re a happy middle ground between a high-shine European Chelsea like the R.M. Williams and the woodsy Blundstones.

That means you get Goodyear-welted construction and built-to-stroll rubber outsoles topped by Hawthorne Muleskinner roughout suede. The texture is “rugged handyman,” but the dusty tan and dark brown color can hold down a coffee date-worthy fit. White tees and white jeans? Sure. Nice pants and a knit sweater or polo? Absolutely. Wranglers and a Stetson. Of course.

Morjas is best known for loafers worn by everyone from Wall Street bros to Greenpoint baristas. Now the Swedish shoemakers are coming for your ankles with a Chelsea boot. If you’ve already graduated from Chelsea Boots 101 by having a classic black or brown calf-leather pair in your closet, consider these brown suede beauties for next semester.

They're fully leather-lined but require absolutely zero break-in time, thanks to a studded rubber sole that feels like walking on a cloud... if clouds had traction. Style-wise, they walk the line (pun intended) between office-ready and weekend cool—equally dialed in when paired with a crisp white dress shirt beneath unforgiving Monday-morning fluorescents or some linen pants while you sip a doppia espresso in Florence.

You already know what that yellow stitching means. Dr. Martens’ chunky, iconic boots bring an EVA insole and the brand's patented oil- and slip-resistant AirWair sole to the water-resistant oiled leather. Add a pull tab for easy entry, and the only thing easier than slipping them on is adding them to cart when you realize they’re on sale.

By cutting out the middleman and selling direct-to-consumer, Meermin offers handsome, Goodyear-welted dress shoes—all made from leather with absurdly tony provenances—for well below what the other guys charge. You can’t go wrong with any of the brand’s boots, but a mocha-hued suede Chelsea looks especially killer in the fall.

Blundstones again, you say? Well, yes. But this pair has a leather lining and plusher sole, which make them both more comfortable and longer lasting, if for a slightly higher price.

From the rugged to the refined. Chelsea boots are named after London's affluent Chelsea neighbourhood, and this model from British quality shoemakers Crockett & Jones' lives up to that name perhaps more than any on this list.

The Spanish shoe-whisperers at Camper fly a little under the radar compared to their British counterparts, but their Chelsea boots offer just as much swaggering rock-god energy as any other pair on this list. Thanks to a cushy OrthoLite footbed, though, you can wear ‘em long after Mick Jagger takes his final bow.

The chatter about Our Legacy's Camion boots never seems to end. That handsome silhouette, the '90s-esque square toe, and the slick side zip make these a modern-day classic.

Viberg makes boots on steroids in its family-owned Canadian factory, turning out museum-worthy footwear designed to stomp through the worst the world—or the ground, at least—has to offer. These Chelseas are cut from a single piece of waxed leather, like a Barbour jacket for your feet.

NYC-based 3sixteen has been on an absolute footwear tear lately—first with its slick Saucony collab, and now with a lug-soled Chelsea boot that stands on business. The whole shoe smashes together slightly disparate details—an elegant tobacco-colored suede, a workmanlike wedge lugged sole, an skyscraper-height ankle—that add up to something fresh in the normally staid world of Chelsea boots. And if you’ve ever broken in a pair of 3sixteen’s top-tier jeans, you already know: things only get better as you put in the steps.

Chelsea boots have always looked cool—anywhere, on anyone, at any time. They were invented in the 1800s, but won their cultural cred later: the Beatles gave them a massive co-sign in the ’60s, then Hedi Slimane anchored his Saint Laurent rocker suits with them while rappers stacked their denim on top of Chelsea boots during the 2010s.

Which goes to show that Chelsea boots work with damn near anything in your closet, from your favorite Levi’s to baggy chinos to louche suits—even crew socks and shorts, depending on the boots and your taste for channeling some Pacific Northwest crunchiness. For more, check out our guide on how to wear Chelsea boots.

Style is subjective, we know—that’s the fun of it. But we’re serious about helping our audience get dressed. Whether it’s the best white sneakers, the flyest affordable suits, or the need-to-know menswear drops of the week, GQ Recommends’ perspective is built on years of hands-on experience, an insider awareness of what’s in and what’s next, and a mission to find the best version of everything out there, at every price point.

Our staffers aren’t able to try on every single piece of clothing you read about on GQ.com (fashion moves fast these days), but we have an intimate knowledge of each brand’s strengths and know the hallmarks of quality clothing—from materials and sourcing, to craftsmanship, to sustainability efforts that aren’t just greenwashing. GQ Recommends heavily emphasizes our own editorial experience with those brands, how they make their clothes, and how those clothes have been reviewed by customers. Bottom line: GQ wouldn’t tell you to wear it if we wouldn’t.

We make every effort to cast as wide of a net as possible, with an eye on identifying the best options across three key categories: quality, fit, and price.

To kick off the process, we enlist the GQ Recommends braintrust to vote on our contenders. Some of the folks involved have worked in retail, slinging clothes to the masses; others have toiled for small-batch menswear labels; all spend way too much time thinking about what hangs in their closets.

We lean on that collective experience to guide our search, culling a mix of household names, indie favorites, and the artisanal imprints on the bleeding-edge of the genre. Then we narrow down the assortment to the picks that scored the highest across quality, fit, and price.

Across the majority of our buying guides, our team boasts firsthand experience with the bulk of our selects, but a handful are totally new to us. So after several months of intense debate, we tally the votes, collate the anecdotal evidence, and emerge with a list of what we believe to be the absolute best of the category right now, from the tried-and-true stalwarts to the modern disruptors, the affordable beaters to the wildly expensive (but wildly worth-it) designer riffs.

Whatever your preferences, whatever your style, there's bound to be a superlative version on this list for you. (Read more about GQ's testing process here.)
