#6759. These Long-Lasting Tubing Mascaras Survive Sweat, Tears, and Long Days

We may earn commission from links on this page, but we only recommend products we love. Promise.

I’ve always been particular about mascara. I need a formula that lengthens my lashes to the heavens, won’t flake or smudge, and stays locked in place from morning to night. After years of testing and reviewing mascaras as a beauty editor, I’ve found that there is only one type of formula that manages to do it all: tubing mascaras.

The best tubing mascaras are in a league of their own. Instead of simply coating lashes with pigment, they wrap each individual lash in tiny, water-resistant polymers that form a flexible “tube” around the hair. The result? A mascara that won’t smear, flake, or run throughout the day, yet slides off effortlessly with warm water when it’s time to take it off.

It almost sounds too good to be true, right? The best tubing mascaras do feel like magic—team Cosmo would know. We’ve tried every tubing mascara out there, and somehow we narrowed our favorites down to this list of seven foolproof (and smudge-proof) formulas, below.

Why we love it: Ask any beauty editor or makeup artist what their favorite tubing mascara is, and most will mention this magic formula. “Liquid Lash Extensions is the tried-and-true, the pioneer of tubing mascaras,” says professional makeup artist Brittany Whitfield. “It’s my absolute favorite for length.” This takes natural lashes to new heights and won’t smudge—even in high heat—yet it still easily washes off at the end of the day. Plus, the formula comes in eight hues, ranging from black to ivy green for plenty of makeup experimentation.

One Cosmo editor says: “I’m a bit of a mascara snob, and I always find myself coming back to this formula. This is completely flake and smudge-free and keeps my lashes looking full and long until I wash my makeup off at the end of the night. It’s a top three mascara of all time in my book.” — MARY HONKUS, CONTRIBUTING BEAUTY EDITOR

Why we love it: For those who want more natural-looking fringe that still appears fresh after 12+ hours, Merit’s clean formula is ultra-reliable. This imparts a soft black tint and ever so slightly lengthens lashes to achieve the no-makeup makeup look in just a few swipes.

One Cosmo editor says: “Clean Volume is the perfect name for this surprisingly un-clumpy volumizing mascara. The formula coats every lash, making my eyelashes look thicker and more fanned out, but it doesn't give them a spidery or clumped together look at all. The wand really helps separate every lash, and it even helps hold a curl for quite a few hours.”—BETH GILLETTE, BEAUTY EDITOR

Why we love it: Don’t let the mini wand on this mascara deceive you—this mascara packs a lot of punch into just a few swipes, and helps you get into all the crevices of your eye to coat every last lash. And it easily comes off with a cleansing balm or some micellar water, without pulling out any of your precious hairs.

One Cosmo editor says: “This tiny mascara should be considered sorcery first, a beauty product second. Upon first impression, I saw the short, stubby spoolie and couldn’t fathom liking the applicator. In just about five swipes, it completely lifted my lashes (no curler in sight) and lengthened them beautifully. It clings to and elongates tiny hairs I didn’t even know existed, completely opening up my eyes in the process.”—JASMINE HYMAN, ASSISTANT BEAUTY EDITOR

Why we love it: Clean mascaras often claim to be flake-free, but this one from Caliray is one of the only formulas that actually delivers. This creates a gorgeous falsie effect in just a few swipes, and stays locked in place. Not even high humidity can make this budge. Come hell or high water, it truly has your back.

One Cosmo editor says: "I love the idea of tubing mascara, but often get annoyed by the flakes throughout the day. But Caliray's is above that, offering lots of length and lift without chunks of mascara falling on my face. And the dark-brown shade basically looks black on my lashes, just ever-so-slightly softer."—BG

Why we love it: “Are you wearing falsies?” You’ll probably get this question when using the Tartelette Tubing Mascara. This formula uses micro-tubing polymers to wrap every individual lash in a conditioning, volumizing, and lengthening blanket. Beyond making lashes look incredible, it nourishes them so they can grow long and strong, too.

One Cosmo expert says: “Tarte's tubing mascara is my default mascara for everyday wear. It doesn't smudge or give me raccoon eyes EVER. I use the black on my top lashes and brown on the bottom. And compared to other tubing mascaras it doesn't tube off mid-day and leave weird flecks under your eyes.”—SARAH MABERRY, COMMERCE EDITOR

Time and time again, we’re amazed by how drugstore mascaras outperform luxury formulas. As far as tubing mascaras go, this one from CoverGirl takes the cake. Its longevity is totally unmatched—it lasts for 30 hours, making it the perfect party girl mascara—and beautifully separates each lash. Every coat creates even more definition sans-clumps for a fanned out, eye-opening effect.

One Cosmo editor says: “The brush is perfection, applying pigment evenly and smoothly with zero clumps. Unlike many tubing mascaras that feel wet or sticky on the lashes, this one hits the sweet spot—just the right texture for easy coverage. I was equally impressed by the staying power. I sweat profusely when I Peloton and am constantly wiping my face with a towel during my workout. This mascara didn't budge—nor did it irritate my sensitive eyes."—LAUREN BALSAMO, BEAUTY DIRECTOR

Why we love it: If you want seriously thick fringe—I’m talking so voluminous your lashes could be mistaken for extensions—a lash primer is a necessity. Luckily, this mascara comes with one built-in. On one side, you’ll find a conditioning primer that nourishes and lengthens, while the other end volumizes and deposits inky black pigment. The end result? Drama for days.

One Cosmo editor says: “Out of all the mascaras I’ve tried lately, this gets me the most compliments. I love the brown primer—it truly helps prep my lashes for the mascara itself, and I love that it’s not white. My lashes definitely look much fuller and longer when I use them together.” —JAYNE LEMIRE, EXECUTIVE ASSISTANT

Not at all. In fact, tubing mascara is less likely to damage lashes than regular mascara because of how easily it washes off—all you need is a bit of water, and the tubing particles slide right off without any rubbing or soap.

Whitfield says that removing tubing mascaras feels like conducting Cinderella’s fairy godmother’s spell. “The second the clock strikes midnight, and you get in the shower or wash your face, the fibers literally crumble right off,” she says. “You’re not fighting against the current with waterproof pigment or oily eye makeup remover to get it off. The water rinses off the mascara with minimal effort and without damaging your lashes.”

Mary Honkus is a contributor for Cosmopolitan with over eight years of experience researching, writing, and editing beauty stories. She’s an authority in all beauty categories and is very particular about mascara. For this story, she tested dozens of tubing mascaras and consulted with other Cosmo editors and a makeup artist to find the best formulas that elongate lashes and stay put until you wash your face at the end of the night.

Jasmine Hyman is the Assistant Beauty Editor at Cosmopolitan, where she writes about the latest beauty trends and must-have products. Her most prized beauty possessions are a meticulous skincare routine and salon blowouts. You’ll also likely find her in bed reading a good book or endlessly scrolling TikTok (spoiler: it’s usually the latter) while listening to Harry Styles’ entire discography on repeat. #6758. How to Avoid Sports Injuries in Your 30s, 40s, and Beyond

At 38 years old, I’ve been fortunate enough to go most of my life without being bothered by sports injuries, despite being consistently and competitively active for the better part of three decades. On the soccer field, I’ve played on and off at club level since primary school. (I’m currently holding it down as a winger on my local club’s 30+ “seniors” team.)

Meanwhile, as a health journalist and all-around enthusiastic exerciser, I’ve thrown myself at countless physical challenges—mostly for fun, sometimes to help pay the rent. Marathons, trail races, triathlons, 24-hour relays, mud runs, obstacle course races, Hyrox—you name it, I was probably obsessed with it for a time. And through it all, the only injury I ever incurred was a torn meniscus (the undignified result of attempting a wildly above-my-paygrade basketball dunk on a charitably lowered rim, at a promotional event). If only the surgeon could have extracted the memory of that day along with the frayed connective tissue he removed from my knee. I’m pretty sure I was wearing jeans, too.

All of this to say, everything was smooth sailing for a very long time, until it suddenly wasn’t. Somewhere around the age of 35, for reasons beyond my comprehension at the time, the wheels started to come off the bus. First, a stress fracture to my foot. I noticed it one night in bed and couldn’t tell you when it happened. Regardless, it put me in a boot for three months.

Next, a torn hamstring during a routine game of pick-up football, making the same run I’d made so many times before. I thought I’d just pulled it, so I kept playing, which of course only made it worse. That decision cost me six months. Then a strained calf muscle at a gentle session of run club. And, finally, shin splints. I don’t even remember how many months that one took me out for, but it felt like forever.

This barrage of sports injuries—all sustained within a roughly two-year period—was unrelenting, each overlapping pang seemingly passing the baton to the next. Needless to say, I was miserable. And the worst part was that I couldn’t understand why this was happening to me. I had been maintaining the same activity level, same diet, same supplements, and the same sleeping habits.

You always hear people talking half-jokingly about how getting older takes its toll on the body. But nothing really prepares you for that moment when you start to actually experience it for yourself. It’s existential. Brutal. Depressing, honestly. Sometimes the best way to deal with these spiral-inducing moments is to simply talk to other people dealing with the same thing. So I recently asked my, er, seasoned soccer team’s group chat: What do you do to avoid and manage sports injuries as you get older?

Kevin, 40, chimes in first: “There’s no avoiding, mate. Just managing.”

“Funnily enough, I’ve actually started doing yoga, which has been great for mobility and stretching out my lower back,” writes Chris, 35, who battles with a recurring quadratus lumborum (QL) issue. “I was always a bit hesitant to give it a go—not because I didn't think it would help, but because I couldn't get past the fact that I'd essentially be following a similar exercise plan to my mum! I think that’s been one of the bigger adjustments as I’ve gotten older: accepting that looking after your body sometimes means doing things you might have previously associated with an entirely different generation.”

“For me, I think the most important thing is to keep going in general,” adds Brian, who recently turned 42. “I remember when I was around 20 years old, my manager at the time, who was in his late 30s and retired from playing, was doing some running on his own before the team training session started. The lads were joking and teasing him about making a comeback, but I remember him saying, ‘If you stop moving at my age, you won't be able to start again.’ I think that’s good advice.”

In some way, I suppose it’s comforting to know that this is something we all have to deal with, whether you’re a Sunday league bench warmer or in contention for the Ballon d’Or. "With age, there’s just a higher incidence of soft-tissue injuries as your muscles lose strength and elasticity,” says Dr. Brian Forsythe, a sports medicine surgeon, associate professor at Rush University Medical Center, and recreational soccer player himself. According to Forsythe, who also serves as head orthopedic officer for MLS team Chicago Fire FC and president of the Professional Soccer Physicians Society, over time athletes across the skill spectrum will ultimately begin running into the same injuries.

You may not be able to halt the inevitable march of time, but there are certainly ways to minimize and slow its toll on your body. This requires some work but could ultimately be the difference between hanging up your boots and kicking on for another 10, 20, or even 30 years.

“It's up to you to take some responsibility,” Forsythe says. “Think about the lifestyle you had when you were 17 or 18—how you took care of your body and what you looked like. Then look in the mirror and ask yourself, ‘How much of my life now resembles what it was then?’ If you’re going out and having a few pints, understand that that inhibits your recovery and decreases protein synthesis. If you’re overweight, that has effects beyond just not looking great in your clothes. It’s increased joint load, increased rotational torque. You’re more likely to get injured. So you have to set realistic expectations of what your body is capable of doing. But you can definitely still do a lot.”

If you’re not ready to call time just yet, here are a couple of strategies you can start implementing today, courtesy of Forsythe and a handful of other experts.

“Number one, and it should be obvious, is just general conditioning,” says Forsythe. Being a slightly less sedentary human being during the work week means you won’t send your body into total shock when you decide to get going over the weekend.

“A lot of injuries come from that mismatch between what we do all week and the intensity we demand from our bodies on the weekend,” says sports medicine physician Dr. Alex McDonald. As Dr. James B. Chen, orthopedic surgeon at DISC Sports and Spine Center, puts it: “The biggest risk isn't necessarily your age, but your schedule.”

“When you think about playing in your teens and twenties, you were probably close to your ideal body weight,” says Forsythe. Ideal body weight, or IBW, refers to the weight at which your body is able to function most efficiently and without a heightened risk of injury. “All of the forces across your joints are multiplied almost exponentially with additional pounds on your frame,” says Forsythe. For example, “the force across your knee can be up to six times your body weight.”

“If you've ever been overweight and you climb up and down stairs and notice that the front of your knee hurts, that's because of those increased pressures and forces,” adds Forsythe.

Jogging a couple of miles a week may help you get through your Sunday workout our pick-up game, but it’s not doing anything to prevent in-the-moment injuries. To do that, you have to train the way you play—whatever game that is. “It's changes of direction," says Forsythe. It's rapid acceleration and deceleration. It's pivoting, twisting, turning.” Basically, bring back whatever drills you used to do on your sports team when you were younger, and adjust them for the demands of the sport you currently play.

No matter how frequently and comprehensively you train, a complete warm-up before any big athletic event is a non-negotiable. "It helps dramatically, which is why you'll always see professionals warm up for 30-45 minutes before a match,” says Forsythe. When it comes to soccer, FIFA actually has developed a warm-up program, called FIFA ll+, specifically for injury prevention. “It’s a free routine that only takes about 20 minutes, and includes running drills plus strength, balance, and landing exercises such as Nordic hamstring curls and single-leg balance work,” says McDonald. “It’s been shown to cut injuries by about a third, but it needs to be done at least twice a week—ideally three or four times a week—to really reduce the risk of injury.”

Stretching after your warm-up is important, too, but just be careful not to overdo it. “If you stretch too aggressively, you can actually predispose yourself to muscle tendon injuries,” says Forsythe. “First warm up, and then stretch out within your range of comfort. But right before a game is not the time to all of a sudden correct tight hamstrings.”

Often, being sidelined by an injury can be more painful than the injury itself, causing guys to get back out there earlier than they probably should. “A common issue that I see is when someone sustains a minor sprain or strain, and they return to sport-specific activity too early,” says Chen, “which can result in either worsening the injury or even picking up a secondary injury.” As annoying as it might be to sit out for 2 or 3 weeks with a strained hamstring, turning that into a tear will bench you for far longer. Believe me.

Playing sports in your 30s, 40s, and beyond also means coming to grips with your body’s increasing need for routine recovery between training sessions and games. "You have to recognize that your body needs more time to recover,” says Forsythe. You may have great cardiovascular fitness and feel ready to play on consecutive days, but your muscles and connective tissues need time to bounce back. “It’s important to respect fatigue, and ensure proper recovery after games or training,” says McDonald.

To be fair, this is universally sound guidance regardless of age. But it's advice that tends to take decades to sink in. “I think that one of the key advantages of playing as you get older is that you know your body better in your 30s and 40s,” Brian’s response concluded. “You understand what it's saying to you now. You might not have even listened when you were 21!”

#6757. The Best Nontoxic Sunscreens, According to Dermatologists

Are the best nontoxic sunscreens just marketing speak? In 2026, SPF formulas labeled as “clean” or “nontoxic” are in demand—and according to dermatologists, branding in this category isn’t mere greenwashing. “While the term ‘nontoxic’ is not strictly regulated, it’s often used to describe sunscreens that avoid certain chemical filters like oxybenzone, homosalate, and octinoxate,” says Marisa Garshick, MD, a board-certified dermatologist in Manhattan and New Jersey. “Some consumers prefer this, due to concerns about irritation, allergies, or environmental impact.”

As top brands meet demand and roll out clean formulas with modern technology, nontoxic sunscreens are taking on an increasingly elegant feel, in the vein of Korean sunscreen formulas—so you can rest assured, heavy, white-cast-ridden mineral SPFs are mostly a thing of the past. Another way in which brands find a point of differentiation in the oversaturated market, as Dr. Garshick points out, is by diversifying with skincare-grade ingredients that boast benefits beyond sun protection alone. She also notes that antioxidants and hydrating ingredients, such as hyaluronic acid and glycerin, are becoming the norm in nontoxic sunscreens.

Which formulas are getting the sun-protection job done—and then some? Ahead, find the nontoxic sunscreens that dermatologists most recommend.

“There is no formal definition that qualifies something as nontoxic, but when it comes to sunscreens, it generally refers to those that don’t contain ingredients like oxybenzone or other chemical filters,” Dr. Garshick explains.

Aparovich, whose role as a biochemist at OnSkin involves safety analysis of cosmetic formulas, dives in deeper. “Nontoxic refers to a sunscreen containing no components that can be potentially detrimental to human health from the overall formulation perspective. It means that neither UV filters nor any auxiliary ingredients—such as stabilizers, emulsifiers, film-forming agents, preservatives, or fragrances—can induce known or suspected hazards, including skin irritation and sensitization, allergic reactions, endocrine disruptions, genotoxicity, and more,” she explains.

While it’s commonly thought that, in sunscreens, nontoxic refers to mineral sunscreens only, Aparovich dispels the myth. “From a human-safety standpoint, sunscreen formulas may include both mineral (physical) and/or organic (chemical) filters, with no adverse effects associated with them,” she stipulates.

Are nontoxic sunscreens as good as traditional sunscreens?

“Mineral or nontoxic sunscreens are just as effective as traditional sunscreens when used correctly,” says Dr. Day. “For my patients with eczema or psoriasis, I actually prefer them. They’re less likely to cause stinging, burning, or flares and provide excellent broad-spectrum protection.” Just like any sunscreen, she says, nontoxic ones need to be applied generously and reapplied often, especially after sweating or swimming.

Overall, Aparovich advocates looking for a sunscreen formula suited to an individual’s skin type. “People with oily, combination, or acne-prone skin should opt for products free from comedogenic ingredients to avoid clogging pores and triggering inflammation, blackheads, or breakouts,” she says. These ingredients include cacao, palm, coconut, and wheat-germ oils and chemicals like acetylated lanolin alcohol, isopropyl myristate, isopropyl linoleate, isopropyl isostearate, isopropyl palmitate, myristyl lactate, myristyl myristate, laureth-4, oleyl alcohol, and ethylhexyl palmitate.

“These are the ingredients with the highest comedogenicity rate,” she says. People with dry or sensitive skin should avoid harsh alcohols, such as alcohol, alcohol denat, ethanol, or SD alcohol, Aparovich adds. “These ingredients can compromise the skin’s barrier and cause irritation.” Additionally, she notes that an ideal nontoxic sunscreen should provide broad-spectrum protection to shield the skin from UVA and UVB rays and help reduce the risk of complex DNA damage in skin cells.

“Another key point is sunscreen’s sun protection factor, or SPF,” she says. “It indicates how effectively sunscreen can protect your skin from UVB rays, the ultraviolet light associated with sunburn.” Aparovich recommends choosing sunscreens with at least SPF 30 but ideally SPF 50, especially when the UV index is three or higher.

“Technically, there are sunscreens that claim to be 100% natural, but that term isn’t regulated,” Dr. Day says. “What matters more is that the product is clinically tested, well tolerated by sensitive skin, and free of known irritants.”

What ingredients should you avoid in sunscreen?

“Three of the most hazardous chemical filters to avoid are oxybenzone (benzophenone-3), homosalate, and octinoxate,” says Aparovich. “Studies have shown oxybenzone to be a common allergen, as well as an irritant to the eyes and skin, and a potential endocrine disruptor.” She adds that homosalate and octinoxate are linked to possible adverse effects on the endocrine system and that all three ingredients have been shown to have a negative impact on marine life and coral reefs. Additionally, Aparovich recommends avoiding formaldehyde-releasing preservatives like imidazolidinyl urea, Quaternium-15, and diazolidinyl urea, along with fragrances classified as allergenic, such as cinnamal, citral, farnesol, coumarin, eugenol, geraniol, and others. “These chemicals can trigger allergic reactions and skin irritation,” she notes. Dr. Garshick recommends seeking paraben-free products, as the ingredient can irritate some skin types.

When we test and review a product, we take a holistic approach to deliver well-rounded product recommendations. First, we lean on Vogue’s vast network of experts—from board-certified dermatologists to celebrity estheticians—to gain professional acumen on the industry’s standout products, ones these specialists would actually use on their clients. We pair their expertise with our editorial best practices to curate the thoughtful edits you read on our site.

As it relates to nontoxic sunscreens, we selected the best based on the following characteristics: ingredients, SPF type, texture, packaging, finish, and the skin-care concerns they address. To do this, we paired our own personal tests of each formula with expert guidance and reviewer insights to determine which we would recommend to you.

#6755. The “Choppy Rachel Bob” Is the Most Flattering Winter Hair Trend—How to Recreate

Jennifer Aniston, the inspiration that you are.

I may be dressing like a walking blanket thanks to the drop in temperature (comfort > style, am I right?), but at least my hair—and more specifically, my recently cut bob —looks great. There's something about winter that calls for a total beauty refresh (and in my case, a drop in fashion presentation, but we shall overlook that), and seemingly, Meghann Fahy would agree, having just debuted a chin-length style inspired by the ’90s. So long, Goldilocks!

The The White Lotus actor debuted the haircut in question on The TODAY Show earlier this week, where she spoke all about her role in new thriller The Good Daughter. And while we're, of course, excited to hear all about the series, it was the bob chat that piqued our interest.

When asked about her new ’do, Meghann shared that the reference photo she showed her hairstylist was of “the Rachel,” Jennifer Aniston's iconic haircut from Friends. And yes, that is thee haircut that sent approximately every woman with a television to her local hair salon in the mid-’90s.

"It's like looking in the mirror," Meghann joked when shown a picture of Rachel Green.

Now, while we're not talking an exact replica of the original, Meghann's take feels like a much more wearable update (because we can’t all have that ’90s volume at all times). So, if you, too, want to recreate the style for winter 2026, because why wouldn't you, ask for a blunt yet choppy silhouette to create the illusion of fuller strands, and a little textured wave for movement and body. Oh, and, of course, show a reference picture of Rachel. Duh!

And honestly, winter is the perfect time to try the shorter style. The neck-skimming length looks oh-so-chic when peeking out from oversized scarves, while the shape gives hair a little more structure when the weather is doing its absolute worst.

Rachel Green (and Meghann Fahy), you've done it again.

#6753. Jamie Campbell Bower Is the Rising Style Star You Need to Know

When it comes to Hollywood’s red carpets, the menswear assortment is—more often than not—very safe. Snoozy, even. Sure, classic suits and tuxedos will always have a place at a splashy movie premiere, but whatever happened to fun? No one’s fun anymore!

That being said, there are a few actors who are taking a more eccentric and unexpected approach to their fashion choices. Take Alexander Skarsgard, and his freaky shoes. Or, more recently, there’s Jamie Campbell Bower, who has been bringing a unique fashion energy to his press tour for The Lord of the Rings: The Rings of Power this month. (The star recently joined season three of the hit Prime Video series.)

You may know Bower for his music, or for portraying the villainous Vecna on Stranger Things, but we are fast associating him with an impeccable sense of style. Throughout his press tour this month, he and his stylist Nathan Henry have been infusing traditional tailoring looks with cool, off-kilter accessories—like brooches and headpieces!—that make the outfits feel a whole lot more fresh and modern.

Last night in London, for one, Bower emerged wearing a black cape coat from Matières Fécales—the avant-garde Parisian label that is fast becoming a celebrity favorite. Punctuating his sleek outerwear? There was a bejeweled crown headpiece, oversized eyeball and flower brooches, and even a spider-like jewelry piece that fit like a glove. Talk about fun!

Wearing pieces by Wooyoungmi, John Richmond and Adelina Bernard.

Some of his other recent Lord of the Rings looks have been more subdued—but equally as slick and contemporary. For the New York premiere, there was a perfectly-slouchy black pinstriped suit with layered silver necklaces; For the Mexico City screening, he chose a black suit with a burgundy velvet lapel, and a crisp white shirt with a high collar and oversized lapels. These slight exaggerations of silhouette imbued his looks with a little personality: They’re not so formal or standard; rather, they have a little zest to them.

Given he is also a professional musical artist by day, Bower’s rock-and-roll sensibility could play a big part in why his red carpet style feels so noteworthy: He simply has that innate, effortless way of dressing that so many musicians seem to nail. (Think: Mick Jagger, Prince, Bowie.) And we need this energy from a whole lot more men in Hollywood. . . Life is short: Take a risk!

#6751. The 73 Best-Ever Celebrity Fits From Every GQ Men of the Year Party

#6749. Cartier Emergency? Inside the High-Stakes World of a Luxury Fashion Concierge

Private shopping appointments, last-minute deliveries, and the art of whatever the client wants.

Isn’t a fashion emergency fun when it happens to someone else? Think of the dress ripping in My Best Friend’s Wedding, or the frantic dash down Seventh Avenue in The Devil Wears Prada. These crises happen in real life, too, but are considerably less fun when they’re happening to us. How many times have you had an event and realized your look wasn’t quite right just minutes before the Uber arrives? Or stared into your suitcase on vacation wishing you had brought the espadrilles, not the sandals?

These are the sorts of requests handled by Quintessentially, the London-founded luxury concierge company that has spent the past 26 years building a business around the proposition that, for a certain class of consumer, almost anything can be arranged. Now operating in 40 cities, its U.S. operation spans private concierge services, travel, events, and guest management for luxury brands. Memberships start at $25,000 per year.

Its fashion services are particularly revealing: part cultural access and part tactical operation, run through a network of relationships built carefully over decades. Each member is assigned a lifestyle manager and a travel specialist, with a wider team of experts behind them.

“Our lifestyle managers are the lifeblood of Quintessentially,” says Lauren Wilt, U.S. CEO of Quintessentially. Many come from celebrity personal assisting, luxury hospitality, music, and entertainment—industries that reward discretion, speed, and an ability to handle big personalities. And learning the ins and outs of their clients’ idiosyncrasies is the key to their business.

“There isn’t another concierge company that gets to know their clients the way we do,” says Wilt. “This isn’t a self-service booking platform with a suite of benefits. We are a highly personalized, proactive service.”

Proactive being the operative word, and fashion offers plenty of opportunity for a lifestyle manager’s creative problem-solving. “Our members often come to us saying, ‘I’ve heard about this experience—hook me up and book it,’” said Wilt. “But it’s our job to tell them when to go, when to avoid the crowds, and sometimes that the experience they’ve seen on Instagram isn’t actually the experience for them.”

For one particularly devoted member, Quintessentially organized a Paris itinerary that was the stuff shopping dreams are made of. There were styling appointments at Dior ahead of a runway show and after-party, a private Chanel appointment arranged around a wish list submitted in advance, and lunch with Isabel Marant at her studio before attending her show. Hair and makeup were, of course, handled too. The itinerary also included a tour of vintage ateliers and heritage boutiques, with a specialist who negotiated purchases on the client’s behalf. Trop chic.

The service extends beyond the easily bookable, too. Another Quintessentially member was granted private access to Dior’s historic design archives and La Suite Dior, an invitation-only experience involving a private fragrance session, photo shoot, and a private after-hours visit to the Dior boutique. Other members have been invited into Bottega Veneta’s private New York residence, where rare and made-to-order pieces are presented alongside private dining and events.

But it’s not all about the shopping—a concierge service can also carefully curate once-in-a-lifetime experiences. When one member sought to propose to his girlfriend in Paris, Quintessentially arranged for a proposal on a flower-filled private balcony overlooking the Eiffel Tower. Afterward, the couple visited Cartier for a private appointment to choose her engagement ring together. And a surprise upgrade to the Karl Lagerfeld Suite at Hôtel de Crillon was secured.

The team also excels at solving logistical challenges. In one case, a diamond ring was collected in Geneva and delivered to a member in their suite at the Cheval Blanc Paris. And when a guest needed—truly needed—a new Saint Laurent bag and Bulgari necklace hand-delivered to their resort in the Maldives, Quintessentially made it happen.

“A brand sells you the dress; we help you with where to wear it,” Wilt says. Indeed, Quintessentially’s relationships with fashion houses operate in both directions. Luxury brands employ the company to manage the logistics surrounding their most important clients, as well as influencer trips and large-scale fashion shows and shopping events. One recent project for a fashion client involved bringing 100 couples to New York over four days, with Quintessentially coordinating car services, welcome lunches, and all the practical details that allowed the brand to focus on hosting.

But perhaps the most interesting thing about a service like Quintessentially is that it isn’t really about acquiring new things. It’s about the removal of friction, and the ability to move through the world with a degree of spontaneity. The real luxury isn’t the Birkin, the hotel suite, or the private fitting—it’s in never having to wonder how to make it all happen.

#6747. Jennifer Lawrence Makes the Business Suit Her Own

Haven’t you heard? Jennifer Lawrence is a certified businesswoman now. The actor launched her haircare brand, Mes, late last month and she’s bringing the boardroom into her closet.

Lawrence left her house today—with a to-go coffee cup in one hand, house keys in the other— looking perfectly entrepreneurial in a long, slim-fitting black blazer and flowy trousers. While the suit itself was a departure from her standard jeand and T-shirt fare, Lawrence found a way to make even a business suit work with her undone, bohemain style.

An expert accessorizer, Lawrence added all of her style signatures to her sharp suiting. Beneath her blazer, the actor wore a lavender long-sleeve tee and a black floral velvet burnout scarf—a switchup from the vibrant pink-and-gold checked Yaser Shaw shawl she wore out yesterday, but a statement scarf nonetheless. Just as classic J.Law were her easy yet luxe burgundy velvet slip-on shoes from The Row. (No matter the season, Lawrence is liable to rock a slide, from her leopard-print Ancient Greek slippers to a pair of embellished Vans.)

But the most Lawrencian touch of all had to be the actor’s boxy black croc Hermès Kelly, which she prefers to wear haphazardly flung open. That’s one way to say, I’m not a regular boss, I’m a cool boss.

#6746. How a Strip Club DJ Helped Xtube Kill the Porn Studio

For decades, anyone driving down the far-east stretch of one of Toronto’s main arteries would likely have caught a glimpse of a white Cadillac perched atop the entrance of a strip mall beckoning them to stop and step through a set of dark blue doors, behind which lay a world of male fantasy. So iconic was Caddy’s that when the strip club closed in 2017 the Toronto Sun sent out a reporter to mark the moment. In a story titled ‘Last Dance’, the paper somewhat melodramatically bewailed the end of an institution that had provided thousands upon thousands of Torontian men with an ‘escape from the drudgery of [ . . .] lives, wives and troubles’.

Judging by the long streak of one-star ratings on Caddy’s now inactive reviews page, its glory days were long gone by the time the classic car was taken down for good—and it wasn’t the only strip club to have suffered such a fate. By 2017 only twelve clubs were still operating out of the nearly fifty joints that had been dotted around Toronto at the turn of the century. Strip clubs have always been controversial, criticized for monetizing the objectification of women in a way many find too crude. Yet the stripper—at once desired and despised, exploited yet feared for her power to corrupt the good man—has not vanished. She permeates popular culture, from blockbusters and music videos to fitness crazes. Why, then, have strip clubs been shutting down? The reason was clear to Canadian stripper and activist Valerie Scott. “It’s called the internet,” she said in the report on Caddy’s closure; now, anyone seeking to escape the drudgery of reality had only to turn to their phone.

As fate would have it, Caddy’s made a cameo appearance in the story of how adult entertainment moved online. A little over a decade before the club played its last song it hired a man named Kurtis Potec, a former welder who’d just traded his tools for the DJ booth. “It wasn’t great; it wasn’t horrible,” Kurtis told me over a video call years later. “It was something to pay the bills.” Young and restless, he was a jack of all trades trying to find his way in life, but playing the tunes to which strippers danced and derobed turned out not to be Kurtis’s ultimate calling either. When he spotted a newspaper ad for a desk job at an online start-up not far from Caddy’s he figured he’d try his luck in the emerging virtual world.

“That was probably one of the most unique interviews of my life,” Kurtis told me with a chuckle. After a cursory skim of the twenty-something’s CV, the interviewer began to spout some “pretty vulgar, sexual-related terms” to check whether Kurtis was prepared for the world of online pornography. On the internet, there were no gatekeepers to control what people said, “so I guess they wanted to prepare me for the worst.”

He got the job. Kurtis’s new employer was Xtube, the first tube site targeted at gay men. The start-up’s first office was housed in a warehouse on the outskirts of the city, piled up to the ceiling and wall to wall with boxes, leaving only a two-foot-wide passage for Kurtis and his handful of co-workers to reach their desks. Inside the perilously stacked towers were countless pornographic DVDs awaiting digitization and to be uploaded online, along with merchandise like mouse pads and socks emblazoned with the company logo. Other than the three founders and the main financier—a Chinese man whom the others referred to as Mr X—the only other employee was a video encoder who at times appeared to live in the back room, where he worked and chain-smoked in solitude.

Kurtis, by contrast, was drawn into a far more public role. When one of the co-founders left, spooked by the threats that started rolling in from porn producers angry at finding their films on Xtube, Kurtis essentially picked up the day-to-day running of the site. Soon, he became the face of the company. Literally. “If you had any issues on the site, there was my ugly mug plastered in the corner,” he said. His associates didn’t use their real names, let alone their faces, and they had Kurtis—still technically a junior employee—front the company. “They were older and smarter than I was,” he told me, laughing.

By 2008, a year after Pornotube halted user uploads over fears of illegal content, business at Xtube was booming. Each month, it was pulling in hundreds of thousands of dollars from advertising. Advertisers were enthralled by this new medium, which allowed them to target their messages with unprecedented precision. Traditional media, including TV and magazines, had long promised access to certain demographics, but the internet offered the ability to drill deeper and glean details about people’s lives without asking a single question. Those being tracked hardly resisted. Many did not grasp the scale of the surveillance, while others were simply distracted by all the free stuff, like porn. Xtube’s users, Kurtis said, “really liked it.” But most of his days were spent fielding calls from within the industry, and their reaction was less enthusiastic. “People hated us.”

Around this time Kurtis started feeling depressed and began using drugs to cope with a job that, more and more, involved people shouting at him. “I got very used to hearing the words: I’m going to sue you.” But the threats were toothless. Free-speech laws governing the internet meant that user-generated platforms were, in essence, not legally responsible for what users uploaded.

All the studios could do was stand by and watch as their films were repeatedly stolen and re-uploaded by fans and, insiders complained, by tube sites themselves. It didn’t take long before their powerlessness began to attract investors’ attention. User-generated platforms seemed legally untouchable. And while there were plenty of platforms selling ad space, few received as many visits as the free porn sites. Kurtis was told to fend off callers claiming they wanted to buy Xtube. But one day in 2008 a man on the line refused to take no for an answer. He kept repeating: “Just give me a number.”

Kurtis walked into the back room, where one of the owners was sitting, and asked if there was any amount of money that would make it worth parting with Xtube. The slightly facetious reply was 35 million Canadian dollars. ‘I walked back, picked up the phone and said: “You’re not gonna like this,”’ Kurtis recalled. The man on the phone didn’t hesitate; he’d be there early next week to sort out the details.

In the months that followed a German in his early thirties would regularly occupy the carpet by Kurtis’s desk. Fabian Thylmann would stretch out on it to ease a back battered by his now frequent flights to Toronto. Fabian was a self-proclaimed nerd who as a teenager in the late nineties had developed one of the earliest pieces of software capable of tracking what internet users clicked on. Crucially, it allowed website owners to earn money when a link on their page redirected a visitor to make a purchase elsewhere. Hawking online advertising space had been a fairly unsurprising evolution of traditional marketing, but here was something that made it possible to monitor what people did next, and profit from it. The internet was turning into a game of digital chess, with money to be made by those who could anticipate, and manipulate, the whims of anyone who entered their domain.

Fabian’s first company had given him a rare glimpse into the back end of some early online businesses. Many were trying to sell porn, which was one of the first things people were willing to pay for on the internet. But these were small, makeshift operations, and few were making real money. In the early noughties, most of the people buying smutty DVDs or renting pay-per-views alone in hotel rooms hardly knew how to use the internet. Sure, they would eventually find their way to adult sites. But Fabian realized that a truly successful enterprise could do far more than tap into existing demand—it could bring pornography to people who had not even looked for it, through pop-ups, ads and search results, turning it from something you searched for into something that found you. Once porn became part of the internet’s background noise, those who’d never have dared to cross the threshold of a sex shop or an erotic cinema, or breach any of the other hurdles the curious had once faced, could now stumble upon porn with a single click.

The purpose of tube sites, like that of virtually every internet platform since, was not to replicate what existed in the physical world but to construct a new, disembodied mode of interaction built around visual stimuli—what we now call “content.” Their rise marked the start of a new era, when every step online began to be tracked. It was a turning point in the story of how data, harvested without people’s informed consent, became one of the world’s most valuable commodities, eventually proliferating to a point where it could be used to teach artificial intelligence to think and act like us. The tale of tube sites, then, is also the story of how the digital world came to be organized around priorities that do not necessarily align with those of the people who inhabit it.

The practice Fabian helped pioneer as a teenager is today known as affiliate marketing—put simply, the business of getting people to click on things. It’s how content creators make money when they offer their followers discounts on whatever must-have food supplement, skin cream or therapy app they claim to love. And the business of funneling people’s attention was first stress-tested in the porn industry. Long before influencers started peddling protein powder, porn sites were paying webmasters to drive clicks to their content, offering a cut for every paying visitor who came to their site through a tracked banner ad or link. It was the logic of the early thumbnail galleries, like the one built by the pale programmer who had filched sandwiches from Mario Salieri.

One of the key reasons why Amazon grew to become the place for online shopping was its early adoption of an affiliate programme which paid website owners a commission to direct people to products sold on the platform. Once people got accustomed to buying all their stuff on Amazon, the companies that actually made the stuff—anything from phone chargers to vacuum cleaners to cat litter—realized that if their products weren’t on the site, no one would buy them. Needless to say, affiliate marketing is still one of the main legs of Amazon’s business model, and its foundations were laid by people like Fabian, who understood that to make real money online you need to attract plenty of people and make sure they keep coming back. This is called “the network effect,” and it is one of the basic laws of the digital economy—the more people use your service, the more powerful you become.

As sites such as Xtube were attracting a critical mass of visitors, they could essentially dictate terms to studios that were now, in desperation, drawn into partnerships with the platforms. The tube site’s offer was to feature ten or fifteen minutes of footage and call it “advertising.” The only problem was that the average visit to a porn site ends—rather abruptly—after about nine minutes.

Still, as free content washed over the Web, studios started uploading their own clips to tube sites, hoping a few hundred or thousand viewers might click the affiliate link and pay for the full film. If they did, the revenue would be split 50:50 with the tube site. “We put a lot of studios out of business,” Kurtis said, “they couldn’t afford to get traffic from us.” Companies like Xtube quickly learned to steer what users clicked on next, and once they controlled enough traffic to funnel millions of potential customers towards paying sites or advertisers, they became powerful.

This dynamic has since spread beyond porn. Many news outlets now depend on posting their stories to Facebook, TikTok or X, hoping to lure readers back to their own sites, where some might eventually pay for their journalism. Few do. Like porn, news has become something most of us expect to get for free. The number of independent newspapers has collapsed, many swallowed by conglomerates that rely on scale to profit from advertising and, increasingly, from affiliate links.

Fabian’s ambitions were clear from the beginning. Xtube was offering more than a quick buck. It was a stepping stone in the making of an empire. He wanted to “basically take over the adult industry,” Kurtis said. As the traditional studios buckled under the tube-site business model, many went up for sale, which meant that their huge libraries of content could be bought on the cheap. For a site like Xtube, buying studios and their subscription platforms made perfect sense. It gained access to more content, and could funnel its huge traffic towards paid sites it now controlled. If people paid for porn, the company profited; if they preferred the free stuff, it profited too.

It was a business model that thrived on relentless expansion, and Fabian had his sights set on a much bigger rival. In nearby Montreal, another group of young men were running a site that would become to pornography what McDonald’s is to the burger: Pornhub. To simplify, it was founded in 2007 by three Concordia University students—Matt Keezer, Steph Manos and Sam Youssef—whose company, Mansef, was already running several porn businesses, including the still influential production studio Brazzers (a slang take on “brothers”). Publicly, Youssef posed as a concerned studio owner and railed against tube sites on the industry forum GoFuckYourself.com: “They will not steal it and get away with it. Their days are counted!” What few in the industry realized was that Pornhub was run by the same group.

In a 2023 interview about his most recent venture Youssef said that he and his co-founders made the decision to sell Pornhub to Fabian in 2010 for roughly $140 million because he “didn’t feel comfortable at all with what the company had become.” He sounded almost surprised to have found himself in the sex industry, despite having made his money from sites such as JuggFuckers and RacksAndBlacks.

When I tried to contact them all in 2020, Youssef declined to talk to me about those early years, saying he was never really “much involved” with the business he had founded. Keezer likewise responded: “To be honest with you, I never had any involvement in the ‘industry.’ I just made Pornhub in 2007 with a couple of friends and sold it [ . . .] and that was about it.” I had no response from Manos. This detachment was typical of the internet entrepreneurs who profited from unleashing pornography. Many preferred to remain anonymous, even as the industry’s most important workers literally have to bare all. In a move that reflected the trolling culture of the nerds who were taking over porn, Fabian—the new king of smut—renamed his empire Manwin.

#6745. What Is an Upper Bleph? Here’s What to Know About the Eyelid Surgery

Doctors explain how the procedure works, when a brow lift may be needed, and nonsurgical options.

In the world of plastic surgery, the nose, breasts, and butt have commanded a lot of time and attention under the knife. But as tech advances and patients seek out more subtle ways to manage aging skin, doctors have shifted their focus to other areas, some once deemed too risky to touch. Blepharoplasty, or bleph, for short, is a plastic surgery procedure that trims, redistributes, or removes excess skin on the eyelids.

The procedure can be done to help improve peripheral vision or for more cosmetic purposes, like removing hooded eyelids. Blephs can be done on the eyelid itself, which is known as an upper bleph, or a lower bleph, which is done below the eye to treat puffy or saggy skin. Even the most effective eye creams and potent skin care formulas sometimes may not be enough to correct excess skin around the eyes, but before you schedule your consultation, see what doctors have to say about the procedure to determine if it’s the best option for you right now.

An upper bleph specifically addresses the eyelid, where skin and sometimes fat closest to the nose are removed. Dr. Lyle Leipziger, chief of plastic surgery at North Shore University Hospital and LIJ Medical Center in New York, explains that the procedure involves removing skin and excising or repositioning deeper fatty tissue to address droopiness, puffiness, and under-eye bags. “As we age, the skin around the eyes naturally loses elasticity, which can cause the upper eyelids to droop, look heavy, or create a tired appearance. This can also be exacerbated by excess upper eyelid fatty tissue,” says Leipziger. During surgery, he explains, excess skin is removed, and surgeons may also remove or reposition small amounts of fat to create a smoother, more defined upper eyelid contour. “The goal is to open up the eyes and create a brighter, more refreshed appearance while preserving the patient’s natural eye shape,” he adds.

Dr. Lesley Rabach, a board-certified facial plastic surgeon and co-founder of Rabach Aesthetics in NYC, says that the best candidates for an upper bleph are people who have a “normal” eyebrow position with excess skin, also known as dermatochalasia. “When considering an upper bleph, your surgeon needs to look at the eyebrow position, eyelid skin, and fat, as well as how much the eye opens when determining if an upper bleph is the correct procedure,” she notes. Rabach says that sometimes a brow lift and/or ptosis repair—which reconstructs the eyelid muscles or connects it to the forehead muscle to lift it—is part of the surgery as well.

Additionally, Leipziger adds that blephs are ideal for individuals who have excess skin or fullness in their upper eyelids that makes their eyes look heavy, tired, or aged. “This is especially common as we get older and lose elasticity in the delicate skin around the eyes,” he points out. “Ideal candidates are generally in good overall health, have realistic expectations, and are bothered by drooping upper eyelid skin and possible fatty tissue.” He recommends having a consultation with a board-certified plastic surgeon to determine whether overhanging skin, excess fatty tissue, brow position, or another anatomical factor is contributing to the appearance of the upper eyelids or if it’s something else.

Rabach says the results are both subtle and obvious at the same time. “They remove the extra skin that often has a crepey texture while restoring the youthful appearance of the eyelid,” she says. “An upper bleph should restore the natural youthful architecture of the upper eyelid that is natural to the person.”

By removing excess skin and, according to Leipziger, when appropriate, repositioning small amounts of fat from the upper eyelids, the procedure helps create a more open and defined eye area. “Patients should experience a significant improvement with their eyes looking brighter and less tired, without ever looking ‘done’” says Leipziger. He also notes that while an upper bleph usually lasts a long time, it doesn’t slow down or stop the way your skin will naturally age. So you may see some sagging or loss of elasticity return over time. “The goal is a natural-looking enhancement rather than a dramatic change in appearance,” Leipziger expresses.

The eye area is very delicate, but blephs are less invasive than you’d think. Rabach performs the procedure at her practice several times a week using just local anesthesia. “However, we use very thin stitches that stay in place for close to one week and must be removed. While there is little to no pain, it’s hard to camouflage the stitches, so we tell our patients they will feel perfectly fine to work from home even after the surgery that day, but not be working from the office,” says Rabach.

Leipziger agrees that recovery from a blepharoplasty is generally minimal compared to other cosmetic surgeries. He says most patients experience some swelling, bruising, and mild discomfort during the first week, which gradually improves. “I typically recommend taking about five to seven days off from work and social activities for an upper blepharoplasty, and a little longer when a lower blepharoplasty is performed,” says Leipziger, adding that most people can resume normal activities within two weeks.

For long-term eye lifting and opening, blephs are the way to go, but Leipziger says several nonsurgical treatments can help improve the appearance of the upper eyelids—even if only temporarily. It all depends on your specific concerns. “Botox can provide a subtle brow lift by relaxing specific muscles, which can help open up the eye area. Certain laser and radiofrequency treatments can also improve skin quality and mild skin laxity by stimulating collagen production and tightening the skin. These options can be beneficial for patients with early signs of aging or minimal excess skin,” says Leipziger. The biggest difference is that the nonsurgical options cannot remove excess eyelid skin or fat. “For patients with significant skin laxity or hooding, an upper blepharoplasty is typically the most effective solution for achieving a more open, refreshed appearance,” Leipziger clarifies.

According to Rabach, a fat transfer is another option to restore hollowness along the orbital bone. This technique can help smooth crepey texture that can set in with age. She also recommends lasers such as CO2 to help improve skin quality, but specifies they aren’t a replacement for surgical removal of excess skin.

As previously mentioned, eyelid skin—even if there’s less of it—will continue to age after an upper bleph. “Long-lasting results come from general health and care of the area. Always wear sunscreen and wear sunglasses to protect the skin, as it’s the thinnest skin on the body and very delicate and prone to sun damage,” says Rabach.

Along with the non-negotiable sunscreen, Leipziger recommends medical-grade skin care products and ingredients such as retinol or peptides to support skin quality. “Additionally, patients may benefit from complementary nonsurgical treatments, such as Botox, laser skin rejuvenation, and/or chemical peels. While surgery cannot stop the aging process, proper skin care and maintenance can help patients enjoy their refreshed appearance for many years,” Leipziger concludes.
